v0.60.95 (2026-10-09)  — $$, $PPID, $SECONDS, $LINENO
  - $$ is the shell's task (the console's is the kernel's first, 0; a
    session's its own), the same inside $( ) as bash's; $PPID the task
    that started it (0 for the console); $SECONDS the seconds since it
    started, and SECONDS=N counts on from N; $LINENO the line of the
    follow script now running (a block over lines gives its first line,
    where bash gives each command's: written down).
  - $(( )) reads $$ $? $# and $1..$9 too, as bash; they were errors.
  - Found on the way: builtin_redirect's depth for $(( )) never came back
    to 0, so a > after one on the same line was not taken; it counts each
    ( and ) once now (biredir-test gains that line).
  - scripts/shellvars-test.sh checks them, $LINENO against bash's.

v0.60.94 (2026-10-09)  — REDIRECTIONS ON BUILTINS AND FUNCTIONS
  - `slurp hi > F` printed "hi > F": only cook, a loop's done and a { }
    group took a redirection. Now a builtin's or a function's line takes
    > F, >> F and < F (1> and &> as >) wherever they sit, and runs as
    { cmd ; } > F (both nested). 2> F makes F empty, as a builtin has no
    stderr of its own; 2>&1 needs nothing. Not inside quotes, $( ),
    $(( )) or (( )), so (( 3 > 2 )) is still arithmetic; a cook line and
    a pipeline's stages do their own as before.
  - Found on the way: gcc had inlined builtin_redirect, array_assign and
    dish_into into run_one, whose frame grew to 1.6 KB on every level of
    a function's recursion, and `r() { r ; } ; r` ran the kernel stack out
    before the eight-deep stop (func-test hung). They are noinline now and
    run_one's frame is 828 bytes. << and <<< are left to the documents.
  - scripts/biredir-test.sh compares eleven files and three printed
    lines with bash.

v0.60.93 (2026-10-09)  — HISTORY EXPANSION AT THE PROMPT
  - A typed line's !! is the last line, !N leftovers' line N, !-N the Nth
    back, !word the last line starting with word, !$ the last line's last
    word, as bash's at its prompt; the line that comes out is shown before
    it runs and is what history keeps. An event not there says so and
    nothing runs (status 1). Left alone: in '...', \!, a ! before a
    blank, = or ( (so `! cmd` and != stand), "hi!", $! and ${!. Typed
    lines only, as bash, not scripts. Unlike bash, a ! inside (( )) or
    $(( )), or right after [ (a glob's [!a]), is never history: typed tests
    use both, and bash would take them for events.
  - mixer-test.sh waits for the prompt after sizzle and then 3 s, not a
    flat 6 s; it came back silent twice under a loaded full check
    (docs/flakes/2026-10-09-mixer.log), and its error now names the
    exception.
  - scripts/histexp-test.sh types eleven lines.

v0.60.92 (2026-10-09)  — JOB NUMBERS
  - A job put in the background (cook ... &) or set aside (Ctrl-Z) gets a
    number, one more than the highest still alive, as sh's; orders shows
    it (%N at the end of the line) and the set-aside line names it.
    kill, plate, steep and rest take %N, %% and %+ (the newest) and %-
    (the one before); a bare % is still the newest process, as kill %
    always was. A job that has ended keeps its number until it is waited
    for, so rest %1 after kill %1 has its status.
  - scripts/jobspec-test.sh checks eleven things with two and three jobs.

v0.60.91 (2026-10-09)  — . FILE
  - `. FILE [ARGS]` is POSIX's dot command (a special builtin, so it keeps
    its name): FILE is followed in this shell as a sourced file, not as a
    new shell's script. What it sets and defines stays; without ARGS the
    caller's $1..$9 are its (a shift there is the caller's); OPTIND and
    smoke are the caller's, so an EXIT smoke it sets is the shell's;
    return leaves the file, and from a function the dot ran in, only the
    file. Unlike bash, exit there leaves the file and not the shell.
  - scripts/dot-test.sh compares four lines and a clockout with bash's .

v0.60.90 (2026-10-09)  — NICKNAME AND FORGET (ALIAS, UNALIAS)
  - `nickname NAME=TEXT` is sh's alias under a kitchen name: a command
    typed at the prompt whose first word is NAME runs TEXT in its place,
    read again (so TEXT may hold ; and |), and NAME inside its own TEXT
    is not taken again. Bare nickname lists them by name as alias does,
    `nickname NAME` shows one (status 1 if none); `forget NAME` (unalias)
    and `forget -a`. inspect says "a nickname for `TEXT'" (-t alias).
    Not in follow scripts, as bash does not expand aliases in one; a
    clockout forgets them.
  - Unlike bash, a nickname is taken as each command runs, not as the
    line is read: `forget hi ; hi` does not run hi here (written down).
  - scripts/nickname-test.sh compares fifteen lines with bash's alias.

v0.60.89 (2026-10-09)  — BRACE EXPANSION
  - x{a,b}y is xay xby; {1..5} and {5..1}, {a..e}, {01..10} padded,
    {1..10..3} by threes, {-2..2}; nested (a{b,c{d,e}}f) and one after
    another ({x,y}{1,2}), as bash, before every other expansion (so {$v,y}
    is $v's value and y). Left alone: in quotes, \{ \}, {a}, {}, {a..},
    inside $( ), ${ } and $(( )), and in a leading NAME=value. For lists
    too.
  - scripts/brace-test.sh compares nine lines with bash.

v0.60.88 (2026-10-09)  — ARRAYS
  - a=(W...) makes an array, its words split once as sh splits them
    ("$v" one, $v several), a=() an empty one; a[I]=V sets one (I is
    arithmetic). ${a[I]} (-1 the last), ${a[@]} and ${a[*]}, "${a[@]}" a
    word each as "$@" (none: no word), "${a[*]}" one, ${#a[@]} the count,
    ${#a[I]} a length. $a and ${a} are the first element and a=V sets it,
    as sh. stash keeps an array whole and gives it back; discard drops it.
  - An array is one variable, its elements parted by \x1f, so 255 bytes
    in all and dense, not sparse: a[5]=x on three fills 3 and 4 with
    nothing where bash would leave holes (written down). A value is 255
    bytes now everywhere (VAL_MAX; it was 95).
  - scripts/array-test.sh compares eleven lines with bash.

v0.60.87 (2026-10-09)  — EGGTIMER (TIME)
  - `eggtimer CMD` is sh's time under a kitchen name, and a keyword as
    time is: it times the next command, a whole loop, if, case or { },
    or a pipeline, then says `real` to the hundredth (the timer's grain,
    100 Hz; no user or sys here). What it times prints and ends as it
    would, its status kept, expanded once; && and || skip it with what it
    times.
  - scripts/eggtimer-test.sh checks seven uses, and sleeptest's 500 ms
    timed as 0.50 s.

v0.60.86 (2026-10-09)  — $(< FILE)
  - $(< FILE) is the file's text, read by the shell with no program
    started, as bash; the same trailing-newline and splitting rules as
    any $( ), and $f in FILE works. A file that is not there, or not the
    cook's to read, is empty with status 1.
  - scripts/catsubst-test.sh compares seven lines with bash and checks no
    spoon.elf was started.

v0.60.85 (2026-10-09)  — ${NAME^^}, ${NAME,,} AND ${!NAME}
  - ${NAME^} and ${NAME^^} raise the first letter or all of them,
    ${NAME,} and ${NAME,,} lower them; a pattern after (${v^^[le]}) picks
    which characters may change, as bash.
  - ${!NAME} is the variable whose name NAME holds (a script argument's
    number too, so k=2 ; ${!k} is $2); one that is not set is empty.
  - scripts/casevar-test.sh compares eight lines with bash.

v0.60.84 (2026-10-09)  — SLURP -N, -E AND -E
  - slurp takes echo's options as bash's echo does: leading words made of
    n, e and E only (-ne too, "-n" quoted too) are options, any other
    word is printed (-x, --, -, -nx). -n leaves off the newline; -e turns
    \n \t \\ \a \b \e \f \r \v \0nnn \xHH into what they name, keeps an
    unknown \q as it is, and stops everything at \c; -E (the default)
    leaves them.
  - scripts/slurpopt-test.sh compares eight files with bash's echo's,
    byte for byte.

v0.60.83 (2026-10-09)  — HERE-DOCUMENTS IN A SCRIPT'S BLOCKS
  - A <<WORD inside an if, a loop, a { } or a function written over lines
    in a script works now (v0.60.74 skipped it): as follow joins the
    block, each document's lines are read where they are and kept in a
    table, the line naming it by \x02 and a letter where <<WORD was; the
    command it is on gets it each time it runs, expanded then, so a loop
    sees each turn's $i. done <<WORD after a block too.
  - The table lasts as long as the script, so a function defined in a
    block and called later gets its document; one from a script that has
    ended is an empty document, not the terminal. Twenty-six at most.
  - scripts/blockdoc-test.sh compares a script of five such blocks with
    bash.

v0.60.82 (2026-10-09)  — SMOKE (TRAP)
  - `smoke 'CMD' SIG...` is sh's trap under the smoke alarm's name, for
    EXIT (0) and INT (SIGINT, 2). EXIT runs CMD when a follow script ends,
    however it ends (exit N keeps N), or at clockout for one set at the
    prompt; INT runs CMD when Ctrl-C stops a command in a script, which
    goes on with its next line instead of ending. '' ignores INT, - or no
    command resets, bare smoke lists them as trap does. A script starts
    without its caller's (ignored ones stay ignored) and gives them back.
  - Not here: other signals, and INT at the prompt itself.
  - scripts/trap-test.sh runs four scripts against bash's trap, and
    soupOS's own: a caller's EXIT, INT on a typed Ctrl-C, clockout.
  - Found when its first draft was named smoke-test.sh and wrote over the
    gate: two earlier new tests had done the same to old ones. v0.60.65's
    subst-test.sh replaced the $(COMMAND) test of v0.60.3, and v0.60.58's
    random-test.sh the entropy-pool test across boots; check.sh then ran
    the new one twice and the old one not at all. Both old tests are back
    (subst-test waits for the prompt now, not a fixed 8 s, which a loaded
    full check outran), the new ones are paramsub-test.sh and varrandom-test.sh,
    and lint-test.sh now fails when check.sh names a script twice or names
    one that is not there.

v0.60.81 (2026-10-09)  — THE PROMPT WAITS FOR THE REST
  - A typed line that leaves an if, for, while, until, case, { or a
    function open, or that ends in && || or |, asks for more with a >
    prompt until it is whole, as sh, joins the lines as follow joins a
    script's, and runs them as one. Ctrl-C drops it (130); Ctrl-D, or the
    terminal going, is "unexpected end of file" (2). History keeps the
    joined line, so the up arrow runs the whole again.
  - join_onto() and more_line() came out of follow and the typed
    here-document so both use them.
  - scripts/cont-test.sh types eleven such commands.

v0.60.80 (2026-10-09)  — BUILTINS READ <<< AND <<WORD
  - A builtin, a function or an if condition with <<< WORD or a
    here-document on its line reads the text as its input, take above all
    (`take a <<< x` read the terminal, v0.60.76 noted it). `done <<< WORD`
    and `done <<WORD` (and fi, esac, }) feed a whole loop, as done < F.
  - heredoc_mark leaves a mark where <<WORD was, so the document goes to
    the command it was on: before, the first cook of the line took it,
    even `cook a.elf ; cook b.elf <<EOF`, leaving b.elf the terminal.
  - here_take() (out of cook) and feed_text() are what all of them use.
  - scripts/bihere-test.sh compares ten commands with bash.

v0.60.79 (2026-10-09)  — TASTE AND [ ] AS BUILTINS
  - `taste EXPR` and `[ EXPR ]` are taste.elf's test without starting a
    program: one word, !, -e -f -d (as SYS_STAT sees a path), -z -n, = !=,
    -eq -ne -lt -le -gt -ge (64-bit now); 0 true, 1 false, 2 for a usage
    error, a bad integer or a [ without its ]. A quoted "" is a word, so
    [ -z "" ] is true. `cook taste.elf` still works.
  - menu-test.sh skips the empty name its letters-only pattern takes from
    `(p, "[`.
  - scripts/tastebi-test.sh compares eleven lines with bash's test and [.

v0.60.78 (2026-10-08)  — "$@" AND "$*"
  - "$@" was one word: its arguments were joined inside the quote, so
    `for x in "$@"` went round once and a program got one argument for
    them all. It is a word for each argument now, as sh (the quote closed
    and opened between them, so "x$@y" is xa..., ...y), and no word at all
    when there are none. After NAME= it is joined, as sh joins it.
  - $* was not known. Now bare it is split as $@ is, "$*" is one word
    joined by spaces, and one empty word when there are none.
  - Here-documents know $@, $* and $#.
  - scripts/atstar-test.sh compares ten lines and a script with bash.

v0.60.77 (2026-10-08)  — PLUCK (GETOPTS)
  - `pluck OPTSTRING NAME [ARGS]` is sh's getopts under a kitchen name:
    the next option of ARGS, or of $1..$9 without them, into NAME, with
    OPTARG (the rest of the word or the next word, for a letter with a :
    after it) and OPTIND; -acb a letter at a time; at --, a word that is
    not an option, or the end, NAME ? and status 1. An unknown letter or
    a missing argument is NAME ? and a word on the terminal, or quietly
    ? or : with OPTARG the letter when OPTSTRING starts with :.
  - Where it is inside a -acb is kept by the shell and starts again when
    OPTIND is set to something pluck did not write; each follow script
    starts at OPTIND 1, as a new sh does, and the caller's comes back.
  - scripts/pluck-test.sh compares nine lines and a script with bash's
    getopts.

v0.60.76 (2026-10-08)  — SEAL (READONLY)
  - `seal NAME[=value]...` is sh's readonly under a kitchen name: a sealed
    variable cannot be set (=, take, a for loop, a second seal), discarded
    or stashed; each says so and is status 1, and a for loop over one does
    not run its body. A NAME not yet set is sealed empty; bare `seal`
    lists them. A stash given back as its function returns takes a seal
    put on it inside along, as bash's local does.
  - Seen on the way: `take a <<< x` reads the terminal, not the string; a
    here-string reaches programs only. For the next queue.
  - scripts/seal-test.sh compares ten lines with bash's readonly; the bash
    side wraps each failing command in eval, since bash -c gives up at the
    first readonly error and the prompt does not.

v0.60.75 (2026-10-08)  — { ... ; } GROUPS
  - `{ A ; B ; }` runs A and B here, in this shell, so what they set
    stays; its status is the last one's. Like a loop's done, its } takes
    > F, >> F, < F and | PROG for the whole group; && and || after it, X |
    before it, and groups in functions, ifs and loops all work. inspect
    calls { and } keywords; follow joins a group over lines.
  - compound_end() finds where any compound ends (done, fi, esac or }),
    in place of three copies of the same choice.
  - scripts/group-test.sh compares eleven lines with bash.

v0.60.74 (2026-10-08)  — ELIF, AND COMPOUNDS OVER LINES IN SCRIPTS
  - `if A ; then X ; elif B ; then Y ; else Z ; fi`, up to eight elifs:
    the conditions are tried in order and the first true one's branch
    runs; none and no else is status 0, as sh. elif conditions are
    conditions to follow -e, and inspect calls elif a keyword.
  - Found on the way: follow ran a script a line at a time, so an if, a
    loop, a case or a function written over several lines did not work in
    a script at all. Lines are joined now while a compound is open (an
    if, for, while, until, case or { against fi, done, esac or }), with ;
    between them or a space after then, do, else, in, ;; and the like.
    A here-document inside such a block is not read (written down).
  - scripts/elif-test.sh compares nine lines and a script under -e with
    bash; scripts/multiline-test.sh runs a script of compounds over lines.

v0.60.73 (2026-10-08)  — DISH -V
  - `cook dish.elf -v NAME FORMAT [ARGS]` is bash's printf -v: dish runs
    with the rest, and what it wrote, every byte and the last newlines
    too, is NAME's value (96 bytes at most, as every value), not the
    terminal's. A program cannot set the shell's variables, so the shell
    sees it on the line as typed (nothing expanded twice) and runs dish
    with its output captured. A NAME that is not a name is status 2.
  - scripts/dishv-test.sh compares eight lines with bash's printf -v.

v0.60.72 (2026-10-08)  — INSPECT (TYPE)
  - `inspect NAME...` is sh's type under the health inspector's name: a
    shell keyword, a function (and its body), a shell builtin, or a
    program and where cook would find it, in sh's order; `inspect -t`
    says only which (keyword, function, builtin, file), as type -t. Not
    found is status 1.
  - cmd_names (the list Tab finishes from, and inspect's builtins) lacked
    break, continue, return, exit and four self-tests; it has them, and
    menu-test.sh now holds it to dispatch() too.
  - scripts/inspect-test.sh compares four lines with bash's type.

v0.60.71 (2026-10-08)  — 64-BIT ARITHMETIC, AND BASH'S NUMBERS
  - $(( )), (( )), ${v:off:len} and here-documents' $(( )) are 64 bits
    now, as bash's: they wrap at 2**63 as bash's do; / and % come from
    libgcc (__divdi3, __moddi3), which the kernel already linked; shifts
    take their count mod 64; ** squares and multiplies, so 3**1000000000
    is done at once. The 32-bit gap v0.60.66 wrote down is closed.
  - Numbers as bash reads them: 0x1f hex, 017 octal, BASE#DIGITS for a
    base from 2 to 64; a digit too big for its base (08, 2#12) is an
    error.
  - scripts/wide-test.sh compares twelve lines with bash.

v0.60.70 (2026-10-08)  — $( ) IN HERE-DOCUMENTS
  - A here-document's $(CMD) is CMD's output, its newlines kept (but the
    last), as sh; nested ones too; left as typed under <<'WORD'. In a
    script and at the prompt. The gap v0.60.68 wrote down is closed.
  - It runs while a cook is being set up, so what that cook is about to
    use (a pipe in or out, a feed, its document) is put aside around it:
    a cook inside CMD would otherwise take it.
  - scripts/docsubst-test.sh compares two documents' files with bash's
    and types one.

v0.60.69 (2026-10-08)  — A QUOTED * IS A *
  - In ${NAME#pat} (and ##, %, %%), ${NAME/pat/rep} and case patterns, a
    quoted * ? [ ] is the character itself and the rest of the pattern is
    still a glob, as sh: ${v#"a*"}, "$p", \*, case "a"*) and a"["b). Case
    used to take a pattern with any quote in it as wholly literal. The gap
    v0.60.64 wrote down is closed.
  - glob_match takes \x as x itself; glob_pattern makes an expanded
    pattern ready for it (quoted glob characters escaped, quotes dropped).
  - scripts/quoteglob-test.sh compares eight lines with bash.

v0.60.68 (2026-10-08)  — HERE-DOCUMENTS AT THE PROMPT
  - A typed line with <<WORD asks for the document a line at a time with
    a > prompt, up to a line that is WORD, then runs with it as the cook's
    stdin, as sh. Ctrl-C drops the lot (status 130); Ctrl-D on an empty
    line or the terminal going ends the document there, with bash's
    warning.
  - Found on the way: a document was expanded when it was read, before the
    line it belongs to ran, so `v=1 ; cook x.elf <<EOF` saw no v, in
    scripts too. It is kept as typed now and expanded as the cook starts.
    $(( )) is expanded in documents too ($( ) is not, written down).
    And heredoc_mark took the << of $(( 1 << 4 )) for a document (which
    the prompt then waited on); it skips $( ), $(( )) and (( )) now.
  - scripts/promptdoc-test.sh types four documents; heredoc-test.sh gains
    the same-line variable and $(( )).

v0.60.67 (2026-10-08)  — TAKE READS AS READ DOES, AND TAKE -R
  - The queue had it backwards: take did nothing with backslashes, which
    is read -r. Now plain take is sh's read: \x is x, `\ ` is a space
    inside a word, \\ is one backslash, and a \ ending a line joins the
    next line to it. `take -r` keeps every backslash, as read -r. Tabs part
    words as spaces do, in both (a typed tab is taken now too).
  - scripts/taker-test.sh reads one file three ways (take, take -r, one
    name) and two typed lines, against bash's read and read -r.

v0.60.66 (2026-10-08)  — BIT OPERATORS AND ** IN ARITHMETIC
  - $(( )) and (( )) know & | ^ ~ << >> and **, with &= |= ^= <<= >>=,
    at bash's precedence: ** right to left and below the unary signs (so
    -2**2 is 4), shifts between + - and the comparisons, & ^ | between ==
    and &&. A negative power is an error. Shift counts are taken mod 32.
  - The arithmetic is 32 bits (a long on i386); bash's is 64. Values past
    2**31 differ: written down, not changed (64-bit division would need
    libgcc's helpers in the kernel).
  - Found on the way: `(( a | b ))` would have been taken for a pipe;
    pipe_bar skips (( )) now, as split_segs does.
  - scripts/bits-test.sh compares thirteen lines with bash.

v0.60.65 (2026-10-08)  — ${NAME/PAT/REP} AND ${NAME:OFF:LEN}
  - ${NAME/pat/rep} replaces the first longest match of a glob, //
    every one, /# one at the front and /% one at the back; an empty
    pattern changes nothing, except anchored, where it puts rep at that
    end, as bash. \/ is a / in the pattern; the replacement is expanded,
    $X and ${X} in it.
  - ${NAME:off} and ${NAME:off:len} slice it; both are arithmetic, so n,
    $n and 2+1 work; a negative offset (written `: -2` or `:(-2)`) counts
    from the end, and so does a negative length; past the end is empty.
  - scripts/paramsub-test.sh (named subst-test.sh until v0.60.82)
    compares twelve lines with bash.

v0.60.64 (2026-10-08)  — ${NAME#PAT} AND ${NAME%PAT}
  - ${NAME#pat} and ${NAME##pat} take the shortest or longest match of a
    glob off the front of the value, ${NAME%pat} and ${NAME%%pat} off the
    back, as sh. The pattern is expanded first ($X in it) and is a glob,
    * ? and [..] alike; on $1..$9 too. A quoted glob character in the
    pattern is still a glob (sh would take it as itself): written down.
  - scripts/trim-test.sh compares eleven lines with bash.

v0.60.63 (2026-10-08)  — && || THAT STOP EARLY, AND ?: IN ARITHMETIC
  - In $(( )) and (( )), the right side of && is done only when the left
    is true and of || only when it is false; c ? a : b does one arm
    (right to left, so 0 ? 1 : 0 ? 2 : 3 is 3). A side not done is still
    read, but assigns nothing, ++ nothing, and dividing by zero there is
    no error, as in sh.
  - Found on the way: parentheses held only a comparison, so (a=1),
    (1 || 0) and (c ? a : b) inside them did not work; they hold a whole
    expression now, commas included.
  - scripts/ternary-test.sh compares thirteen lines with bash.

v0.60.62 (2026-10-08)  — & BETWEEN COMMANDS
  - A lone & ends a command as ; does: `cook a.elf & rest $!`, `a&b`, and
    `& ;` as before. A cook (or chef cook) goes into the background with
    $! set; a builtin or a function runs at once, the & not printed, there
    being no second shell to run it in (bash would fork one: `x=1 & ...`
    sets x here, not in bash). 2>&1, >&2 and &> are left alone.
  - scripts/amp-test.sh compares ten lines with bash, all ones where bash
    prints in a fixed order. Its bash side had to write \& for & in a
    ${b//pat/rep}: since bash 5.2 a bare & there is the matched text.

v0.60.61 (2026-10-08)  — DONE > FILE, DONE < FILE
  - `done > F` and `done >> F` (and fi, esac) send a whole loop's, if's
    or case's output to a file: the compound feeds spoon (cat), which
    cook sends to F, so builtins and programs in the body both land there.
    `done < F` reads F into the compound, as `spoon.elf F | while` would,
    which makes `while take line ; do ... ; done < F` work. The status is
    the compound's, as sh's; a < file that is not there is status 1 and
    the loop does not run.
  - `done 2> F` stays refused, now with a message naming what is allowed:
    builtins have no stderr of their own, so it could only half work.
  - scripts/loopredir-test.sh compares six files byte for byte and the
    statuses with bash.

v0.60.60 (2026-10-08)  — ! COMMAND
  - `! cmd` runs cmd and turns its status round, 0 to 1 and anything else
    to 0: of a whole pipeline, in if, while and until conditions, with &&
    and ||, twice over, and on a function. A bare ! is 1. follow -e does
    not stop on a ! command, as bash -e does not.
  - Found on the way: `slurp x | cook prog` said "No such program: cook"
    (pipe_into put a cook of its own in front), and so did `a.elf | cook
    b.elf`; a cook starting a later stage is skipped now. A program that
    is not there gives $? 127, as sh's "not found"; it used to leave $?
    as it was.
  - scripts/bang-test.sh compares eighteen lines with bash.

v0.60.59 (2026-10-08)  — SHIFT
  - `shift [N]` drops the first N (1 if none) of a script's or function's
    arguments; $1, $#, $@ and ${1} follow. Fewer than N, or a negative N,
    changes nothing, status 1; a word that is not a number, status 2, as
    bash's. sh's special builtin, so it keeps its name.
  - scripts/shift-test.sh compares five prompt lines and a script with
    bash, a function's own arguments included.

v0.60.58 (2026-10-08)  — $RANDOM
  - $RANDOM, ${RANDOM} and RANDOM in arithmetic give a number from 0 to
    32767, new at each expansion, from the kernel's entropy pool. Assigning
    RANDOM does not seed it, unlike bash: there is nothing to seed.
  - scripts/varrandom-test.sh (random-test.sh until v0.60.82) draws a hundred: all in range, at least 95
    distinct (a hundred from 32768 repeat by chance about one run in
    seven, so the queue's "no repeats" would have been a flake), and all
    six faces of $((RANDOM % 6)).

v0.60.57 (2026-10-08)  — FOLLOW -E
  - `follow -e FILE` stops the script at the first command that fails, with
    that command's status, as bash -e does. Not counted: the condition of an
    if, while or until, and a command an && or || follows. A failure in a
    function the script calls stops the script too. The flag is the
    script's own: it is put back when the script ends, so the prompt and a
    script without -e run on as before.
  - scripts/errexit-test.sh runs two scripts with -e and one without,
    against bash -e and bash.

v0.60.56 (2026-10-08)  — STASH (LOCAL)
  - `stash NAME[=value]` in a function is sh's local under a kitchen name:
    the variable is the function's own, and what it was (or that it was not
    set, and whether it was handed) comes back when the function returns;
    a function it calls sees the function's (sh's dynamic scope); without
    a value it is unset; once per call, so a second stash just sets it.
    Each call keeps its own, so a recursive function's survive its inner
    calls. Outside a function it is an error, status 1. Like hand, its
    NAME=$v words keep an expanded value whole.
  - scripts/stash-test.sh compares nine lines with bash's local.

v0.60.55 (2026-10-08)  — [ABC] AND [A-Z] IN GLOBS
  - glob_match knows bracket expressions: a set, ranges, [!x] or [^x] for
    none of them, a ] first being one of the set, and a [ with no ] being
    just a [. File globs (glob_expand counts a [ as a glob now) and case
    patterns both use it; a quoted or backslashed [ stays text.
  - scripts/bracket-test.sh compares eleven lines with bash in a bowl of
    four files.

v0.60.54 (2026-10-08)  — THE MENU MATCHES THE SHELL
  - `menu` still named jobs, clip, netinfo, ifconfig, arp, ping, nslookup
    and fg, none of them commands since the kitchen renames (orders,
    scraps, faucet, plumbing, table, sip, sniff, plate), and left out some
    thirty that were: steep, the network ones (reserve, holler, takeout,
    hatch, pass, vault), the sound ones (whistle, sizzle, hum, hush), larder,
    skewer, discard, break, continue, return, exit and the self-tests. It
    lists all of them now, with Sound and Network sections.
  - scripts/menu-test.sh reads the menu and dispatch() from the source and
    checks both ways; cat and special stay off it on purpose (the CTF
    build's).

v0.60.53 (2026-10-08)  — BACKSLASH
  - \ is sh's escape now: outside quotes \X is X as text (\  \$ \; \| \'
    and so on), inside "..." \\ \" \$ and \` are the character and other
    pairs stay as typed (so "\n" reaches printf as \n), inside '...' it is
    just a backslash. It gave \ no meaning anywhere before, so "a\\c"
    stayed two backslashes and \; still split the line.
  - expand_seg writes each escaped character in the quoting every reader
    after it already understands (single quotes, or put_lit's form for a
    " inside "..."), so uargv, slurp, first_word and unquote need nothing
    new; split_segs, pipe_bar and the case-pattern scan skip escaped
    characters, since they read the line before it is expanded.
  - scripts/backslash-test.sh compares thirteen lines with bash.

v0.60.52 (2026-10-08)  — HERE-DOCUMENTS IN SCRIPTS
  - In a follow script, `cook prog <<WORD` takes the lines up to one that
    is exactly WORD as the first stage's stdin (a < on the line wins), with
    $NAME, ${NAME}, $1..$9 and $? expanded in them as plain text, and
    nothing expanded with <<'WORD'. The body is fed through the pipe
    here-strings use. At the prompt there is no next line to take, so it is
    a script's (sh would prompt for the lines).
  - scripts/heredoc-test.sh runs a script with four here-documents and
    compares the five files it writes with bash's.

v0.60.51 (2026-10-08)  — MUSIC-TEST COUNTS FROM THE MUSIC
  - Two full checks failed music-test with "the effects never showed",
    the music alone measuring 2466 and 2987, about twice its usual 1291.
    The four seconds before the effects were counted from typing `hum`;
    loading the lump is slow under load, so the effects could start inside
    the 1.0-3.5 s music-alone window, raising the baseline above what the
    effects add. They are counted from the kernel's [music] line now, when
    the score starts. The failure did not come back on demand (twelve runs
    beside a full check passed on the old timing), so this removes the
    likely cause rather than a reproduced one; a FAIL still prints its
    numbers.

v0.60.50 (2026-10-08)  — (( EXPR ))
  - The arithmetic command: status 0 when the expression is not zero, 1
    when it is (an error says so, status 1). The evaluator, here and in
    $(( )), gains assignment (= += -= *= /= %=, right to left), ++ and --
    before and after a name, !, && and || (both sides evaluated), and the
    comma. split_segs no longer splits a line inside (( )), so
    `(( a > 1 && b < 2 ))` stays one command.
  - scripts/arithcmd-test.sh compares twelve lines with bash.

v0.60.49 (2026-10-08)  — DISH (PRINTF)
  - dish.elf is printf under a kitchen name, as the host's (coreutils):
    %s %d %i %x %o %c %%, the - and 0 flags, widths, a precision (the most
    of a %s, the fewest digits of a number), the escapes \n \t \r \a \\ \"
    and \c (no more output), the format used again while arguments remain,
    "" or 0 for one that is not there. Numbers are 32-bit, so a negative
    %x or %o is not GNU's 64-bit one.
  - scripts/dish-test.sh compares fifteen formats with `env printf` byte
    for byte.
  - Found on the way, queued: the soupOS shell gives \ no meaning, so
    "\\" inside double quotes stays two backslashes where sh makes one.

v0.60.48 (2026-10-08)  — $! AND REST (WAIT)
  - $! is the last job started with & (empty before the first). `rest PID`
    (sh's wait, under a kitchen name) waits for that background job of
    this shell's and takes its status; a job that already finished, even
    one the prompt has reported and collected, still gives its status (the
    shell keeps the last sixteen, as sh remembers them); no such job is
    127. `rest` alone waits for all of them, status 0. The job waited for
    is in the foreground meanwhile, so Ctrl-C reaches it (sh's Ctrl-C
    would stop the wait and leave the job running).
  - A job is this shell's when it was started on the same terminal; a task
    with no terminal of its own is on the console, which the first build
    missed (every rest said 127).
  - scripts/rest-test.sh compares twelve lines with bash.

v0.60.47 (2026-10-08)  — RETURN AND EXIT
  - `return [N]` leaves a function with status N, or the last command's
    without one (dispatch now remembers $? as the command began, since it
    clears it first); outside a function it says so, status 2 as bash's.
    `exit [N]` ends a follow script the same way, from inside its loops
    and its functions, and only that script (a script that followed it
    carries on). At the prompt exit points at clockout and does nothing.
    Loops a return or exit passes through end without counting it as a
    break; a function's body starts with no loops of the caller's to
    break, as bash has it.
  - scripts/return-test.sh compares eight lines and four scripts with bash.
    (The first build put follow's cleanup in call_function, whose last
    lines look the same: the shell then thought it was always in a script
    and every exit silently ended the line. The test caught it.)

v0.60.46 (2026-10-08)  — A LOOP, A PROGRAM, THEN A LOOP
  - `until ... ; done | stack.elf | while take x ; do ... ; done` hung the
    shell (while alike): split_segs looked for the | before a compound
    first, so `done | stack.elf` became one segment piping into the second
    loop, and the first loop's done never got its out. The closing word is
    split off that segment too now (split_closer), and a piped compound
    whose closing word also pipes on is the left side of the next one: it
    runs first with its output captured (as a builtin on the left does, up
    to 256 KB) and the next compound reads it through take
    (into_compound, which the X | while case uses too now).
  - loopin-test grows to eleven lines: until into a program into while,
    for into rack into while, if into raise into case.

v0.60.45 (2026-10-08)  — <<< HERE-STRINGS
  - `cook prog <<< WORD`: WORD (quotes removed, $v expanded, spaces kept
    inside quotes) and a newline are the first stage's stdin, fed through
    a pipe by the small feeder task the shell loops use. The later of <
    and <<< wins, as in bash.
  - scripts/herestr-test.sh compares eight files with bash.

v0.60.44 (2026-10-08)  — BREAK AND CONTINUE
  - `break [N]` and `continue [N]` in for, while and until, as sh: N loops
    out (1 by default; all of them if N is more than are open), from
    inside if or case, from a while's condition, and in a piped loop.
    Outside a loop they say so and the status is 0, as bash's. (sh's
    keywords keep their names.) exec_range stops while one is pending and
    each loop takes its share after its body.
  - scripts/break-test.sh compares ten lines with bash.

v0.60.43 (2026-10-08)  — UNTIL
  - `until COMMANDS ; do ... ; done`, while's loop running while the
    condition fails, wherever while goes: nested, `done | prog`, and
    `prog | until take x`. Its status is the body's last, 0 if it never ran.
  - scripts/until-test.sh compares six lines with bash.
  - Found on the way, queued as item 8: `... done | prog | while ...` (a
    loop, a program, then a loop) hangs the shell, until and while alike.

v0.60.42 (2026-10-08)  — ${NAME:-WORD}, ${NAME:=WORD}, ${#NAME}
  - The default when unset or empty, the same that also sets the variable,
    and the length; for a name or a script argument (${1:-none}). The word
    is expanded first, so ${U:-$S-x} and ${U:-"q r"} work as in sh; any
    other form in braces is left as typed.
  - scripts/param-test.sh compares twelve lines with bash.

v0.60.41 (2026-10-08)  — A=1 CMD: A VARIABLE FOR ONE COMMAND
  - `NAME=value [NAME=value...] CMD`, as sh: each is set and handed to
    programs for CMD alone (a builtin or a function sees it too), then put
    back as it was: unset, or its old value and whether it was handed.
    `A=1 B=2` with no command sets both (only one at a time before).
    try_assign's note "not supported: written down, not pretended" is
    retired.
  - scripts/prefix-test.sh compares eight lines with bash: a program, the
    value put back, two at once, a builtin, a function, over a handed one,
    a quoted value.

v0.60.40 (2026-10-08)  — W=$V KEEPS THE WHOLE VALUE
  - v="a b" ; w=$v expanded to `w=a b`, no longer an assignment: v0.60.39
    said "No soup for you: 'w=a b'" and left w empty. sh does not split an
    assignment's value. While the first word is still NAME=... (no blank
    outside quotes since the =), the blanks an expansion brings go in as
    text too (put_lit's mode 2), for $NAME, $1..$9 and $(...) anywhere in
    the value (w=x$v, w=$v$v, w=pre-$v-post).
  - scripts/assign-test.sh compares nine lines with bash, plain words still
    splitting among them.

v0.60.39 (2026-10-08)  — WHAT AN EXPANSION GIVES IS TEXT
  - expand_seg put a value into the line as it was, and everything that
    read the line after it (uargv, slurp, first_word, unquote, cook's
    tokens) took its ' and " for quotes and, on a cook line, its | < > &
    for pipes and redirections: v="it's" ; slurp $v printed "its", and
    v='x > Y.TXT' ; cook call.elf $v would have made Y.TXT. sh never
    re-reads an expansion. Each character from $NAME, $1..$9, $@ and
    $(...) now goes in through put_lit, written so those readers take it
    as text: outside quotes ' and " wrapped in the other quote and | < > &
    in single quotes; inside "..." only " needs it. Globs are left alone,
    as sh globs an unquoted expansion.
  - scripts/litexp-test.sh compares thirteen lines with bash and checks
    the > made no file.

v0.60.38 (2026-10-08)  — SLURP SPLITS WORDS AS ECHO DOES
  - Measured first, against bash: programs' arguments (uargv) and for's word
    list already split an unquoted $VAR at blanks, as sh does; slurp alone
    did not, for expanded text or typed text: `slurp a    b` and `slurp $v`
    (v="x   y") kept every space where echo prints "a b" and "x y", and an
    empty $v left two spaces. slurp now splits its words at blanks outside
    quotes and prints them with one space between; quoted stretches keep
    their spaces and '' is a word of its own, as echo has them.
  - scripts/wordsplit-test.sh compares eleven lines with bash.
  - Queued: quote characters that come out of an expansion are still read
    as quotes (v="it's" ; slurp $v loses the '), which sh never does.

v0.60.37 (2026-10-08)  — A WHOLE LOOP INTO A PROGRAM
  - `for/while ... done | PROG`, `if ... fi | PROG`, `case ... esac | PROG`:
    the whole compound's output goes to PROG, as in sh. Before, `done |
    PROG` was taken for a plain done and the pipe dropped without a word
    (`while slurp y ; ... done | skim.elf -n 3` printed to the screen for
    ever). split_segs notes PROG on the closing word; exec_range starts PROG
    and runs the compound as the feed (v0.60.33's streaming), so an endless
    loop stops when PROG has what it wants. It nests: a loop piped inside a
    piped loop, and `prog | while take ... done | prog`.
  - Anything else after done, fi or esac (`done > F`) is refused with a
    message rather than ignored.
  - scripts/pipeloop-test.sh compares eight lines with bash.

v0.60.36 (2026-10-08)  — TALLY -S AND -W
  - tally.elf -s SEP puts SEP between the numbers (as written: any length,
    empty too) and a newline after the last; -w pads them with zeros to the
    width of FIRST or LAST as typed, as GNU seq measures it: `seq -w 007 9`
    is three wide, a leading + does not count, the step never does, and a
    negative is padded after its sign (-09).
  - seq-test.sh grows from ten forms to twenty-one, all byte for byte.

v0.60.35 (2026-10-08)  — SIFT -N AND -O
  - sift.elf -n puts "N:" before each line (or match); -o prints only the
    matching part, each match on a line of its own, non-overlapping and as
    written when -i folds. As GNU grep 3.12 does it, measured: with -v or an
    empty pattern -o prints nothing, though the lines still count for the
    exit code; -c counts lines whatever -o says; -n does nothing with -c.
  - A trap found measuring it: in an interactive shell here `grep` can be
    a function wrapping ugrep, which differs on exactly those three cases.
    Test scripts run /usr/bin/grep, GNU 3.12 (checked); probe with
    `command grep`.
  - grep-test.sh grows from seven forms to fourteen.

v0.60.34 (2026-10-08)  — A PROGRAM INTO A SHELL LOOP
  - `cook spoon.elf F | while take line ; do ... ; done` (and for, if, case
    after the |): the shell itself reads the program's output. split_segs
    marks the segment before a | that is followed by a compound; exec_range
    starts it writing into a pipe (a cook line runs alongside the loop; a
    builtin is captured and a task feeds it in), runs the compound with
    take reading that pipe instead of the keyboard (no echo), then closes
    the pipe, so a program still writing ends, and collects it.
  - take from a pipe returns 1 at the end even when a last line without a
    newline was read (its words still set), as bash's read: `while take`
    does not run for that line.
  - The loop runs in this shell, so what it sets stays set (as ksh, zsh and
    bash's lastpipe do; plain bash would forget). Programs inside the loop
    still read the keyboard, not the pipe.
  - scripts/loopin-test.sh compares eight lines with `bash -O lastpipe`.
  - Found on the way, queued: soupOS does not split an unquoted $VAR into
    words, so `slurp $line` keeps "a  b" where bash's echo prints "a b".

v0.60.33 (2026-10-08)  — A BUILTIN'S PIPE STREAMS
  - v0.60.27 ran the left side of `builtin | prog` to the end before the
    right side started, so `forever | skim.elf -n 2` (forever a function
    that loops for ever) never came back: on v0.60.32 the prompt was still
    gone after 60 s. Now cook starts the right side first and then calls
    back (pending_feed); the left side runs with a terminal that writes into
    the pipe as it prints (512 bytes at a time). When the reader has gone
    the terminal stops writing and counts a Ctrl-C, which ends a loop on
    the left as sh's SIGPIPE would. The capture buffer and feeder task are
    gone.
  - bpipe-test adds the never-ending function: 13 lines, 14 files.
  - Found on the way, queued as item 8: `while ... ; done | prog` takes
    `done | prog` for the loop's done and drops the pipe without a word.

v0.60.32 (2026-10-08)  — CD -, $PWD AND $OLDPWD
  - PWD and OLDPWD are ordinary variables, as in sh: logging in (at the
    console or over SSH) sets PWD, every cd that moves sets OLDPWD to $PWD
    as it was and PWD to the new bowl, a cook may set or discard either,
    and `cd -` goes to $OLDPWD and prints it ("cd: OLDPWD not set" and
    status 1 without one). A failed cd is status 1 now (it was 0).
  - Variables go from 16 to 24 slots so those two take none of a cook's;
    when every slot is full cd still moves, the two just do not follow.
  - scripts/cd-test.sh compares seven lines with bash, PWD=mine and a
    discarded OLDPWD among them.

v0.60.31 (2026-10-08)  — 2>&1 > F IN SH'S ORDER
  - v0.60.21's 2>&1 meant "wherever stdout ends up", so `2>&1 > f` sent
    stderr to f. sh takes the copy where stdout points when it reads the
    2>&1: before the > that is the terminal (or the pipe, when a | follows),
    after it the file. cook now notes which came first; 2>&1 before a > on
    the last stage sends stderr to the terminal's own stdout (err_as_out 2),
    so `x=$(cook pair.elf A B 2>&1 > f)` catches the message in x and f
    stays empty, as in bash.
  - stderr-test grows to twelve lines and fifteen files.

v0.60.30 (2026-10-08)  — LONG WORD LISTS, AND NOTHING RUN CUT SHORT
  - `for i in $(cook tally.elf 6000)` ran 64 times: every command was
    expanded into 256 bytes, cut silently, and a loop kept 64 words. A for
    loop now expands its words into 32 KB and keeps up to 8192 (6000 runs
    as bash's does), its $(...) capture grows to fit, and a list longer
    than that says it was cut and runs what it kept.
  - Expansion takes a size and says when it cut (expand_into). A plain
    command still has 256 bytes, but one too long once expanded is now
    refused, status 1, rather than run cut short, where it could have named
    another file.
  - scripts/longword-test.sh: two 6000-word loops against bash, the cut
    message, the refusal.

v0.60.29 (2026-10-08)  — POUR IS CAT
  - pour read at most 8 KB (then a "truncated" note), dropped every byte
    outside printable ASCII, and always added a newline, so `pour X | prog`
    (possible since v0.60.27) was not `cat X | prog`. It now streams the
    whole file in 4 KB pieces; into a pipe or a $(...) the bytes go exactly
    as they are. On a terminal it still drops control bytes, so a file
    cannot send escape sequences to a cook's screen, and it adds a newline
    only when the file lacks one, so the prompt starts on its own line. A
    missing file is status 1.
  - bpipe-test gets three pour cases: exact, 226 KB, and 4 KB of random
    bytes through mince.elf against hexdump -C.

v0.60.28 (2026-10-08)  — MUSIC-TEST LISTENS FOR THE EFFECTS
  - music-test judged fixed seconds of the capture: music 1.0-3.5 s, with
    effects 5.0-6.5 s, music again 7.5-9.0 s. The effects are typed four
    seconds into the music, and measured they start at 6.00-6.25 s, so the
    "with effects" window held a quarter second of them at best and passed
    on 2227 against a bar of 1807; under a full check's load they started
    later still and it failed (twice today, the reruns passing).
  - It now finds their start in the capture: the first quarter second half
    again as loud as the loudest quarter second of the music alone (which
    swings from 430 to 2275; the effects measure 3800-6200), and measures
    from there. With effects is now 4040-4490 against the same bar, twelve
    runs six at a time all pass, and a FAIL line carries the numbers (check.sh
    keeps only FAIL lines, which is how two failures' numbers were lost).

v0.60.27 (2026-10-08)  — A BUILTIN INTO A PROGRAM
  - `slurp hi | raise.elf`, `myfunc | stack.elf`: a command that is not a
    cook line can now pipe into programs. Only cook lines made pipes before;
    anything else got `|` and the program's name as words. The left side
    runs with its stdout captured (as for $(...), up to 256 KB), a kernel
    task feeds the capture into a pipe, and the right side runs as a cook
    line with that pipe as its first stage's stdin (a `<` there wins). The
    status is the right side's. The left side finishes before the right
    starts, so one that never ends holds the line (sh would stream).
  - scripts/bpipe-test.sh compares nine lines with bash, 6000 lines from a
    function and a reader that stops after one among them.
  - Found on the way, queued: pour is an 8 KB viewer that adds a newline,
    not cat; and a for loop's words are cut at 256 bytes, so
    `for i in $(cook tally.elf 6000)` sees only the first 64.

v0.60.26 (2026-10-08)  — PROGRAMS GET AN ENVIRONMENT
  - `hand NAME`, `hand NAME=value` is sh's export under a kitchen name (the
    queue said export; its own rule says every new builtin gets one). A
    handed variable goes to every program run after it, in every stage of
    a pipeline; a name handed before it is set is handed once it is, and
    `discard` drops both the variable and the mark, as sh does. `hand`
    alone lists them.
  - The kernel keeps each process's environment (512 bytes of NAME=value
    lines) and a new syscall, SYS_ENV (13), copies it out; ulib's getenv()
    reads it. labels.elf is env, and printenv given a NAME (status 1 if it
    was not handed).
  - scripts/env-test.sh runs eight lines in soupOS and in bash (export,
    unset, env, printenv) and compares ten files. tab-test's `| lab<Tab>`
    is `| lay<Tab>` now: labels.elf made "lab" two programs.

v0.60.25 (2026-10-08)  — RACK BY A FIELD, ONCE
  - rack.elf takes -k N[,M] (from the start of field N to the end of field
    M, or of the line), -t C (fields split on C; otherwise a field starts
    with the blanks before it, as GNU sort counts them), and -u (the first
    line of each run the keys call equal, with the whole-line comparison
    off for it, as GNU sort does). Flags combine (-nru) and a value may sit
    in the same word (-k2,2, -t,).
  - sort-test.sh grows from five forms to fifteen, all matching the host's
    LC_ALL=C sort byte for byte.

v0.60.24 (2026-10-08)  — SPREAD
  - spread.elf turns tabs into spaces as `expand` does under LC_ALL=C:
    stops every 8 columns, or every N with -t N; a backspace steps back a
    column and is kept, a newline starts the count again, and every other
    byte, \r included, is one column (GNU expand does not reset on \r).
  - scripts/spread-test.sh compares six forms with the host's expand byte
    for byte.

v0.60.23 (2026-10-08)  — STACK
  - stack.elf prints lines last first, as `tac` does: each line keeps its
    own newline, so a last line without one joins the next line out
    ("a\nb" gives "ba\n"), as tac prints it.
  - scripts/stack-test.sh compares ten forms with the host's tac byte for
    byte, 180 KB among them.

v0.60.22 (2026-10-08)  — FUNCTIONS
  - `name() { cmd ; cmd ; }` on one line defines a function (sixteen per
    shell; defining one again replaces it). `name a b` runs the body with
    $1..$9, $# and $@ set for it and the caller's given back after, as
    `follow` does for a script; its status is the body's last. The body is
    kept as written and expanded at each call, so loops, case and ${NAME}
    work inside; variables are shared with the caller, as in sh. Eight
    calls deep at most. Clockout forgets them with the variables.
  - split_segs keeps a definition whole (its braces counted outside quotes,
    ${...} not counted). scripts/func-test.sh compares ten lines with bash.
    qemu_keys.py types { } @ and ^ now.
  - Not yet: piping a function (or a builtin) into a program; queued.

v0.60.21 (2026-10-08)  — 2>
  - `cook X.elf 2> ERR.TXT`, `2>> ERR.TXT` and `2>&1`, on any stage of a
    pipeline: the redirection belongs to the stage it follows, as in sh.
    A program's stderr used to go to the console (the serial log) or the
    session whatever was asked. The file gets the checks > has (one shared
    open_for_writing now), and every 2> file is opened before any stage
    starts.
  - 2>&1 sends stderr wherever stdout ends up, the pipe or the > file or
    the screen: a flag on the process, not a second handle, so nothing is
    closed twice. (`2>&1 > f` meant what `> f 2>&1` means until v0.60.31.)
  - scripts/stderr-test.sh runs ten lines with pair.elf (cmp) and compares
    twelve files with bash's. The host side cannot send cmp's stdout to
    /dev/null: GNU cmp goes silent, its EOF message included, when it does.

v0.60.20 (2026-10-08)  — THE GATE FAILURE ITS OWN LOG SAID PASSED
  - A full check failed the gate's jobs check ("no ring-3 output after the
    kill") though the kept log passes it. The check was
    `after N | grep -qE TESTOUT` under pipefail: grep -q stops at its first
    match, awk dies of SIGPIPE writing the rest, and pipefail fails the
    pipeline. Replayed on that log: 39 false failures in 2000 runs on an
    idle host, none without pipefail. 96 piped greps (81 lines, 31 scripts) had the
    shape, and the negated ones (`! ... | grep -q X`) could pass while X was
    there. They now pipe into `grep ... >/dev/null`, which reads to the end
    (0 of 2000 either way), and scripts/lint-test.sh fails on any new one.
  - The same run's desk failure ("services are not busy", fbcon at 55%)
    did not come back: 12 desk segments at once, and 12 more beside a full
    check that took 425 s instead of 150, measured fbcon at 2% at most. The
