v0.60.19 (2026-10-08)  — CASE
  - `case WORD in PAT|PAT) ... ;; (PAT) ... ;; *) ... ;; esac` on one line,
    in a loop, nested, with several commands in an arm and the last ;;
    optional. The first arm that matches runs and no other; none matching
    is status 0. Patterns are expanded like words and matched with the
    shell's globs (* and ?), or as plain text where quoted.
  - scripts/case-test.sh types fourteen lines and compares their output
    with bash's. qemu_keys.py can type ( and ) now.

v0.60.18 (2026-10-08)  — CTRL+R
  - Ctrl+R at the prompt searches the leftovers, newest first, as bash's
    reverse-i-search does: the line reads (leftovers)`query': match.
    Typing narrows it, Ctrl+R again steps to an older match (past copies
    of the one shown), Backspace widens it again from the newest. Enter
    runs the match; Ctrl+C or Ctrl+G gives back the line as it was; any
    other key (End, an arrow, Esc) keeps the match on the prompt to edit.
    "(no leftovers)" says the query matches nothing.
  - scripts/search-test.sh runs each of those and reads back what ran.

v0.60.17 (2026-10-08)  — LEFTOVERS OUTLIVE THE SHIFT
  - A cook's history is kept on the disk: the shift's end (clockout, an
    SSH session ending, reheat or freeze) writes their LEFTOVER file, the
    last 16 lines oldest first, and logging in, at the console or over
    SSH, loads it. The up arrow and `leftovers` survive a clockout and a
    reboot. Not after every line: a file changing under every command
    changed under `portions` and `peek -l` as they measured it (du-test
    and lsl-test caught that), and bash writes at the end too. A power cut
    loses the shift in progress.
  - Where: /home/<cook>/LEFTOVER, only while that home is still the cook's;
    the headchef's home is the root, where everyone looks, so theirs is
    /etc/LEFTOVER. Owner read and write only: another cook is refused by
    the shell and by the kernel's ring-3 open.
  - Not in the CHALLENGE=1 build, whose ring-3 open is unchecked on
    purpose: every cook's history would become something to read there.
  - scripts/leftovers-test.sh boots the same disk twice, with a reheat
    between. cooks-test now expects fire to hand over three entries: the
    home, the file and the history.

v0.60.16 (2026-10-08)  — LAYER
  - layer.elf puts up to eight files side by side as `paste` does: the Nth
    line of each on one line, a tab between, or the -d list taken in turn
    (starting over each line; \t \n \\ and \0 for none). A file that has
    run out gives an empty field until all have; - is stdin.
  - scripts/layer-test.sh compares ten forms with the host's paste byte
    for byte. qemu_keys.py can type \ [ and ] now.

v0.60.15 (2026-10-08)  — KNEAD
  - knead.elf folds lines to a width (-w N, default 80) as `fold` does
    under LC_ALL=C, and with -s at the last space or tab. Tabs, backspace
    and carriage return move the column as they do in fold; a character
    wider than the width by itself gets a line of its own.
  - scripts/knead-test.sh compares nine forms with the host's fold byte
    for byte, widths 1 to 80, with and without -s.

v0.60.14 (2026-10-08)  — MINCE
  - mince.elf dumps a file or stdin in hex, in `hexdump -C` form: offset,
    sixteen bytes in two groups, the printable ones between bars, `*` for a
    full line repeating the one before, and the length last. It streams.
  - scripts/mince-test.sh compares eight forms with the host's hexdump -C
    byte for byte, 4 KB of random bytes among them.

v0.60.13 (2026-10-08)  — A CLIPBOARD PER COOK
  - The clipboard was one buffer for the whole kernel: what the saucier
    cut over SSH, the headchef at the console could paste, and the other
    way round. Each cook now has their own (src/clip.c keeps one per uid).
    Keyed by cook, not terminal: `$(scraps)` runs on a capture terminal of
    its own, and one cook's two sessions share theirs.
  - fire wipes the fired cook's, so a cook hired into the same uid starts
    with an empty one.
  - scripts/clip-test.sh has the console and two SSH sessions at once; on
    the previous kernel three of its five checks failed.

v0.60.12 (2026-10-08)  — THE NEXT COOK STARTS CLEAN
  - The console's shell outlives the cook at it, and clockout left three
    things behind for whoever clocked in next: the history (`leftovers`
    listed the headchef's `hire` lines, and the up arrow brought them
    back), shell variables ($SAUCE still set) and the clipboard (Ctrl+V
    pasted what the last cook cut with Ctrl+U). Clockout now wipes all
    three, and $1..$9, before the next login.
  - scripts/clockout-test.sh leaves one of each as the headchef and looks
    for them as the next cook; it failed on all three before the change.
  - The gate's boot segment gave proof.elf's hold 7.5 s and it needs
    6.5-7 s; it failed about one run in three, on the last commit's build
    too. The drive now waits for the hold's result line (UNTIL).

v0.60.11 (2026-10-08)  — DREGS KEEPS ONLY THE DREGS
  - dregs.elf read its whole input into a buffer that doubled, and the bump
    allocator kept every old copy: `tally.elf 1000000 | label.elf | dregs.elf
    -n 3` (14 MB) filled swap and dregs died ("could not swap ... back in").
    It now keeps only what could still be among the last N lines, dropping
    the rest whenever its 64 KB buffer fills, and grows only when N lines
    take most of the buffer by themselves.
  - headtail-test.sh has that case (it failed before the change) and a
    300 KB line from a file and through a pipe; it waits for each prompt
    with UNTIL rather than a fixed two seconds.

v0.60.10 (2026-10-08)  — TAB KNOWS WHERE IT IS
  - Tab used to offer the builtins and the current bowl's names for the
    first word, and only the current bowl's for any other, listing them all
    at once when more than one fit. It now finishes what fits where the
    word stands: builtins at the start of a command (the line's, or after
    ; && || then do else), programs (.ELF in the root and the current bowl)
    after `cook` or a |, and paths anywhere else, through bowls
    (/tabs/pa<Tab> looks in /tabs), after > and < too.
  - One fit gets a '/' after a bowl or a space after anything else. More
    than one: the part they share; the next Tab lists them, as bash does.
    Lowercase typing stays lowercase on an all-capitals 8.3 name (cook
    fli<Tab> is flip.elf).
  - Tab lists a bowl only if the cook may read it: completing into another
    cook's home shows nothing, as peek would. scripts/tab-test.sh types
    fifteen lines and reads back what each became; removing the check makes
    it fail.

v0.60.9 (2026-10-08)  — LABEL
  - label.elf numbers every line as `nl -ba` does: the number right-aligned
    in six columns, then a tab, empty lines included, and a newline added to
    a last line that has none. It streams in 4 KB pieces, so a million lines
    go through a pipe in constant memory. nl's logical-page delimiter lines
    are not special.
  - scripts/label-test.sh compares ten forms with the host's nl -ba byte for
    byte, up to seven-digit numbers.
  - qemu_keys.py has UNTIL:<text>, which waits for <text> in the serial log
    after the last line typed; label-test waits for each prompt that way
    instead of guessing how long a million lines take.
  - Found on the way: dregs.elf keeps its whole input in memory, so 8 MB of
    piped lines fills swap and it dies. Queued as the twenty-fifth queue's
    item 5.

v0.60.8 (2026-10-08)  — FLIP
  - flip.elf turns every line backwards, byte by byte, as rev does under
    LC_ALL=C: the newline stays at the end of its line and a last line with
    no newline comes out with none. It reads a file or stdin.
  - scripts/flip-test.sh compares nine forms with the host's rev byte for
    byte (an empty line, a 6 KB line, a file of only newlines, pipes, and a
    file flipped twice coming back unchanged).

v0.60.7 (2026-10-08)  — KITCHEN NAMES FOR THE TEST AND DEMO PROGRAMS
  - As the user asked after v0.60.6: hello.elf is greet, hog glutton, spin
    whisk, crash drop, lazy proof (dough proofs slowly, as demand paging
    does), deep stockpot, hotcold fridge, marker ticket, memtest measure,
    systest mise (mise en place), unhex unwrap, upper raise. Every program
    on the disk now has a kitchen name.
  - No kernel code depended on the names (only comments and help text);
    soupyc's upper() string function is not the program and keeps its
    name. Their output markers (LAZY ..., "hello from ring 3", ...) are
    unchanged, since the gate and tests read them. CLAUDE.md's mentions
    are updated; this file's history keeps the names each release had.

v0.60.6 (2026-10-08)  — KITCHEN NAMES FOR THE NEW COMMANDS
  - The commands added since v0.52.0 had taken Unix names, against the
    queues' own rule; the user asked for kitchen names. Builtins: who is
    brigade, last is logbook, du is portions, run is follow (a recipe is
    followed), unset is discard, read is take (an order is taken).
    Programs: grep.elf is sift, head skim, tail dregs, sort rack, uniq
    cull, tee divvy, cut slice, tr swap, seq tally, test taste, find
    forage, cmp pair - each .elf, and their messages start with the new
    name (pair's "pair: EOF on ..." where cmp says "cmp:").
  - The Unix-named programs that were there before are renamed too, as
    the user asked next: cat.elf is spoon, echo call, wc weigh, ls peek,
    rm toss, mkdir newbowl. Their stderr markers (WCOUT, LS, LSL, RM,
    MKDIR) keep their names, since the gate and tests read them. The test
    and demo programs (hello, hog, spin, crash, lazy, deep, hotcold,
    marker, memtest, systest, unhex, upper) keep theirs.
  - Unchanged: sh's own syntax (for, if, while, do, done, then, else, fi,
    $?, $((...))). The tests still compare each tool with the host's
    grep, sort, cmp, wc and the rest under their real names; only the
    soupOS side is renamed. ~/profile is now started with follow.

v0.60.5 (2026-10-08)  — ~/profile AT LOGIN
  Twenty-third queue, item 3.
  - If the cook's home holds a file named profile (the headchef's: /profile),
    it is run as `run` runs a script - through run_line, with the cook's
    permissions and a fresh Ctrl-C mark - after the welcome and the
    message of the day at every interactive login: console, pass, vault;
    not `ssh host cmd`. Variables it sets stay for the session; an error in
    it is said and the login goes on.
  - scripts/profile-test.sh, in check.sh: a cook writes ~/profile (X=7, a
    line appended to a file, an unknown command); it runs at the next
    console login and at an interactive SSH login but not for `ssh host
    whoami` (two lines); $X is 7 after login; the unknown command is said
    and the login carries on.

v0.60.4 (2026-10-08)  — read
  Twenty-third queue, item 2.
  - `read NAME...` reads a line from the terminal (console, session, or a
    capture's empty input) into variables as sh's read does: a word each,
    the last name taking the rest, the spaces around trimmed. At end of
    input before anything was typed the names are set empty and the status
    is 1.
  - Found by the test: the line splitter cut at a ; inside $( ... ), so
    `$(read Q ; cook echo.elf st=$?)` never reached the substitution. It
    no longer splits inside $( ... ), as it already did not inside quotes.
  - scripts/read-test.sh, in check.sh: a script asks, the console answers
    "hello  world" and "one two three"; a whole line, `read X Y` with the
    last taking the rest, and read at end of input inside $(...) (status
    1) match sh fed the same two lines.

v0.60.3 (2026-10-08)  — COMMAND SUBSTITUTION
  Twenty-third queue, item 1.
  - $(COMMAND) runs COMMAND (a program, a pipeline, a builtin: anything a
    line can hold) with a capture terminal in place of the shell's and the
    task's - every builtin writes through the one and every program
    through the other, which a spawned program inherits - and puts what it
    wrote in place: trailing newlines dropped, the rest become spaces, as
    sh's splitting makes them of an unquoted $(...). Right after NAME= the
    result is kept whole, as sh keeps an assignment's. Not inside '...'.
    The status is COMMAND's.
  - Found by the test: the shell's own "cooking X..." and "[X exited with
    code N]" lines were captured; they are not printed into a capture. And
    a program's stderr, which on a session's terminal goes to that
    terminal, was captured too; a capture passes stderr to the terminal it
    replaced, so only stdout is captured, as with sh.
  - Written down: a Ctrl-C pressed while a $(...) runs is seen when it
    ends; nesting stops at two.
  - scripts/subst-test.sh, in check.sh: eight lines - in an argument, an
    assignment kept whole, for over a $(...), wc -l < F, an empty result,
    a pipeline, '$(...)' kept, the status after - leave what sh leaves.

v0.60.2 (2026-10-08)  — SCRIPT ARGUMENTS
  Twenty-second queue, item 3.
  - `run FILE a "b c" d`: the words after the file (quotes grouping them)
    are the script's $1..$9; $# is how many, $@ all of them joined by
    spaces; not expanded inside '...'. A script run from a script gets its
    own, and the caller's are given back after. Outside a script $# is 0.
  - scripts/args-test.sh, in check.sh: a script run with `one "two words"
    three` leaves what `sh P.sh one "two words" three` leaves - $#, $1,
    $2 with its space, $@, for over $@, a nested script's own $1, the
    caller's back after it - and $# is 0 at the prompt. qemu_keys can now
    type '#'.

v0.60.1 (2026-10-08)  — while LOOPS, AND Ctrl-C STOPS A SHELL LOOP
  Twenty-second queue, item 2.
  - `while COMMANDS ; do COMMANDS ; done`: the condition is the commands
    between while and do; the status is the body's last, or 0 if the body
    never ran, as sh does. for and while share one match for done, so
    either nests in the other; `while X` splits like `if X`.
  - A shell loop runs no program between some commands, so Ctrl-C had
    nothing to kill there. Each terminal now counts Ctrl-C presses (term_t
    intr: the console's from the keyboard IRQ, a session's from its stream
    input); the outermost run_line notes the count, and loops, scripts
    (run) and the line's remaining commands stop once it has moved, status
    130 as sh gives for SIGINT. A program running at the press is still
    killed as before.
  - scripts/while-test.sh, in check.sh: a counting loop with $((...)), a
    loop that never runs and for inside while leave what sh leaves, with
    the same status after; Ctrl-C stops an endless `while cook test.elf 1
    = 1` and the next line runs with $? 130.

v0.60.0 (2026-10-08)  — ARITHMETIC
  Twenty-second queue, item 1.
  - $(( EXPR )) in expand_seg: integers, + - * / % and parentheses, unary
    - and +, the comparisons < <= > >= == != (1 or 0), variables as NAME
    or $NAME (unset or not a number: 0), with C's precedence as sh has.
    A recursive-descent evaluator. Division by zero, or something that is
    not an expression, is said, and the command is not run, status 1. One
    difference, written down: sh running a script ends the whole script on
    such an error; soupOS stops that command only.
  - Found by the test: echo.elf, rm.elf and mkdir.elf had room for 16
    arguments and dropped the rest silently (echo printed 16 of 17
    values). 128 bytes of arguments hold up to 64; they take 64 now.
  - scripts/arith-test.sh, in check.sh: 17 expressions (precedence,
    parentheses, the comparisons, negative division and remainder, an
    unset name) give sh's values; N=$((N+1)) twice counts to 2; 1/0 is
    said, its command not run, status 1.

v0.59.4 (2026-10-08)  — cmp.elf
  Twenty-first queue, item 3.
  - user/cmp.c: two files byte by byte, with GNU cmp's messages and codes
    in the C locale, measured on the host: the same, exit 0 and silence;
    "A B differ: char N, line M" on stdout and exit 1; "cmp: EOF on 'A'
    after byte N, line M" when the common part ends a line, "..., in line
    M" inside one, "... which is empty" for an empty file, on stderr and
    exit 1; "cmp: A: No such file or directory" and exit 2.
  - The wording was measured, not remembered: in a UTF-8 locale GNU cmp
    says "byte" for "char" and uses curly quotes; the first run (written to
    the UTF-8 wording) differed on exactly those, and on "in line".
  - scripts/cmp-test.sh, in check.sh: eight pairs (differ both ways, the
    same, a prefix on either side, empty, missing, a prefix with no final
    newline) give the host's stdout, stderr (from the serial log) and
    status.

v0.59.3 (2026-10-08)  — for LOOPS AND if BLOCKS
  Twenty-first queue, items 1 and 2 (one executor for both).
  - run_line is now split, expand and execute: the line is split into
    segments on ; && || as before, with do, then, else and if separated
    from the command after them (repeatedly: `do if ...`, `then for ...`);
    exec_range walks the segments, and `for NAME in WORDS ; do ... ; done`
    and `if ... ; then ... ; [else ... ;] fi` find their matching done or
    fi by counting nesting and run their bodies through it. Each command
    is expanded just before it runs, so $NAME takes each value in turn.
    The words of a for are expanded (vars, globs, quotes dropped); its
    status is the last command's; an if whose condition failed and has no
    else leaves 0, as sh does. Eight levels deep at most.
  - glob_expand (the v0.58.3 glob code, now with a callback) serves both
    program arguments and for's words; set_var serves NAME=value and for.
  - Found by the test: the first split of `do if X` left `if X` whole, so
    the condition was empty and every turn took the then branch.
  - scripts/control-test.sh, in check.sh: eight lines - a for, a for over
    a glob, if/else both ways, if inside for, for inside if with a quoted
    word, the status after a loop and after an if with no else - leave the
    same files as sh running the same lines.

v0.59.2 (2026-10-08)  — find.elf
  Twentieth queue, item 3.
  - user/find.c: the starting path, then every name under it, one per line,
    as find prints; -name PATTERN (* and ?, on the last component; quote
    it, or the shell globs it first, as with sh) and -type f|d. A bowl it
    cannot list is reported on stderr and makes the exit code 1, as find
    does, and the rest carries on. SYS_STAT tells it whether the start is
    a bowl.
  - scripts/find-test.sh, in check.sh: a tree copied in with mtools (files
    three deep, an empty bowl); five forms list what the host's find lists
    over the same tree, both sorted, since FAT and ext4 list in different
    orders; for the ordinary cook, find /home lists saucier's closed home
    but does not enter it, says so, and exits 1.

v0.59.1 (2026-10-08)  — ls.elf -l
  Twentieth queue, item 2.
  - ls.elf -l prints mode, owner and size for each entry through
    SYS_STAT, in serve's columns, with machine-readable "LSL" lines on
    stderr. Programs cannot read the roster to turn a uid into a name, so
    ustat_t now carries the owner's name too (owner_name[16]).
  - scripts/lsl-test.sh, in check.sh: for /, /etc and /home, every entry's
    mode, owner, name and size from ls.elf -l equals serve's (57, 2 and 1
    entries); for the ordinary cook, ls -l /etc shows the kitchen they may
    not read; /etc's file sizes equal mtools'. (The first run compared a
    size taken before a later login had grown /etc/logins.)

v0.59.0 (2026-10-08)  — SYS_STAT
  Twentieth queue, item 1.
  - Programs could open and list but not ask about a path. SYS_STAT (12)
    fills a ustat_t: size (0 for a bowl), bowl or not, owner, mode. It
    needs search on every bowl above the path (users_reach) and nothing
    on the path itself, as stat does; the CHALLENGE build leaves it
    unchecked, like its open.
  - test.elf's -e -f -d use it, so the difference written down in v0.58.1
    is gone: a file the cook may not read exists for them, as with test.
  - scripts/stat-test.sh, in check.sh: the ordinary cook's test -f sees
    /etc/kitchen (rw----, unreadable to them); nothing inside saucier's
    closed home exists for them, though the home is still a bowl; the
    headchef reaches the file inside. testcmd-test's 21 cases still match
    the host's test.

v0.58.3 (2026-10-08)  — GLOBS
  Nineteenth queue, item 3.
  - A program argument with an unquoted * or ? is matched against the
    names in the bowl it names (or the cwd); only its last component may
    hold the pattern. Matches are sorted by byte, as sh sorts in the C
    locale; a leading * or ? does not match a name starting with '.'; no
    match passes the word as it is, as sh does. A word with any quote in
    it is not expanded (sh would expand its unquoted parts; written down).
  - Arguments that no longer fit in 128 bytes are refused with "The
    arguments are too long" and status 1; stage_add_arg used to cut them
    short silently.
  - scripts/glob-test.sh, in check.sh: *.txt, ?.txt, a*, *, a pattern with
    no match, a quoted pattern, *.TXT and /G/*.dat give what sh gives over
    the same names; thirty long names under /MANY/* are refused, status 1.
    testcmd-test's per-case wait is 1 s (it was the check's longest test).

v0.58.2 (2026-10-08)  — LOWERCASE 8.3 NAMES FROM OTHER TOOLS
  Found by glob-test.
  - Linux and mtools write a lowercase name that fits 8.3 (a.txt) as A.TXT
    with case flags in byte 12 (bit 3: base lowercase, bit 4: extension
    lowercase) and no long name. soupOS ignored the flags and showed
    A.TXT. fill_entry now applies them, except when byte 12 carries
    FAT_PERM_MARK (soupOS's own mode before v0.41.0).
  - lname-test.sh: an mtools-copied lc.txt is listed by ls.elf as lc.txt.

v0.58.1 (2026-10-08)  — test.elf
  Nineteenth queue, item 2.
  - user/test.c: a condition's truth as the exit code (0 true, 1 false, 2
    a usage error or a bad integer), as test does: one argument is true if
    not empty; ! negates; -e -f -d FILE; -z -n STRING; = and !=; -eq -ne
    -lt -le -gt -ge. Made for && and ||: `test.elf -f F && pour F`.
  - Two differences, written down: programs have no stat call, so -f is
    "opens for reading and is not a bowl" and -d "can be listed", and a
    file the cook may not read counts as absent; -a and -o are not
    supported (&& and || do the same).
  - scripts/testcmd-test.sh, in check.sh: 21 cases, each run as `cook
    test.elf ... ; cook echo.elf $?`, exit as the host's test does over the
    same file and directory.

v0.58.0 (2026-10-08)  — SHELL VARIABLES
  Nineteenth queue, item 1.
  - NAME=value as the whole of a command sets a variable in this shell (the
    value unquoted, so A="two  words" keeps both spaces); $NAME and ${NAME}
    are replaced in run_line's expansion pass with $? and ~, not inside
    '...'; an unset name is empty; `unset NAME` removes one. Each shell
    (the console, every session) has its own sixteen.
  - Not supported, and written down: `A=1 cmd` (a variable for one command
    only); exported environments (programs get no environment).
  - scripts/vars-test.sh, in check.sh: a script run with `run` leaves the
    same files as sh running the same lines ($A, ${A}y, "$B", '$A', an
    unset name, A=$A$A, unset, assignments joined by ;, S=$?); an SSH
    session does not see the console's variable, nor change it.

v0.57.7 (2026-10-08)  — seq.elf
  Eighteenth queue, item 4.
  - user/seq.c: `seq LAST`, `seq FIRST LAST`, `seq FIRST STEP LAST` with
    GNU seq's rules for integers: FIRST and STEP default to 1; a run that
    cannot reach LAST in STEP's direction prints nothing; STEP 0 is an
    error; -3 is a number, not an option.
  - scripts/seq-test.sh, in check.sh: ten forms (one, two and three
    arguments, negatives, counting down, empty runs, seq 0, a pipe into
    sort.elf -r) compared byte for byte with the host's seq.

v0.57.6 (2026-10-08)  — BOWLS GET LONG NAMES, AND DELETES TAKE THEIRS AWAY
  Eighteenth queue, item 3 (found testing item 1).
  - fat_mkdir wrote no long names: `mkbowl "/a bowl"` stored the bowl as
    A BOWL, a space inside an 8.3 name, and cd and rmbowl could not find
    it. A bowl whose name 8.3 cannot hold now gets an alias and a
    long-name chain the way fat_write gives a file one (an 8.3-only name
    if the directory has no run of free slots, as for files).
  - Found the same way: deleting a long-named file marked only its short
    entry, and fsck reported "Orphaned long file name part". New
    erase_lfn_before() clears the LFN entries just before a short entry
    that carry its checksum; fat_delete and fat_rmdir both call it, so
    strain, relabel and rmbowl leave no orphans.
  - scripts/lname-test.sh, in check.sh: a bowl named with a space can be
    entered and written in; a long-named bowl and file are removed whole;
    mtools finds a kept long-named bowl and the long-named file inside;
    fsck is clean. On v0.57.5 three of the four failed.
  - Lowercase bowls now get long names too (they always needed them: 8.3
    has no lowercase), so /etc and /home are ETC~1 and HOME~1 with long
    names "etc" and "home" on disk, and du shows "saucier/" rather than
    "SAUCIER/". du-test's judge, which walked the image by 8.3 names, now
    assembles long names as a FAT reader does.

v0.57.5 (2026-10-08)  — run FILE
  Eighteenth queue, item 2.
  - `run FILE` runs a file of command lines as the cook who runs it: each
    line through run_line, so ; && || $? ~ and quotes work; '#' lines are
    comments; nothing stops it but the end or a clockout; its status is
    the last line's. The cook must be able to read the file. Scripts may
    run scripts, eight deep at most, so one that runs itself stops with a
    message instead of running the kernel stack out.
  - scripts/run-test.sh, in check.sh: a script with a comment, && ||,
    quotes, ~ and $? leaves the same files with the same contents as sh
    running the same lines (status 42 after); a cook's script runs as the
    cook (~ is their home, /etc stays closed, status 1); a script the cook
    may not read is refused; a script that runs itself stops eight deep
    and the shell carries on.

v0.57.4 (2026-10-08)  — BUILTINS TAKE QUOTED ARGUMENTS
  Eighteenth queue, item 1.
  - Builtins took their raw text, so `pour "my file.txt"` looked for a name
    with quotes in it. Two helpers with uargv's rules: first_word() reads
    one word (quotes dropped), used by split_two (decant, relabel), perms
    and stir; uq_args() unquotes the rest of the line for the builtins
    that take one path or a text: cd, pour, strain, jot, mkbowl, rmbowl,
    du, slurp, hash.
  - Found by the test, and queued as item 3: `mkbowl "/a bowl"` reports
    success but fat_mkdir writes no long names, so the bowl is stored as
    `A BOWL` (a space inside an 8.3 name) and cd and rmbowl cannot find
    it. Quoting itself is tested here with an 8.3 bowl name.
  - scripts/bquote-test.sh, in check.sh: pour, stir (a quoted name and
    quoted text with two spaces), decant then relabel (both kinds of
    quote), perms, strain on names with spaces; mkbowl, cd and rmbowl on a
    quoted bowl; slurp keeps its quoted spaces.

v0.57.3 (2026-10-08)  — ~ IS THE COOK'S HOME
  Seventeenth queue, item 2.
  - A word that is ~ or starts with ~/, unquoted, at the start of a word
    (after a space, or right after < or >), becomes the current cook's
    home: /home/<cook>, or / for the headchef. It is done in run_line with
    $?, so builtins (cd ~, pour ~/X) get it as well as programs and
    redirections. '~', "~/x", a~b and ~x are left alone, as in sh.
  - One difference from sh, written down: with a home of /, sh makes ~/X
    into //X; soupOS makes it /X.
  - scripts/tilde-test.sh, in check.sh: > ~/H.TXT and cat.elf ~/H.TXT
    land in the cook's home; cd ~ and pour ~/H.TXT work; a line mixing ~,
    '~', "~/x", a~b, ~x and ~/ matches sh run with HOME=/home/cook; the
    headchef's ~ and ~/X are / and /X.

v0.57.2 (2026-10-08)  — QUOTES IN THE SHELL
  Seventeenth queue, item 1.
  - Arguments were split on every space, so `grep.elf "two words"` and
    `tr.elf ' ' _` could not be written. '...' and "..." now keep what
    they hold, spaces and | < > & ; included, as one argument, and the
    quotes are dropped, as sh does; $? is expanded inside "..." but not
    inside '...'.
  - How a quoted space reaches a program, decided and written down:
    programs still get one argument string, now with the quotes left in,
    and the new ulib uargv() splits it as sh would, dropping them. It
    replaces nine copies of the same space-splitting tokenizer (grep,
    head, tail, sort, uniq, wc, tee, cut, tr); echo, cat, ls, rm and mkdir
    use it too, so echo joins its arguments by one space as sh's echo
    does. The shell unquotes the words it uses itself: the program's name
    and the files after < > and >>. run_line does not split on ; && ||
    inside quotes.
  - Builtins still take their raw text: `pour "my file"` is not unquoted.
    Written down; programs are where quoting matters for now.
  - scripts/quote-test.sh, in check.sh: eight lines (spaces kept inside
    quotes, grep and tr with quoted arguments, | ; && inside quotes, '$?'
    kept, a file name with a space written with > and read with cat)
    match sh byte for byte. qemu_keys can now type both quotes.

v0.57.1 (2026-10-08)  — $? IN THE SHELL
  Sixteenth queue, item 3.
  - `$?` is replaced by the last status when its part of the line runs, as
    in sh: in `a || echo $?` it is a's.
  - Found while writing it: a program killed by a fault had status
    code & 0xFF of its -(128+exception) exit code, 114 for a page fault,
    which meant nothing. A negative code now gives -code, sh's 128+n: 137
    for a kill (as before), 142 for a page fault. One difference from sh,
    written down: Ctrl-C gives 137 (every kill is the same kill), where sh
    says 130 for SIGINT.
  - scripts/status-test.sh, in check.sh: $? after an exit 0 (0), after
    hello.elf (42), after crash.elf's fault (142), after an unknown command
    (127), twice in one part after || (42 42), after Ctrl-C (137), and
    after a builtin the cook may not run (1). qemu_keys can now type '$'.

v0.57.0 (2026-10-08)  — ; && AND || IN THE SHELL
  Sixteenth queue, item 2.
  - run_line splits a command line on ; && and || (a single & or | stays
    with its command) and runs the parts left to right, as sh groups
    them: `a ; b` both, `a && b` b only after a 0, `a || b` b only after
    a failure; a skipped command leaves the last status standing. The
    console, `ssh host cmd` and /etc/rc all go through it.
  - A builtin's refusal is now a failure: deny(), cook_err() and the
    headchef's gate set the status to 1 (an unknown command was already
    127, a program its exit code, a kill 137).
  - Found by the test: each part kept the space before the operator, so
    `reheat ` was an unknown command and `pour /etc/kitchen ` looked for a
    file with a space in its name. Parts are trimmed.
  - scripts/chain-test.sh, in check.sh: nine chains (; && || alone and
    mixed, an unknown command, a pipe inside a chain) leave the same
    marker files as the host's sh running the same lines with (exit 42)
    for hello.elf; for the ordinary cook a refused builtin makes && skip
    and || run. qemu_keys can now type ';'.

v0.56.9 (2026-10-08)  — tr.elf
  Sixteenth queue, item 1.
  - user/tr.c: translate, -d delete, -s squeeze, with GNU tr's rules in
    the C locale: ranges (a-z) and the escapes \n \t \\; a '-' at either
    end of a set is itself; the second set padded with its last character;
    a character named twice takes its later mapping; -s squeezes runs of
    the last set named; -d -s deletes the first and squeezes the second.
    A backwards range is refused, as tr refuses it.
  - scripts/tr-test.sh, in check.sh: one input with mixed case, digits,
    runs, punctuation, spaces and a tab; ten forms compared byte for byte
    with the host's tr.

v0.56.8 (2026-10-08)  — cut.elf
  Fifteenth queue, item 4.
  - user/cut.c: -f LIST with -d C (a tab by default), GNU cut's rules: the
    list takes N, N-M, N- and -M, comma-separated; fields come out in the
    line's order, each once, whatever order the list names them; a line
    with no delimiter is printed whole; a last line with no newline gets
    one. -dC and -fN may be written attached.
  - scripts/cut-test.sh, in check.sh: a passwd-like file (with a line
    with no colon), a tab-separated one and a comma one with empty fields
    and no final newline; ten forms (lists out of order, open ranges at
    either end, a field past the last, attached options, a pipe) compared
    byte for byte with the host's cut.

v0.56.7 (2026-10-08)  — tee.elf, AND O_APPEND FOR PROGRAMS
  Fifteenth queue, item 3.
  - SYS_OPEN gains O_APPEND (2), mapped to VFS_APPEND and checked like
    O_WRITE (write on the file, or on its bowl for a new one).
  - user/tee.c: stdin to stdout and to up to six files, -a to append.
    A file that cannot be opened is reported on stderr (so stdout stays
    exactly the data, as GNU tee keeps it) and the rest carry on; the
    exit code is then 1.
  - scripts/tee-test.sh, in check.sh: one file, -a appending (onto a file
    with no final newline) and two files, stdout and every file compared
    byte for byte with the host's tee doing the same steps; the ordinary
    cook's tee into /etc is refused and logged and exits 1 while their own
    home's file and stdout still get the data. The first run caught tee
    printing its complaint into stdout; fixed before commit.

v0.56.6 (2026-10-08)  — wc.elf LEARNS -l, -w AND -c, IN THE HOST'S FORMAT
  Fifteenth queue, item 2.
  - wc.elf printed "1 3 14" its own way and read only stdin. It now takes
    -l, -w and -c and a file, and prints GNU wc's format as measured on the
    host: one count bare; several each right-aligned to the width of the
    byte count, one space apart; a file argument's name after. Whitespace
    is space, tab, newline, CR, VT and FF, as the host counts it. The
    WCOUT serial marker the gate reads is unchanged.
  - One known difference, written down rather than hidden: GNU pads to 7
    when its input is a pipe, and wc.elf cannot tell a pipe from a
    redirected file (there is no fstat), so it always uses the file rule.
  - scripts/wc-test.sh, in check.sh: 13 forms (each flag and pair, no
    final newline, an empty file, 20000 lines, stdin by redirection, odd
    whitespace) compared byte for byte with the host's wc. append-test now
    expects " 1  3 14", the host's format, for its piped wc line.

v0.56.5 (2026-10-08)  — uniq.elf
  Fifteenth queue, item 1.
  - user/uniq.c: adjacent duplicate lines folded into one, with -c (counts
    in GNU's "%7d " format) and -d (only lines that repeat); streams, with
    two growing line buffers; a last line with no newline is a line and
    is printed with one.
  - scripts/uniq-test.sh, in check.sh: adjacent and non-adjacent repeats,
    repeated empty lines, a last line with no newline equal to the one
    before, a single unterminated line and an empty file; eight forms,
    sort.elf | uniq.elf -c among them, each compared byte for byte with the
    host's uniq.

v0.56.4 (2026-10-08)  — > KEEPS BIG OUTPUT, AND SAYS WHEN IT CANNOT
  Fourteenth queue, item 5 (found measuring sort.elf).
  - A redirect kept the whole output in one block of the 8 MB kernel heap,
    doubled with krealloc, and wrote it on close. Past about 2 MB the
    doubling failed; the write returned -1, programs ignored it, and the
    close wrote what there was. Proven on v0.56.3: a 5 MB copy through `>`
    left exactly 2097152 bytes, with exit code 0 and nothing said.
  - The bytes now live in 4 KB physical pages (all of RAM is
    identity-mapped), and the close streams them to the FAT through
    fat_write_from, the whole-file write generalised to pull from a source
    (fat_write is that with a plain buffer). >> preloads the pages.
  - A write that cannot get memory, or a flush that fails (a full disk),
    marks the handle; vfs_close now returns -1 for it, and at process exit
    the kernel prints "[prog: the output to /F did not reach the disk
    whole]" on the cook's terminal, logs it, and turns an exit code of 0
    into 1.
  - Measured after: sort.elf results of 1, 3 and 5 MB through `>` all match
    LC_ALL=C sort; a 7 MB one on a disk with less room is refused loudly
    (log, terminal, exit 1). Note on measuring: a driver that counted
    "sort.elf exited" matched two lines per process and typed the next
    command early; it now counts only the [proc N] line.
  - scripts/redirect-test.sh, in check.sh: a 5 MB output through > matches
    its source (cmp); one that does not fit is reported on the terminal and
    in the log and exits 1; fsck is clean. On v0.56.3 the first two failed.

v0.56.3 (2026-10-08)  — sort.elf
  Fourteenth queue, item 4.
  - user/sort.c: lines in order, -r and -n, with GNU sort's rules in the C
    locale (unsigned bytes, prefix first; -n reads blanks, '-', digits and
    a '.' fraction, a line with no number is 0, and -n ties fall back to
    the whole line's bytes; -r reverses all of it; a last line with no
    newline is printed with one). The input is read whole into memory,
    then a merge sort over pointers.
  - Largest input, measured through a pipe into wc.elf: 5 MB (191219
    lines) in 13.75 s and 7 MB (267205 lines) in 36 s, both exactly the
    input's line and byte counts, swapping hard. The next doubling of its
    buffer would need 16 MB, so about 8 MB is the ceiling; past it malloc
    fails and sort says so.
  - Measuring found a bug that is not sort's: output redirected with `>`
    past about 2 MB is cut short or lost, and the program still exits 0.
    The redirect buffers the whole file in the 8 MB kernel heap and a
    failed growth is ignored. Queued as item 5.
  - scripts/sort-test.sh, in check.sh: words with case, duplicates, a
    prefix pair, an empty line, bytes above 127 and no final newline;
    numbers with negatives, decimals, blanks, -0, lines with no number and
    ties; five forms compared byte for byte with LC_ALL=C sort.

v0.56.2 (2026-10-08)  — head.elf AND tail.elf
  Fourteenth queue, item 3.
  - user/head.c: the first N lines (-n N, default 10) of a file or stdin,
    bytes copied as they are, stopping once it has N lines.
    user/tail.c: the last N; the whole input is read into memory (a pipe
    cannot be read backwards), growing by doubling, then the lines are
    found from the end. Both copy a last line with no newline without one,
    as GNU head and tail do.
  - scripts/headtail-test.sh, in check.sh: 13 forms over a 25-line file, a
    3-line file with no final newline, an empty file and a 2000-line file
    past tail's first 4 KB, with -n 0, files shorter than N and pipes
    (head into tail too); each compared byte for byte with the host's head
    and tail. The pipe stopping early (head after 4 lines while cat still
    writes) needs no work: a write with no reader left returns -1.

v0.56.1 (2026-10-08)  — grep.elf
  Fourteenth queue, item 2.
  - user/grep.c: lines containing a fixed string, from a file or stdin,
    with -v, -c and -i (ASCII folding), like grep -F. Lines of any length
    are matched whole (the line buffer grows); a last line without a
    newline is still a line and is printed with one; exit 0 if anything
    was selected, 1 if not, 2 for usage or an unreadable file.
  - scripts/grep-test.sh, in check.sh: one input with mixed case, an empty
    line, a 3000-byte line and no final newline; seven forms (plain, -v,
    -c, -i, -c -i, -v -i, as a pipe stage) each written to a file inside
    soupOS and compared byte for byte with LC_ALL=C grep -F on the host;
    and no match exits 1.

v0.56.0 (2026-10-08)  — >> APPENDS, AND REDIRECTION ASKS THE BOWL
  Fourteenth queue, item 1.
  - `>>` appends: VFS_APPEND starts the write buffer with what the file
    holds (the whole-file write on close keeps it), and makes the file if
    it is not there. The tokenizer had always split `>>` into two `>`, so
    the second became the output file's NAME; it is one token now.
  - Found on the way: `>` to a NEW file never checked the bowl. Proven on
    v0.55.6: the ordinary cook's `cook echo.elf evil > /etc/EVIL.TXT` made
    the file in a bowl closed to cooks. A new file now needs write on its
    bowl, as SYS_OPEN has always asked; > and >> alike.
  - scripts/append-test.sh, in check.sh: `>` then two `>>` give the same
    bytes as the host shell's (cmp); a pipeline's output appends; >> makes
    a missing file; a cook appends in their own home; neither > nor >>
    makes a file in /etc for a cook; fsck is clean.

v0.55.6 (2026-10-08)  — A MESSAGE OF THE DAY
  Thirteenth queue, item 3 (item 2, swap shares, is set aside; see the
  queue's notes).
  - /etc/motd, if it exists, is shown after every interactive login: the
    console, the pass (which is the console) and vault sessions. Not to
    `ssh host command`, whose output a script may be reading. Control
    characters other than newline and tab are dropped, so the file cannot
    send a terminal escape sequences; at most 2 KB is shown. The file is
    the headchef's: /etc is closed to cooks, and users_seal_system now pins
    /etc/motd rw-r--.
  - scripts/motd-test.sh, in check.sh: a motd with an embedded clear-screen
    escape; a console login and an SSH login show it with the ESC byte
    dropped, `ssh host whoami` gets its answer and no message, and the
    file is the headchef's, rw-r--.

v0.55.5 (2026-10-08)  — ONE COOK CANNOT FILL THE DISK AGAINST THE HEADCHEF
  Thirteenth queue, item 1.
  - Nothing stopped a cook filling the volume. Proven on v0.55.4: with the
    disk nearly full, the ordinary cook copied files into their home until
    no cluster was left; then the headchef's `vault 22` failed ("could not
    read or write the host key on disk") and a newly hired cook got no
    home (with a message wrongly saying the home was not theirs). The
    roster and login record still squeezed through, rewriting clusters
    they already held, but only until they needed one more.
  - The last FAT_RESERVE_CLUSTERS (128, 256 KB) are now kept, as ext keeps
    5% for root: fat_alloc_cluster refuses a cluster from the reserve
    unless users.c's hook allows it - the headchef, or a task inside a
    kernel-internal write made on anyone's behalf (users_sys_begin/end
    around the roster, the login record, the host key, AUTHKEYS rewrites
    and homes). The free count was already kept up to date. kmalloc does
    not zero, so the new task field is set in both creation paths; left
    out, a cook's task could have started "inside" a system write.
  - hire now says when a home could not be made (disk full) rather than
    that it belongs to someone else.
  - scripts/reserve-test.sh, in check.sh: with 160 clusters free, the
    cook's copies stop at the reserve (130 left, refusal logged); the
    headchef still opens the vault and hires a cook, who gets a home, with
    the roster saved; fsck is clean.

v0.55.4 (2026-10-08)  — REFUSALS CANNOT FLUSH THE LOGIN RECORD
  Twelfth queue, item 3.
  - /etc/logins kept the newest 64 lines whatever they were. Measured on
    v0.55.3: 64 wrong logins typed at the console took 81 s and pushed the
    headchef's real login out of the record. Through the pass and chef
    (up to 8 s a guess, no lockout) the same takes about 8-9 minutes;
    through the vault, which locks an address out after 10 failures in 5
    minutes, about half an hour.
  - Decided: keep successes and refusals apart rather than only keeping
    more lines, since more lines only raises the count a guesser needs.
    The newest 48 of each are kept, in time order, so a refusal can only
    push out a refusal. Re-measured: after 64 refusals the headchef's real
    login is still there, with 48 refusals kept.
  - last-test.sh seeds 48 successes and 48 refusals; after its logins each
    kind is back to 48 with its own oldest gone, and all six real logins
    are still in the record.

v0.55.3 (2026-10-08)  — ONE COOK CANNOT FILL THE PROCESS TABLE
  Twelfth queue, item 2.
  - The process table has eight slots and nothing limited how many one
    cook took. Proven on v0.55.2: the ordinary cook started eight hog.elf
    in the background and clocked out, and the headchef's `cook hello.elf`
    got "Too many orders".
  - proc_spawn now limits an ordinary cook to PROC_PER_COOK (4) slots,
    zombies included since they hold a slot until reaped, and all ordinary
    cooks together to PROC_MAX - PROC_RESERVE (6), so two are always left
    for the headchef, who is not limited. Each refusal says which limit
    and is logged.
  - owner-test.sh, a second boot: cook's fifth hog.elf is refused by the
    per-cook limit; saucier's third by the reserve (four plus two); and
    the headchef still cooks hello.elf twice.

v0.55.2 (2026-10-08)  — chef GETS THE BRAKES
  Twelfth queue, item 1.
  - `chef <command>` asks for the headchef's secret, so it is a door like
    the vault and the pass, but it had no brakes. Measured on v0.55.1: 20
    wrong guesses from one SSH session in 8.8 s, 137 a minute, none
    recorded. Now (ordinary build) each attempt is recorded in /etc/logins
    as "headchef" from "chef:<cook>", and a wrong one costs 1, 2, 4, then
    8 s, counted per cook so more sessions do not reset it; a right secret
    clears the count. Measured after: 8 a minute, all 20 recorded.
  - The CHALLENGE build keeps the old chef: its stage 2 is a single chef
    with an offline-cracked preimage, and that build stays as it was.
  - privs-test.sh: two wrong secrets at chef cost 1 s then 2 s, and the
    record holds both refusals and the success, naming the cook.

v0.55.1 (2026-10-08)  — THE PASS GETS THE VAULT'S RULES
  Eleventh queue, item 2.
  - The pass door hands its caller THE console's shell. Measured on
    v0.55.0: with the headchef logged in at the console, a stranger on the
    pass typed `whoami` and was the headchef, with no password; ten wrong
    passwords took 8 s, the client's own pace; refusals were recorded as
    "console".
  - The pass now accepts a caller only while the console is at its login
    prompt, and tells anyone else the console is in use. A caller who
    hangs up logged in leaves the console clocked out (any foreground
    program gets a Ctrl-C), so the next person does not inherit the
    session. Over the pass a wrong secret costs 1, 2, 4, then 8 s, and the
    count lives in the console's login loop, so reconnecting does not
    reset it. Logins and refusals are recorded with the caller's address.
    The login prompt now asks again on a bare Enter, which the pass sends
    on arrival. No idle limit: the pass is the console, which never times
    out.
  - pass-test.sh logs in through the pass (it used to rely on the shared,
    already logged-in console - the hole itself), and a second boot checks:
    a caller is turned away while the headchef is logged in; three wrong
    secrets take 7 s or more (10.4 measured); a caller can log in; one who
    hangs up logged in leaves the console clocked out and the next caller
    is let in; both events are logged; /etc/logins names 10.0.2.2.

v0.55.0 (2026-10-08)  — WHAT A COOK MAY NOT DO
  Eleventh queue, item 1.
  - hatch, the HTTP file server, checked nothing. Proven on v0.54.2: the
    ordinary cook ran `hatch 80` and the host fetched /etc/kitchen (every
    PBKDF2 secret) and /HOSTKEY.ED (the vault's private key) over HTTP,
    undoing the last two releases. hatch now serves only public files:
    read for all cooks on the file and search on every bowl above it
    (users_public), whoever opened it, since an HTTP client is anonymous;
    anything else gets 403 Forbidden and a log line.
  - Proven on v0.54.2 too: the cook closed the vault the headchef had
    opened, changed its settings, and rebooted the machine. vault and pass
    with arguments, reheat, freeze, spill, fatstress and bowltest are now
    the headchef's (one gate at dispatch, each refusal logged); a bare
    `vault` or `pass` still shows the status, and `chef <command>` still
    runs one as the headchef for a cook who knows the secret.
  - scripts/privs-test.sh, in check.sh: the cook's hatch answers 403 for
    the roster and the host key and 200 for a public file; all nine
    commands refuse the cook, each logged; the vault stays open and the
    pass shut; the bare status works; chef elevates; the machine never
    goes down.

v0.54.2 (2026-10-08)  — EVERY SYSTEM FILE HAS A KNOWN OWNER AND MODE
  Tenth queue, item 3.
  - /HOSTKEY.ED, the vault's private host key, was rwxr-x. Proven on
    v0.54.1: an ordinary cook read its 32 bytes, and the public key
    derived from them on the host was the one the vault served, so a cook
    could impersonate the server.
  - users_seal_system pins every system file the kernel writes or trusts
    to the headchef: /etc and /home rwxr-x, /etc/logins and /AUTHKEYS
    rw-r--, /HOSTKEY.ED rw----, /etc/kitchen rw---- (ordinary build;
    CHALLENGE keeps stage 2's readable roster). Applied at boot and by the
    code that writes each file (host key creation, fire's AUTHKEYS
    rewrite).
  - /etc/rc is deliberately not pinned: it runs as the headchef, so
    resetting its mode would bless whatever a cook had written. Instead
    run_rc refuses an /etc/rc that is not the headchef's or that other
    cooks can write, like sshd's StrictModes. Defence in depth: /etc is
    already closed to cooks, and the test confirms a cook cannot write it.
  - secrets-test.sh: a cook cannot read the host key from the shell or a
    program, cannot write /etc/rc, an /etc/rc made writable by cooks is
    not run at the next boot, and every system file shows its pinned owner
    and mode after a session of use. On v0.54.1 three of these failed.

v0.54.1 (2026-10-08)  — FIRE REMOVES THE COOK'S SSH KEYS
  Tenth queue, item 2.
  - /AUTHKEYS lines name a cook, not a person, and fire left them. Proven
    on v0.54.0: saucier had a key, was fired, a new saucier was hired, and
    the old key logged straight into the new account. fire now removes
    every "saucier ssh-ed25519 ..." line (ssh_forget_keys, beside the
    parser in ssh.c) and says how many; other cooks' lines and bare
    (headchef) lines stay.
  - scripts/secrets-test.sh, in check.sh: real ed25519 keys from
    ssh-keygen for saucier, cook and a bare line; after fire and rehire
    the old key is refused, cook's and the headchef's still work, fire
    reports one key removed, and mtools sees the other two lines kept. On
    v0.54.0 the same test failed three of its five checks.

v0.54.0 (2026-10-08)  — THE ROSTER IS THE HEADCHEF'S ALONE
  Tenth queue, item 1.
  - /etc/kitchen was rwxr-x. Proven on v0.53.4: an ordinary cook read
    every cook's PBKDF2 secret with pour and with cat.elf, free to attack
    them offline. In the ordinary build the roster is now the headchef's,
    rw----, set every time it is saved and once at boot so an older disk
    is closed too. The kernel reads and writes it directly, so login,
    `secret` and `hire` work for cooks who cannot read it.
  - The CHALLENGE build keeps it readable on purpose: reading it IS stage 2
    ("Salt to Taste"). That build was compiled by check.sh but never
    booted, so nothing would have noticed a hardening change reaching it.
    New scripts/challenge-test.sh, in check.sh in a tree of its own,
    builds and boots it and checks an ordinary cook reads the headchef's
    unsalted AlphaSOUP-32 hash.
  - cooks-test.sh: a cook cannot read the roster from the shell or a
    program, still changes their secret and logs in with it, and the
    roster stays rw---- after the rewrite.

v0.53.4 (2026-10-08)  — du
  Ninth queue, item 5.
  - `du [bowl]`: each entry with the clusters and bytes under it, then the
    total, which also counts the bowl's own clusters. Clusters are the
    chains' real lengths walked through the FAT (fat_chain_clusters,
    fat_first_cluster), not sizes rounded up. A bowl the cook may not read
    is marked, not entered.
  - mtools' mdu turned out not to be a usable judge: it never counts a
    bowl's own cluster and does count each `..` entry as a file (::/etc
    reports 2 for two one-cluster files; a bowl holding one one-cluster
    file reports 2). The judges are the FAT and fsck instead.
  - scripts/du-test.sh, in check.sh: du / equals the clusters in use in the
    FAT and fsck.fat's count; its bytes equal the image's files; a home's
    and /home's clusters and bytes equal a walk of the image; a cook sees
    another's closed home marked. The first run was off by 52 bytes, one
    login line written after du ran; the test now measures last.

v0.53.3 (2026-10-08)  — IDLE SESSIONS END
  Ninth queue, item 4.
  - The vault has four session slots, and a forgotten session held one
    forever. `vault idle <seconds>` ends a session whose client has sent
    no keystrokes for that long (0, the default, is never; /etc/rc can set
    it). The client is told why in a DISCONNECT it prints ("idle for 5
    seconds: the vault ended this session"), the ending is logged, and the
    session leaves the way a hang-up does, so its slot and shell are freed.
    The console never times out.
  - scripts/idle-test.sh, in check.sh: by default a session idle 7 s
    survives; with `vault idle 5` a silent one ends in 4.5-8 s with the
    reason shown; one that keeps typing outlives the limit; the ending is
    logged; the console still answers and `who` lists no ended session.

v0.53.2 (2026-10-08)  — last
  Ninth queue, item 3.
  - Logins left only klog lines, which die with the machine. Every console
    and SSH login, and every refused password, now appends a line to
    /etc/logins: when, ok or refused, the cook (a refused name is cut to
    15 printable characters), and console or the client's address. The
    newest 64 lines are kept. The file is always the headchef's, rw-r--,
    though it is written as whoever is logging in; a mutex serialises the
    four SSH workers and the console. SSH's "none" probes are not logins
    and are not recorded.
  - `last` shows the record newest first, refusals in red.
  - scripts/last-test.sh, in check.sh: the disk is seeded with a full
    64-line record; a first boot has a refused and an accepted console
    login and an SSH login after a wrong password; after a reboot `last`
    shows all of them newest first, mtools reads the file, the oldest
    seeded line is gone with 64 kept, the file is the headchef's, and fsck
    is clean.
  - console-test failed twice in full checks. The first explanation given
    here (a slow last answer) was wrong; the kept transcript of the second
    showed "cook: eadchef". The test's boot wait stopped at the first 2 s
    of silence, so under load it typed into the bootloader, which ate the
    first byte. Measured: a byte sent once the kernel runs reaches the
    login; one sent before it exists is lost. Reproduced by freezing QEMU
    3 s during the bootloader: the old test failed 2 of 2 with
    "eadchef", the fixed one (which waits for the login prompt) passed
    2 of 2. Committed after v0.53.3's work began; see that commit.

v0.53.1 (2026-10-08)  — A CLOSED BOWL CLOSES WHAT IS INSIDE IT
  Ninth queue, item 2 (found while proving item 1).
  - Permissions were checked on a path's own entry and never on the bowls
    above it, so the v0.52.2 homes were closed only to `cd`. Proven on
    v0.53.0: intern, refused at `cd /home/saucier` (rwx---), read
    /home/saucier/RECIPE.TXT with pour and with cat.elf (SYS_OPEN), and
    listed the bowl with ls.elf (SYS_READDIR, which checked nothing).
  - users_may now also requires search (x) on every bowl above the path,
    so the shell and the syscalls agree; SYS_READDIR now needs read on the
    bowl. Both syscall checks are ordinary-build only, as the open check
    always was: the CHALLENGE build keeps its unchecked ring-3 file access.
  - users-test.sh: pour, cat.elf and ls.elf are all refused on the closed
    home, with log lines; the owner still reads the file and a cook still
    lists their own home.

v0.53.0 (2026-10-08)  — FIRING HANDS A COOK'S FILES TO THE HEADCHEF
  Ninth queue, item 1.
  - hire takes the smallest free uid and fire frees it, and files record
    their owner as a uid. Proven on v0.52.3: saucier wrote a file and was
    fired, intern was hired with the same uid 2, and owned saucier's file
    and walked into saucier's closed home.
  - uids cannot simply stop being reused: the owner is stored on disk as
    200 + uid in one byte, so only 0-55 exist. Instead `fire` walks the
    disk and hands everything the cook owned to the headchef, and refuses
    while the cook still has a shell or a live process, which carry the
    uid as surely as a file. A cook later hired under the same name does
    not inherit the old home (it is the headchef's) and starts at /.
  - users-test.sh: fire refuses a cook with a process running; firing
    hands over the home and the file; the next hire gets the uid but not
    the files; a rehired name has no home; fsck is clean.
  - Found on the way, queued as item 2: a closed bowl does not close what
    is inside it. intern, refused at `cd /home/saucier`, could still `pour`
    the file by its full path.

  Eighth queue, item 4.
  - Processes have had owners since v0.51.0; now they show. ps and

    and orders shows whose each job is. A bare `cd` now goes home rather

  - The boot segment's ps check parses the new column and asserts the
    owner of lazy.elf; owner-test asserts orders lists the headchef's and

v0.52.2 (2026-10-08)  — HOME BOWLS
  Eighth queue, item 3.
  - `hire` made a cook who could write almost nowhere. Every ordinary cook
    now has /home/<cook>, theirs and closed to other cooks (rwx---), made
    at hire, or at the first login of a cook hired before homes existed;
    /home itself is the headchef's, open for reading and entering. Every
    shell of theirs starts there, console login and SSH session alike.
    The headchef keeps the root, as root traditionally does.
  - Starting in a home would have stranded a cook away from the programs,
    which live in the root: `cook` now also looks in the root for a
    program it cannot find relative to the cwd, as a PATH would.
  - users-test.sh: homes are made at hire and at first login; each cook's
    shell starts at home; saucier cannot cd into cook's; a program runs
    from a home; mtools sees /home and fsck is clean. The SSH pair test

    "failed" on a test that expected hello.elf to exit 0; it exits 42 by

v0.52.1 (2026-10-07)  — secret
  Eighth queue, item 2 (committed with item 1: both live in shell.c and
  were checked together).
  - `secret` changes your own secret: the current one, then the new one
    twice. The current one is asked for ALWAYS, the headchef's own
    included - otherwise an unattended session hands over the account.
    `secret <cook>` lets the headchef set anyone's without it. Stored in the
    build's kind, so PBKDF2 with a fresh salt in the ordinary build.
  - users-test.sh: a wrong current secret is refused; a cook changes their
    own and the headchef sets it again; the old secret stops working and
    each new one logs in; the stored line is PBKDF2.
  - A note on checking: a full check that starts while scripts are being
    edited runs the NEW scripts against the binary it built at its start.
    One did, and "failed" on checks for a command not yet built.

v0.52.0 (2026-10-07)  — who
  Eighth queue, item 1.
  - There can be five shells at once (the console and four SSH sessions)
    and nothing listed them. `who` does: each shell's cook, where it is
    (the console, or the client's address, which the vault now passes to
    the session it starts), how long it has been up and how long since its
    last keystroke; the asker's own line is marked, exec sessions too.

    shell_t carries its start and last-input times.
  - The two-cook SSH test asks `who` from saucier's session and expects the

v0.51.0 (2026-10-07)  — ONLY YOUR OWN PROCESSES
  Seventh queue, item 3.
  - kill, plate (fg), steep (bg) and `kill %` acted on any process, and

    new test: the ordinary cook killed the headchef's program, and killed
    fbcon - the task that draws the console.
  - A process now records its owner, the uid of the shell that cooked it
    (users.c resolves that per task, so an SSH session's programs are its

v0.50.0 (2026-10-07)  — GUESSING A PASSWORD OVER THE NETWORK COSTS TIME
  Seventh queue, item 2.
  - Measured first, with the real client from one address: 124 wrong
    guesses a minute, bounded only by the key exchange and PBKDF2 -
    about 178,000 a day.
  - Each wrong password now pauses its connection before the answer (1 s,
    doubling, capped at 8), and ten failures from one address inside five
    minutes shut that address out at the door - a DISCONNECT saying why -
    until it has been quiet that long. A successful login clears the count.
    Key questions and the client's "none" probe do not count.
  - Measured after: with the pauses alone, 41 a minute. With the lockout,
    10 guesses and then 210 refused connections in the rest of the minute:
    about two a minute sustained, from 124.
  - Two bugs on the way, both in the change itself and both found by its
    own test: the per-connection slot wipe zeroed the peer address the
    listener had just stored, so every failure was recorded against
    address 0 and the lockout never fired; and the refusal's log line ran
    after a graceful close that waits on the peer, so a test that stops
    quickly never saw it. The test's first failure was its own: it read
    "Permission denied, please try again" as the end of a connection.
  - scripts/guess-test.sh boots its own soupOS (it locks the host out,
    which would break any test after it): three wrong guesses take at
    least 7 s, and after ten the right password is refused at the door.

v0.49.0 (2026-10-07)  — A KEY LOGS IN AS ITS OWN COOK
  Seventh queue, item 1.
  - /AUTHKEYS lines were bare public keys, and any listed key opened ANY
    account. Proven before the fix by running the new test against the
    previous kernel: the headchef's key logged straight into saucier's
    account, and a key written with a cook's name in front did not work
    at all.
  - A line is now "cook ssh-ed25519 <base64> [comment]" and the key opens
    that cook's account only. A bare line (the old form, and what a .pub
    file is) still works, as the headchef's key and nobody else's: the
    safe reading of a file written before keys had names.
  - ssh-test.sh installs a bare key and a saucier-tagged one: saucier's
    opens saucier's account and not the headchef's, and the bare key does
    not open saucier's. Gate 99.

v0.48.0 (2026-10-07)  — PROGRAMS REMOVE FILES AND MAKE BOWLS, BY THE SHELL'S RULES
  Sixth queue, item 4, and a permission gap it uncovered.
  - SYS_UNLINK (10) and SYS_MKDIR (11), with rm.elf and mkdir.elf on the
    disk. Paths are copied out of user memory a byte at a time by one
    helper, now also used by readdir.
  - ONE RULE. The shell's may() moved to users.c as users_may(), and the
    syscalls ask exactly what the shell's own commands ask: unlink needs
    write permission on the file (as `strain` does), mkdir write
    permission on the parent (as `mkbowl` does). A bowl cannot be
    unlinked.
  - WHILE THERE: a program's open() checked nothing. Any cook's program
    could read or overwrite a file only the headchef may touch. In the
    ordinary build it now applies the same rule (read needs 'r'; write
    needs 'w' on the file, or on the parent to create it). In the
    CHALLENGE build an unchecked open is part of what the challenge
    teaches, so there it stays as it was. And in every build, open's path
    was only checked at its first byte before the kernel read the rest;
    it now goes through the same copier.
  - scripts/fsops-test.sh, in check.sh, judged by mtools and fsck: nested
    bowls made, a file removed and gone, a missing file and a bowl
    refused; as the ordinary cook, the headchef's file can be neither
    removed nor read, no bowl can be made in the root, but the cook's own
    file in a bowl opened to all cooks can be removed and a permitted file
    read; fsck clean. Its first two runs failed on the TEST: an apostrophe
    the key-typer cannot type silently ended the sequence (its error was
    discarded; the test now fails loudly when typing fails), and the
    cook's file was set up where the cook may not write. Gate 99.

v0.47.1 (2026-10-07)  — FILES ARE DATED
  Sixth queue, item 3.
  - Every file and bowl soupOS made was dated 1980-00-00 0:00: the write
    time and date fields were left zero. They are now stamped from the
    RTC on every create and overwrite (write time, write date, access
    date), and the creation date is set once and kept across overwrites.
    The creation TIME stays as it is: its low byte holds the mode.
  - Checked against the host: mdir shows a file soupOS just wrote dated to
    the minute of the host's UTC clock (QEMU's RTC runs in UTC), and fsck
    stays clean. fsck-test now asserts KEEP.TXT is dated within three
    minutes of the host's UTC time. Gate 99.

v0.47.0 (2026-10-07)  — THE MACHINE COMES UP READY
  Sixth queue, item 2, half of which was already true.
  - The queue said a fresh boot has no address until someone types
    `plumbing dhcp`. Checked first: the kernel has leased one at boot all
    along (kernel.c, with the static defaults as the fallback); every test
    that typed `plumbing dhcp` was asking for a second lease. Nothing to
    build there.
  - /etc/rc: shell commands, one a line ('#' for comments), run as
    headchef at boot before the login prompt, each logged as "[rc] ...".
    soupyc was the queue's suggestion, but it cannot run shell commands,
    and opening the vault is a shell command.

    is booted and NOT ONE KEY is sent to its console; the kernel leases an

v0.46.0 (2026-10-07)  — PASSWORDS THAT SURVIVE THE NETWORK
  Sixth queue, item 1.
  - users.c kept each secret as an unsalted 32-bit AlphaSOUP hash, and any
    preimage logged in. The CHALLENGE build keeps exactly that, because it
    is the whole of challenge stage 2. The ordinary build now sits behind
    `vault`, where a password crosses the network, so it stores salted
    PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256: a 16-byte salt from the entropy pool, 20,000
    iterations kept with the entry, compared in constant time, in a line
    format ("name:uid:$p$iters$salt$key") the old one can be told from.
  - PBKDF2 is in sha256.c with the HMAC states computed once, so an
    iteration is two compressions; `sample` checks it against
    hashlib.pbkdf2_hmac at 1 and 4,096 iterations. A login costs 3-4 ticks.
  - OLD ENTRIES still log in, and the first good login rewrites that entry
    (only that one) as PBKDF2 - the one moment the secret is known.
  - scripts/users-test.sh, in check.sh, judges by what mtools reads back
    from /etc/kitchen: a fresh disk holds only PBKDF2 entries with distinct
    salts, a hired cook gets one too, wrong secrets are refused in both
    formats, an old-format kitchen (built on the host with a port of
    AlphaSOUP-32 that is checked against soupOS's own `hash`) logs in and
    upgrades just the cook who logged in, and the next boot logs in again.
  - It found the FAT bug in the commit before this one on its first run.
    Gate 99.

v0.45.1 (2026-10-07)  — OVERWRITING A LONG-NAMED FILE ORPHANED ITS LONG NAME
  - fat_write made a new 8.3 alias before checking whether the file
    existed, and the existing file's own alias made "~1" look taken. So an
    overwrite renamed the short entry to KITCHE~2 while the long-name
    entries kept ~1's checksum: mtools could no longer find /etc/kitchen
    by name and fsck reported "Wrong checksum for long file name". soupOS
    still found it (by the alias), which is why nothing noticed: users-test
    found it by reading /etc/kitchen back with mtools after `hire`.
  - An existing file now keeps its own short name. fsck-test was supposed
    to cover this path and did not: its `hire` never ran, because the two
    secret prompts were not answered and swallowed the next commands. It
    now answers them, and runs LONGWR.SC twice so long-named files are
    overwritten too.

v0.45.0 (2026-10-07)  — MEMORY PER PROCESS, WHERE YOU CAN SEE IT
  Fifth queue, item 6.
  - `ps` and `kitchen` show each program's memory in pages: RES, resident
    now (the cap is 1024), and SWAP, on disk now - a live count the swap
    code keeps up and down, reset at exec and at exit. Kernel tasks show a
    dash: they live in the kernel's memory.
  - Checked against numbers known in advance: `lazy.elf hold` touches 1500
    heap pages and waits, so ps must show 1024 resident and 1500 plus its
    image and stack pages less 1024 on disk. It shows 1024 and 481 (so 5
    pages of image and stack), and every held page reads back right. The
    boot segment asserts both. Gate 99.

v0.44.0 (2026-10-07)  — PROGRAMS CAN READ DIRECTORIES
  Fifth queue, item 5.
  - SYS_READDIR (9): a path, an index and a udirent_t (name, size, is_dir);
    1 for an entry, 0 past the end, -1 for an error. The path is copied out
    of user memory a byte at a time, each byte checked, and the listing is
    made into a per-call allocation rather than a static, because two
    programs may list at once. The name is the long one when there is one.
  - ls.elf lists a directory from ring 3. scripts/ls-test.sh (in check.sh)
    runs it on the root, then has mtools list the same image: all 44
    entries agree on name, size and type, long names included, and a
    missing directory is an error rather than an empty listing. The boot
    segment checks it ran and saw HELLO.TXT at 47 bytes. Gate 97.

(2026-10-07, no version)  — QUEUE ITEM 5.4 CLOSED BY MEASUREMENT
  Interrupt-driven DMA completion would save ~4 status polls a request
  (20-60 us waits); 0.07% of fatstress, and the swap path's 24% is spent
  with interrupts off in the fault handler. Numbers in the queue.

v0.43.0 (2026-10-07)  — SWAP KEEPS THE WORKING SET (APPROXIMATE LRU)
  Fifth queue, item 3. A story of two wrong turns, both caught by measuring.
  - GLOBAL PRESSURE has an arithmetic answer: eight processes at the
    1,024-page cap is 32 MB against a PMM of about 120 MB, so user
    processes cannot empty it. No mechanism built; recorded here.
  - THE VICTIM. hotcold.elf keeps a hot set used every round and sweeps
    new pages it never revisits. Under FIFO the hot set is always the
    oldest mapped, so it is evicted every round and fetched straight back.
  - Clock (second chance on the accessed bit) was tried first and changed
    nothing - 3,584 page-ins either way - though it gave 8,152 second
    chances, so the bits worked. It is the known weakness: the hot set is
    used in one phase and the sweep runs in another, the hand laps the
    resident set within a sweep, and a page cleared on one lap is evicted
    on the next. Clock approximates LRU only within one revolution.
  - Approximate LRU then: every 32 evictions each resident page's accessed
    bit is read and cleared and a used page is stamped with the epoch; the
    victim is the page with the oldest stamp. It first measured WORSE with
    a sample before every eviction. A model of the exact algorithm on the
    host matched the kernel to the page once given the heap's real cap
    (1,017, not 1,024: the image and stack count too), which showed the
    first workload was the bug - hot plus one sweep was 1,024 pages, a
    hair over memory, and a loop just larger than memory is LRU's worst
    case. Resized to 400 + 400, the model predicted and the kernel
    measured: FIFO 1,200 page-ins in 45 ticks, approximate LRU 400 in 31
    (clock, in the model, 800). Both sampling rates give the same result;
    32 is kept as the cheaper.
  - The boot segment runs hotcold and requires fewer than 600 page-ins:
    FIFO's 1,200 would fail it. lazy.elf's sweep is unchanged (a sweep has
    no working set to keep). Gate 95.

v0.42.0 (2026-10-07)  — FOUR SSH SESSIONS AT ONCE
  Fifth queue, item 2.
  - Sessions have been separate shells since v0.36.0, but the vault served
    one connection, because its state was one static struct. Now a table
    of four slots, each served by its own worker task, and the protocol
    code's `S` is whichever slot the calling task serves, so 180 uses of
    it read exactly as before. Everything that was quietly shared moved
    into the slot: the output ring, the key-exchange hash buffer, the
    /AUTHKEYS buffer and userauth's signed-data buffer - any of which two
    concurrent connections would have trampled. The session's own tasks
    reach their slot through the terminal's write context, never `S`.
  - The listener accepts, finds a free slot and spawns its worker; a fifth
    client gets the busy DISCONNECT. That needed TCP headroom: with four
    sessions holding all four TCP slots, the fifth SYN had nowhere to land
    and timed out before the vault could refuse it. The table is 8 now.
  - TESTED: four sessions held while a fifth is refused; and two at once
    as two cooks (the console hires a saucier first): each sees its own
    whoami and cwd, and Ctrl-C in one kills its program while the other's
    keeps printing. The pair test's first two failures were the test (a
    colour code in a prompt pattern; a relative program path from a
    directory), found by stepping it with output. Gate 93.

v0.41.0 (2026-10-07)  — FSCK: THE PERMISSION BYTE WAS FAT'S CASE FLAGS
  Fifth queue, item 1. Nothing had ever checked the FAT as a whole.
  - fsck.fat -n on images soupOS had written found, every time, a "bad
    short file name" on every entry soupOS had created, which it would
    rename to FSCK0000.000, then a "file of size 0 with a cluster chain"
    and a lost cluster that both turned out to be consequences of the
    rename. Clearing one byte on those entries in a copy made the volume
    spotless, so the cause was proven before anything changed: soupOS kept
    each file's mode in byte 12, which FAT reserves for case flags (bit 0
    "no valid 8.3 name", bits 3-4 "lowercase"). A Linux machine mounting
    the disk would have shown those names in lowercase.
  - The owner and mode now live in the creation-time bytes: byte 13 holds
    200 + uid (valid hundredths stop at 199, so no other tool writes it,
    which also makes it the "soupOS metadata" mark) and byte 14 the mode;
    byte 12 is always 0. Tested on a copy before any code changed: fsck
    accepts that encoding on every entry, and mtools still reads the disk.
    The old layout is still READ: a hand-made old entry reads back as
    rw----, a permission change rewrites it in the new layout, and fsck is
    then clean. users.h's limit is asserted against the byte at compile
    time.
  - A SECOND FINDING ON THE WAY: the "pristine" disk.img was not pristine.
    console-test.sh booted disk.img itself rather than a copy, so every
    check run logged in on the canonical image and left /etc/kitchen in it.
    It boots a copy now, and check.sh fingerprints disk.img and fails the
    run if anything changes it.
  - scripts/fsck-test.sh (in check.sh): fsck must be clean before, then a
    workload through every write path (login, hire, three scripts that
    write, read and delete long- and short-named files, a bowl made,
    filled, emptied and removed, two permission changes, fatstress), then
    fsck must be clean after and mtools must read back a file soupOS wrote.

v0.40.0 (2026-10-07)  — BYTES IN AND OUT, PER CONNECTION
  Fourth queue, item 7.
  - Every TCP connection counts the data it carries each way (reset when a
    slot is taken). kitchen's net section shows them beside each
    connection, so a stuck transfer is visible as a number that stops
    moving, and the close line says them: "[tcp] closed cleanly (sent 7,
    received 7)". The net segment asserts exactly that for holler, which
    sends "souptcp" and gets it back upper-cased. Gate 93.
  - That closes the fourth queue but for the deferred Doom item: swap,
    a shell of its own per SSH session (in three stages), the stack guard,
    rekeying, the IRQ-log question (closed by measurement), DMA (58x), the
    TCP waits (closed by measurement), and these counters. On the way it
    also found and fixed hatch leaking kernel memory and five fat_read
    callers overrunning their buffers.

v0.39.2 (2026-10-07)  — TCP WAITS HALT; THE CPU THEY WERE BLAMED FOR IS NOT THEIRS
  Fourth queue, item 6, closed with the premise measured false.
  - The item said TCP's yield loops burn the cpu. Idle, with an SSH session
    connected: vault 0%, session 0%, machine 100% idle. Under load (the
    host fetching a 1 MB file from hatch in a loop, about 7 MB/s, which
    first needed v0.39.1's fix to work at all): hatch 95%, idle 5%.
  - The loops were changed to halt between checks, like every other wait
    loop since v0.31.0 (net_wait: yield, then halt if interrupts are on).
    Result: hatch 97%, idle 3%. No change. A TSC profile said why: 320
    waits across 46 fetches, because SLIRP's ACK has always arrived by the
    time the loop looks. The cost is the emulated NIC - 47,338 frames at
    about 42,000 cycles each to transmit, plus the ACKs' receive
    interrupts - which is real work charged to whoever is running.
  - Kept, as hygiene and for the rule, with the measurement in the comment
    so nobody mistakes it for a speed-up. hatch now logs the machine's idle
    figure when it closes. Gate 92.

v0.39.1 (2026-10-07)  — HATCH LEAKED KERNEL MEMORY; FIVE fat_read CALLERS OVERRAN
  Found while setting up a measurement for queue item 6, not by looking.
  - To load the network, the host fetched a 1 MB file from `hatch`. It got
    0 bytes in 22 ms while hatch logged "200 (1048576 bytes)". Hatch read
    files into a 32 KB static buffer, fat_read reported the FILE's size
    (not the bytes it stored), and hatch sent that many bytes through a
    uint16_t length. 1,048,576 truncates to exactly 0.
  - THE WORSE CASE WAS PROVEN BEFORE IT WAS FIXED: a 40,000-byte file of
    the letter S came back with 7,232 bytes that were not S, starting at
    offset 32,768 with "/MID.BIN" - hatch's own path buffer, then whatever
    else follows it in kernel memory. Anyone who could reach hatch could
    read kernel memory by asking for a file between 32 and 64 KB. Hatch
    now streams the file through the VFS in 32 KB pieces; hatch-test.sh
    fetches a 40,000- and a 200,000-byte random file and compares both
    byte for byte.
  - The root is fat_read's contract, so every caller was audited. Five
    used the reported size as a byte count: `soup` and `fortune` wrote a
    NUL that far past their buffers, ssh's /AUTHKEYS reader did the same,
    users.c parsed past its buffer, and the ELF loader bounds-checked
    program headers against the file size, so a program over 256 KB would
    pass checks against bytes never read. Each now clamps or refuses
    (`soup` refuses a script over 8 KB rather than run half of it; the
    loader refuses a program over its limit), fat.h says so in capitals,
    and a 10 KB script on the disk checks `soup` in the gate. Gate 92.

v0.39.0 (2026-10-07)  — BUS-MASTER DMA: SWAP UNDER KVM 58x FASTER
  Fourth queue, item 5b, which turned out not to be what it said.
  - The queue blamed polling: a vCPU hammering the status port while the
    host does the I/O. Two hypotheses were tested in a copy of the tree
    before anything was built. Dropping the cache flush after each write:
