    4786 ticks to 4682, 2%. Halting until the drive's IRQ 14 instead of
    spinning, with interrupts turned on in the fault path: 4801, nothing.
  - THEN IT WAS MEASURED. TSC timestamps around each phase of every
    multi-sector command during the greedy swap run, under KVM: issue
    0.9 G cycles, BSY wait 16 G, DRQ wait 0.7 G, flush 3.4 G, and the data
    transfer itself 120.7 G - 85% of the run. 23 million words at about
    5,200 cycles each: KVM takes one VM exit per 16-bit word on this port,
    `rep insw` or not. Under TCG the same transfer is 3.8 G.
  - So the fix is not to wait better but to stop moving data with the
    CPU. The PIIX IDE function (8086:7010, class 01:01) has bus-master
    registers at BAR4; the driver now finds it, enables bus mastering,
    and moves every read and write by DMA through a 4 KB, 4 KB-aligned
    bounce buffer with a one-entry PRD table (kernel memory is identity-
    mapped, so its address is its physical address). Completion is the
    engine stopping and the drive not busy: a dozen port accesses per
    request instead of 2,048 per page. PIO remains the fallback.
  - The greedy run under KVM: 4872 ticks to 84, with all 7173 page-outs
    and 4096 page-ins verified by the program. Every FAT test, mtools'
    reading of what soupOS wrote, and the self-test's write/read/delete
    balance all pass on the DMA path. Gate 91.
  - Not done, and now a small matter: interrupt-driven completion. The
    remaining poll is per request, not per word.

v0.38.0 (2026-10-07)  — SSH REKEYING
  Fourth queue, item 4. RFC 4253 says rekey after a gigabyte or an hour;
  the vault never did, and would have carried the first keys forever.
  - A KEXINIT arriving mid-session is now a rekey: the vault answers with
    its own (unless it sent one first), runs the curve25519 exchange again
    under the current keys, and switches at NEWKEYS. The session id stays
    the first exchange's H for the life of the connection, as the RFC
    requires; until now do_kex overwrote it every time, which nothing
    noticed only because it ran once. Sequence numbers carry on.
  - The vault starts its own after an hour, or after N packets out with
    `vault rekey N` (for testing). From its KEXINIT to its NEWKEYS no
    channel data goes out; the session's output waits in the ring.
  - TESTED BOTH WAYS with the real client: `ssh -o RekeyLimit=2K` makes
    OpenSSH rekey every two kilobytes and a session runs through several;
    and every SSH test now runs with the vault rekeying itself every 8
    packets out, all 22 checks green. The first run of that check failed
    and the instrumentation said why: output goes in kilobyte CHANNEL_DATA
    packets, so a whole login is about a dozen packets and a threshold of
    60 was never reached. Not a bug; the test's number was wrong.
  - Also fixed: `vault rekey 60` set 0, because parse_num does not skip
    the space before the number. Gate 91.

v0.37.0 (2026-10-07)  — THE STACK GROWS, AND HAS A GUARD
  Fourth queue, item 3.
  - The user stack was 16 KB mapped at exec, and running past it walked
    silently into whatever was below. It is now a 1 MB region: the top
    four pages are mapped at exec as before, the rest come on demand
    through the same fault path as the heap (a not-present fault inside
    the region maps a page and retries, evicting a heap page to swap
    first if the process is at its cap), and the lowest page is a guard
    that is never mapped. A fault there is logged as "[user] <prog>:
    stack overflow at 0x..." and the program is killed; the shell goes on.
    The heap's break may now rise only to the guard.
  - deep.elf proves both: 600 levels of recursion with a written
    kilobyte each, every frame checked on the way back up (600 KB, nearly
    forty times the old stack), and `deep.elf forever`, which hits the
    guard. The exit line reports "[stack] grew its stack by N pages".
  - Found on the way: a process's demand-page count was never reset, so
    a reused process slot carried the last program's count into its exit
    line. Both counters are now reset at exec. Gate 91.

v0.36.0 (2026-10-07)  — A SHELL OF ITS OWN FOR EVERY SSH SESSION
  Fourth queue, item 2, stage (c), the last. Until now an SSH login drove
  THE local shell: it shared the keyboard and the screen, and whoever
  authenticated was not whoever the shell thought was logged in.
  - TERMINALS FOLLOW TASKS. task_t gained a terminal and a shell, and every
    task inherits both from the one that spawned it. term_current() is the
    calling task's terminal, so a program's stdout and stderr, a script's
    `pour` and `input`, the self-test and the "[program killed]" message
    all reach the session that caused them with no plumbing of their own.
    Services drop both when they declare themselves.
  - A BYTE-STREAM TERMINAL (term_stream_t): VT100 output with the VGA
    palette mapped to SGR (light grey on black is the user's own default),
    a cursor it tracks itself so the line editor's absolute moves become
    relative ones, its own hard wrap so tracker and far end never disagree,
    and input through the console's translator, now a reusable object
    (keyxlate_t) rather than file-scope state. Its write makes the WRITER
    wait when the vault's 16 KB ring is full, instead of dropping output.
  - PER SHELL: the user (users.c and fat.c ask a resolver, so `whoami`,
    permissions and new files' owners are the session's), the cwd and
    history (shell_t, v0.35.1), the foreground program (a property of the
    terminal now, so Ctrl-C over SSH kills the session's program and
    stdin belongs to it), and the jobs `kill %`, `plate` and the prompt's
    reports see (only those cooked on the same terminal).
  - THE VAULT starts a session shell per channel. `shell` is interactive;
    `exec` runs the one line with no prompt and no echo and returns a real
    exit status (the program's code, 126 not runnable, 127 unknown, 137
    killed). `clockout` ends a session. Hanging up kills the session's
    programs, as SIGHUP would, and waits for the session to end before its
    terminal is reused. Screen-only commands (jot, doom, vgademo, wipe,
    bounce, skewer, bgclock) refuse politely over the network.
  - It worked on the first boot. ssh-test.sh now also checks: exec exits
    127 for an unknown command; a session has its own cwd and clockout
    ends it; NONE of a session's output reaches the console's serial log;
    Ctrl-C over the wire kills the session's program and the shell carries
    on; hanging up with `hog.elf &` running kills it. 19 checks.
  - `pass` still shares the console, deliberately: it is the clear-text
    door, and a shell of its own there would be a login prompt in clear
    text to a stranger. Gate 88.

v0.35.2 (2026-10-07)  — term_t: THE SHELL TALKS TO A TERMINAL
  Fourth queue, item 2, stage (b) of three. No behaviour change.
  - The shell made 690 direct calls to vga_* and keyboard_*: 225 puts,
    198 set_color, 164 printf, 49 putchar and the rest. Every one now goes
    through the term_t in its shell_t (t_puts, t_printf, t_color, t_getc
    and so on), and the local console is the first terminal, term_vga,
    which forwards to vga_* and keyboard_* exactly as before. Left direct
    on purpose: vga_fill_row and vga_set_mirror, which are about the
    screen itself, not about a terminal.
  - ONE FORMATTER. vga_printf's formatter moved to str.c as kvformat,
    which takes an emit callback; vga_printf and term_printf both use it,
    so a terminal that is not the console gets the same bytes, quirks and
    all (a negative %d prints its '-' before the padding; %p is always
    eight digits). A trailing '%' no longer walks past the end of the
    format string. term_printf formats into a 512-byte buffer on the stack
    and hands the terminal one string, which keeps a line together the way
    vga_printf's preempt_disable did.
  - PROVEN BYTE-IDENTICAL, not just gate-green: the previous commit and
    this one were each booted and typed twenty output-heavy commands
    (menu, pantry's %-10s table, roster, two soupyc programs, an unknown
    command, ...). 225 lines each; the three that differ are the kernel
    being 8 KB larger (twice, in free-memory figures) and `fortune`.
  - Still not per-terminal, and stage (c) has to deal with them: output
    that other modules print on the shell's behalf (soupyc, the self-test,
    ring-3 programs' stdout) goes to vga_* directly. Gate 88.

v0.35.1 (2026-10-07)  — shell_t: WHAT BELONGS TO ONE SHELL
  Fourth queue, item 2, stage (a) of three. No behaviour change.
  - shell.c's per-shell state was seven file-scope variables: cwd, the
    history ring and its two indices, prompt_row, prompt_len and the
    pager's rows-per-page. They are one `shell_t` now, reached through
    cur_shell(), which today returns the single instance. Every use was
    rewritten mechanically (forty of them); the gate is the proof the
    rewrite changed nothing.
  - What stayed global on purpose: the 64 KB copy buffer (scratch for one
    command; two shells copying at once is a later problem), the `rand`
    seed, and the preempttest/vgastress flags (one test at a time).
  - What is NOT per-shell yet and must be for stage (c): the logged-in
    user (users.c keeps one), the foreground job (proc.c keeps one), and
    every vga_*/keyboard_* call the shell makes, which stage (b) routes
    through a terminal. Gate 88.

v0.35.0 (2026-10-07)  — SWAP: MEMORY THE MACHINE DOES NOT HAVE, KEPT ON DISK
  Fourth queue, item 1. v0.33.0 killed a process at the page cap; now it
  keeps going.
  - THE SWAP AREA IS THE DISK PAST THE VOLUME. The Makefile makes a 48 MB
    image and formats the first 32 MB; the 16 MB after it, 4096 pages, is
    addressed by slot (eight sectors each) with no on-disk format at all,
    found at boot from fat_volume_sectors() and ata_total_sectors(). A
    disk with nothing past the volume has no swap and behaves as before.
    (An existing disk.img is the old 32 MB one: `rm disk.img && make
    disk`.)
  - Per process: a FIFO of its resident heap pages, oldest mapped first
    as the victim, and a table from heap page to slot. At the cap, the
    fault handler writes the oldest page straight from its user address
    with one eight-sector command, unmaps it, frees the frame, and maps
    the new one; a fault on a page with a slot maps a frame and reads it
    back, evicting first if it must. Both happen in the faulting task's
    context with only the ATA mutex taken, which nothing holds while
    touching user memory, so the ring-0 case (a syscall copying into a
    swapped page) is safe too.
  - PROVEN BY lazy.elf: `greedy` touches all 4096 pages of its 16 MB and
    completes, with every page holding its value: 7173 pages out, 4096
    back in (the first pass evicts 3072, the verify pass brings each one
    back and evicts another). `absurd` claims 32 MB, more than the cap
    and the swap together, and is killed when the swap is full with
    "no page for 0x... swap 4096 of 4096". Every exit returns to the same
    free-page count. Five new checks in the boot segment, and kitchen
    shows swap in use. The exit line now also carries elapsed ticks.
  - MEASURED: under the gate's emulated boot the greedy run takes 296
    ticks, about 11,000 page transfers in three seconds. Under KVM the
    same run takes 5601 ticks, 56 seconds, nineteen times slower, because
    every port access the driver polls on is a VM exit; that is a finding
    for the queue (interrupt-driven ATA completion), not a swap matter.
  - Global pressure, the PMM itself empty, still fails the map; only the
    per-process cap evicts. The queue says which step that is. Gate 88.

v0.34.1 (2026-10-07)  — ATA: STRING PIO
  Groundwork for swap, measured on the way.
  - The driver moved each sector one `inw` at a time: 256 port accesses,
    and under a hypervisor every one is a VM exit. Now `rep insw` and
    `rep outsw`, one instruction per sector, which KVM services as one
    exit.
  - Measured with a new once-per-mount yardstick, the free-cluster count
    that reads the whole FAT (64 sectors): 6-7 ticks before, 3 after. A
    doubling, not the order of magnitude the exit count suggested, so the
    data port was never the bulk of a sector's cost. What remains is per
    command: the 400 ns settle, the BSY/DRQ polling, and the host's own
    latency to service the request. That is why swap, which moves eight
    sectors at a time, will use multi-sector commands: one wait per page
    rather than eight.
  - The fat segment and fat-longname-test are the proof nothing changed
    but the speed. Gate 85.

v0.34.0 (2026-10-07)  — SSH: KEYS, ONE-SHOT COMMANDS, AND A POLITE "BUSY"
  Queue item S8, the loose ends noticed in the first real logins.
  - PUBLIC-KEY LOGIN. `publickey` userauth with ssh-ed25519: the client's
    key blob is looked up in /AUTHKEYS on the disk (one "ssh-ed25519
    <base64> comment" line each, the shape of a .pub file, so
    `mcopy -i disk.img ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub ::AUTHKEYS` installs one),
    the signature over the session id and the request is checked with
    ed25519_verify, and the question form (no signature) gets PK_OK as
    RFC 4252 wants. No password crosses the wire. The server now offers
    "publickey,password".
  - EXEC. `ssh soupOS whoami` types the line into the same shell, streams
    what comes back, and closes when the shell's prompt returns, which it
    spots in the output stream ("@soupOS:" then "> "). The shell's echo and
    its prompt come along, because it is the shell. Exit status is 0: the
    shell does not report one.
  - BUSY. A second client while a session is open used to hang until its
    banner timeout. The vault now polls for newcomers at its wait point,
    and sends each a version line and a plain DISCONNECT with reason 12,
    "too many connections", and the words: the client prints "Received
    disconnect from ...: 12: the vault is busy with another session, try
    again later". A line before the version string was tried first and is
    only shown by `ssh -v`; a DISCONNECT is what the client shows.
  - The per-byte "[tcp] sent 1 bytes" is gone from the services; only the
    outbound connection logs its bytes, which is the one the net segment
    asserts on.
  - Rekeying is still not implemented; a session that moves gigabytes
    would need it. scripts/ssh-test.sh covers exec, key login (a key
    made by ssh-keygen on the host and authorised before the first boot)
    and the busy refusal. Gate 85.

v0.33.0 (2026-10-07)  — MEMORY THE MACHINE DOES NOT HAVE, YET
  Queue item 8, the honest small version: demand paging for the heap.
  - sbrk no longer maps anything. It moves the break, checks it will not
    run into the stack, and returns. A page gets a frame the first time
    something touches it, in usermode_demand_page: a page fault whose
    address lies in the running process's heap below its break, and whose
    error says "not present" rather than "protected", is answered with a
    zeroed page and the faulting instruction is retried. Any other fault
    is the fault it always was: a ring-3 one kills the program, a ring-0
    one is still a panic.
  - THE HOOK SITS AHEAD OF BOTH. The kernel touches user memory too, when
    a syscall copies into a buffer the program has only sbrk'd, and that
    fault arrives from ring 0. lazy.elf's third mode is exactly that: a
    read() into a page it never wrote. Before the hook was moved in front
    of the panic path, that would have taken the machine down; it now
    reads 64 bytes of README.TXT into a page that did not exist a moment
    earlier.
  - The cap (1024 pages a process) applies at touch time, not at sbrk.
    So lazy.elf can claim 16 MB, four times the cap, and touch one page
    in sixty-four for free; the greedy run touches every page, meets the
    cap at 4 MB, and is killed with "[demand] no page for 0x...", which is
    the honest answer to a program wanting memory the machine does not
    have. The shell goes on, and the two hello.elf runs that follow end at
    the same free-page count, so the killed process's 1024 pages came
    back. crash.elf, the fault that was never a heap page, still kills.
  - Each process counts its faulted-in pages and the exit line reports
    them ("[demand] /lazy.elf faulted in 64 pages on first touch"). Six
    checks in the boot segment. Swapping to disk is the item after this
    one, as the queue said. Gate 85.

v0.32.0 (2026-10-07)  — soupyc: A FILE FROM A FILE
  Queue item 7. The language could create, read and delete files, but not
  do the one thing scripts are for.
  - `lines(path)` returns the file as an array of strings, one per line,
    newlines dropped (a trailing \r too, so a file from a PC reads the
    same). A missing file is an empty array; more lines than an array
    holds (48) is an error rather than a silent cut. `write_lines(path,
    array)` writes the array back with a newline after every element,
    replacing the file, and returns the count. Both sit on the heap-backed
    strings and the array pool that were already there.
  - LINES.SC on the disk reads SOUP.TXT (six lines: mixed lengths, an
    empty one, one longer than the old 47-character string), numbers,
    upper-cases and reverses them into SOUPUP.TXT, and reads it back.
    The gate's soupyc segment asserts all three steps.
  - THE BYTES ARE CHECKED ON THE HOST: scripts/lines-test.sh boots a copy
    of the disk, runs the script, has mtools pull both files out of the
    image, and compares SOUPUP.TXT with the same transform done in
    Python. 148 bytes, identical. soupOS reading its own file back only
    proves it is self-consistent; this proves it is right.
  - Worth knowing: `make clean` does not touch disk.img, so a change to
    the files the Makefile embeds needs `rm disk.img && make disk`.
    Gate 79.

v0.31.0 (2026-10-07)  — kitchen, HONEST CPU FIGURES, AND THE FLAKE EXPLAINED
  Queue item 5, which on the way closed items 1 and 6.
  - `kitchen`: the whole room redrawn in place once a second until a key.
    Uptime, address, console size; cpu idle/busy; tasks alive; memory in
    pages and heap; disk free; the TCP listeners and connections; then the
    task table. It draws with the serial mirror off (vga_set_mirror), so
    neither the log nor an SSH session is fed a frame a second, and logs
    one summary line on the way out with ITS OWN share in it.
  - That share is where the work was. The queue said to watch it, and the
    first version read 8%. Not the drawing: 2560 characters cost 0 ticks.
    It was fat_space, which read all 16K FAT entries from disk on every
    call, 60-70 ms of PIO. The free-cluster count is now kept by
    fat_set_entry, every cluster change goes through it, and the first
    fat_space after a mount counts once. `larder` is instant too. The
    `sample` write-then-delete balance check guards the cache. kitchen
    now reads idle 100%, self 0%.
  - CPU% WAS A LIE BEFORE: a tick that landed while the cpu was halted was
    charged to whichever task had halted, so the shell "used" 33-90% at a
    prompt. Every wait loop now halts through cpu_halt(), and the timer
    charges those ticks to idle. `ps` prints the idle figure; the shell
    reads 0-1% at a prompt.
  - Also found on the way: the hardware cursor was four port writes PER
    CHARACTER, each a VM exit under a hypervisor, even on a framebuffer
    boot where the VGA ports go nowhere. It is now written once per
    string, and not at all on a RAM-backed cell store.
  - THE FLAKY JOB-CONTROL CHECK (ITEM 1) IS THE KLOG RACE (ITEM 6). With
    the new parallel soak (six QEMUs at once, which is the load the
    gate's own parallel segments put on the machine) five streams of six
    failed within eight runs, and the saved logs show both shapes of the
    failure are one thing: the kernel's "[proc 5] /marker.elf continued",
    written to the serial port a byte at a time from the shell task, torn
    in two by the "[p:Z] step 15" line of the process it had just woken.
    Torn after "[proc", the grep sees a step "while parked"; torn after
    "cont", the word "continued" never matches. The process printed after
    it was resumed, exactly as it should. klog lines are now written
    whole under preempt_disable (interrupts stay on; an interrupt
    handler's own klog can still land inside a task's line, which is
    noted in the audit). 48 soak runs under the same load: 48 clean.
  - The gate's desk segment runs kitchen and asserts self <= 5% and
    idle >= 50%. Gate 76.

v0.30.2 (2026-10-07)  — THE GATE IN SEGMENTS: 410 s TO 98 s
  The second half of "make the gates go faster".
  - smoke-test.sh was one boot and one five-minute drive sequence. It is
    now eight segments under scripts/gate/ (boot, jobs, fat, vga, desk,
    net, soupyc, audio), each its own headless boot, run at once by
    gate.sh; smoke-test.sh hands over to it so nothing that called the
    gate changes. gate/lib.sh holds the boot, the typing, the grep and the
    verdict, so a segment is only its own sequence and its own checks.
  - THE CUT FOLLOWS ONE RULE: a check that counts lines across the log
    (two IRQ kills, three `killed`, the last two hello.elf exits, the
    marker interleave) lives in the segment whose sequence produces every
    line it counts. Every one of the old gate's 71 distinct assertions
    carried over, verified by diffing the description sets; the only
    difference is that "no kernel panic" is now asserted per segment.
  - First run: 98 s against 410 s, all green. The slowest segment is desk
    at 63 s, so the next cut, if it is ever worth it, is there.
  - check.sh's total is now bounded by doom-fb-test's two ISO builds
    (60 s) and the gate, not by five minutes of fixed waits.

v0.30.1 (2026-10-07)  — scripts/check.sh: THE PRE-COMMIT CHECK, IN PARALLEL
  Tooling, asked for: "is there a way to make the gates go faster".
  - The pieces were always independent - five configurations need a
    compiler each, and every test script boots its own QEMU from a copy
    of the disk - but they ran one after another because `make clean` for
    the next configuration deleted the soupOS.iso the gate was booting
    from. check.sh builds the other four configurations in rsync'd copies
    of the tree and runs the gate and all thirteen side scripts at once
    against the default build here.
  - First run, 16 cores: configs 8-9 s each, side scripts 16-60 s, the
    gate 410 s; 418 s in all, against the ten-plus minutes of the chain
    it replaces, and that chain did not even run most of the side
    scripts. The gate was 372 s on its own, so sharing the machine with
    seventeen other jobs cost it ten percent.
  - Which makes the next step obvious: the wall time IS the gate now.
    Its drive sequence is one boot and five minutes of fixed waits;
    splitting it into segments that boot in parallel is the remaining
    lever, and the one that needs care (counts and pids in the log
    assertions assume the single run).
  - `check.sh quick` is configs + gate only; KEEP=1 keeps the logs.

v0.30.0 (2026-10-07)  — SSH LOGIN: PASSWORD AND A SHELL
  Stage S7, the last of the SSH plan. `ssh headchef@soupOS` works.
  - Userauth takes `password` against the roster (users_check), six
    attempts, then the RFC 4254 connection layer: one session channel,
    pty-req/env/shell/window-change accepted, exec refused, windows
    tracked both ways (ours advertised at 64 KB and re-opened after 32 KB
    consumed; theirs honoured on every send).
  - THE CHANNEL IS THE CONSOLE, the same way the pass and the serial line
    are: bytes in go through console_rx_byte, bytes out come from the
    console sink. The sink runs on whatever task is printing, so it pushes
    into a 4 KB ring under preempt_disable and the SSH task drains the
    ring into CHANNEL_DATA packets between polls for incoming packets.
    The packet reader keeps a partial length header across polls so a
    2-tick poll can time out without corrupting the stream.
  - Verified with the real client driven by pexpect (scripts/ssh-login.py):
    password prompt, greeting, `whoami` and `larder` answered through the
    channel, and a wrong password refused. ssh-test.sh asserts both.
  - The pass (clear text) stays, off by default like everything else; the
    vault is the one to open on a network you do not own entirely. Gate 74.

v0.29.0 (2026-10-07)  — THE VAULT: SSH TRANSPORT AND KEY EXCHANGE
  Stage S6. A real OpenSSH client now talks to soupOS encrypted.
  - src/ssh.c: RFC 4253 version exchange and binary packets; KEXINIT
    offering one of everything (curve25519-sha256, ssh-ed25519,
    chacha20-poly1305@openssh.com); the ECDH reply signed with the host
    key; NEWKEYS; then SERVICE_ACCEPT and a userauth refusal that names
    `password`, so the client ends at "Permission denied (password)" over
    an encrypted channel. `vault <port>` opens it.
  - THE OPENSSH AEAD IS NOT THE RFC 8439 ONE. Two keys: the second half
    encrypts the four length bytes on their own so a receiver can read the
    length before authenticating; the first half does the payload from
    block one; the Poly1305 key is block zero; the nonce is the packet
    sequence number; the tag covers both encrypted parts. This is why
    chacha.h exposed the pieces separately in v0.25.0.
  - The host key is a 32-byte Ed25519 seed at /HOSTKEY.ED, made from the
    entropy pool on first start and read back after, so the fingerprint a
    client stores in known_hosts stays valid across reboots.
  - IT WORKED ON THE FIRST CONNECTION. ssh -vvv: curve25519-sha256,
    ssh-ed25519, chacha20-poly1305 negotiated; "SSH2_MSG_NEWKEYS received";
    "SSH2_MSG_SERVICE_ACCEPT received" (the first packet the client had to
    decrypt); "Permission denied (password)". The fingerprint it printed
    matched the kernel's. Four primitives with vectors each, and the
    composition held without a single wire-level fault; that is what the
    vectors were for. scripts/ssh-test.sh asserts all of it plus the
    fingerprint surviving a reboot of the same disk.
  - One session at a time in static buffers, packets capped at 4 KB. Not
    hardened against an adversary; for a private network, as the header
    says.
  - FOUND WHILE OPENING `pass` AND `vault` TOGETHER: TCP had one listener
    slot, and each service re-armed it to its own port every second, so
    the log alternated "listening on port 23 / 22" and a client reached
    whichever had armed it last. The listener is now a table of four
    (TCP_MAX_LISTEN); tcp_listen adds a port, tcp_stop_listening removes
    it, and tcp_accept takes the port it collects for. Verified with pass,
    vault and hatch open at once, each answering its own client.
  - Also found: `pass` and `hatch` had been defined inside the Doom guard,
    so a DOOM=0 build had no web server and no remote shell. The guard now
    starts where the WAD-dependent commands do. Gate 74.

v0.28.0 (2026-10-07)  — ED25519, AND SHA-512 UNDER IT
  Stage S5. The host key SSH will prove itself with.
  - RFC 8032 hashes with SHA-512, so src/sha512.c came first: the same
    shape as sha256.c with 64-bit words, 80 rounds and a 128-byte block.
    The 80 round constants and the IV were DERIVED in Python from the cube
    and square roots of the first primes, not typed in; so were Ed25519's
    d, 2d, the base point, sqrt(-1) and the group order. A table copied by
    hand is where a silent wrong digit lives.
  - The field is implemented once: curve25519.c's arithmetic is exposed
    through fe25519.h and ed25519.c builds on it. Points are extended
    coordinates with the unified addition formula, so doubling is the same
    code and the ladder has no special cases. Scalars mod L use the
    schoolbook reduction, the one piece that is not field arithmetic.
  - IN `sample`: both SHA-512 vectors; RFC 8032's first three cases, each
    checking the public key from the seed, the signature over the message,
    and that it verifies; then a byte of a signature changed, which must
    not. All passed first run; three keygen+sign+verify rounds take 3
    ticks. The X25519 vectors still pass on the shared field.
  - Gate 74; sample is 46 tests (47 under slow).

v0.27.0 (2026-10-07)  — KILL REACHES A PROCESS BLOCKED IN THE KERNEL
  The hog the gate never killed (found in v0.26.0), fixed at the root.
  - proc_kill set the flag and relied on the process reaching a safe point
    to notice. A process blocked in a pipe read never does: it sits on the
    pipe's wait queue until its peer writes, and if the peer is the thing
    you are trying to get rid of, that is never. So `kill %` on
    `hog.elf | wc.elf &` flagged wc, wc stayed parked AND stayed the newest
    process, the second `kill %` flagged it again, and hog ran on at 90%.
  - Now a task records which queue it is parked on (task_t.blocked_on),
    and proc_kill pulls a running-but-blocked target off it with
    task_unblock. Every block site in the kernel loops and re-checks its
    condition, so an early wake is safe at all of them; the pipe loops
    already check the kill flag and return, and the syscall exit does the
    teardown. Reproduced before, verified after: wc dies on the first
    kill, the second reaches the hog, `ps` shows neither.
  - Two new gate checks assert exactly that. The gate's back half no
    longer runs under a hog, which is also the likeliest cause of the
    flaky job-control check (queue item 1); watch it.
  - A DETOUR WORTH RECORDING: the first build of this fix hung the shell
    the moment a process trapped, and so did a build with only the new
    field and no logic. The kernel stacks were at the same addresses as
    before, which ruled out heap layout and pointed at the build: the
    Makefile had no header dependencies, so editing task.h recompiled
    task.c alone and left twenty-two other objects with the old struct
    layout. -MMD/-MP with -include fixes that; touching task.h now
    rebuilds 23 objects. The lesson, for the next time an unrelated-
    looking change "breaks" the kernel: make clean first.
  - Gate 74.

v0.26.0 (2026-10-07)  — X25519
  Stage S4. The key exchange, and the bulk of the arithmetic.
  - src/curve25519.c: the field in sixteen 16-bit limbs held in signed
    64-bit words. Every product fits an int64, the carry is one pass, and
    the reduction uses only 2^256 = 38 mod p. Slow next to a radix-2^25.5
    implementation and a key exchange happens once a connection, so that
    is the right trade here: one exchange is under a tick.
  - RFC 7748 vectors in `sample`: both scalar multiplications and the
    iterated test at one round. The THOUSAND-ROUND chain is what catches a
    carry bug the single vectors get lucky on; it passed, but it takes
    1.5 s on a quiet machine, so it runs under `sample slow` with its own
    scripts/x25519-test.sh rather than in the gate.
  - That split was forced by a finding, not a preference. With the chain
    in plain `sample` the gate failed its mouse checks: the rounds took
    1812 ticks instead of 151, because `ps` later in the same run showed
    hog.elf at 90% CPU. The gate's `cook hog.elf | wc.elf &` then `kill %`
    twice never kills the hog: both kills land on wc.elf, which is blocked
    in a pipe read and never unwinds to honour the flag, so it stays the
    most recent process. The gate has been running its back half under a
    hog for as long as that line has existed. Queued as the next item.
  - Gate 72; sample is 41 tests, 42 under slow.

v0.25.0 (2026-10-07)  — CHACHA20-POLY1305
  Stage S3. The cipher SSH will actually run traffic through.
  - src/chacha.c: ChaCha20 with the 32-bit counter, Poly1305 in five 26-bit
    limbs with 64-bit products (what a 32-bit machine can do without a
    bignum), and the RFC 8439 AEAD on top. The pieces are exposed separately
    too, because chacha20-poly1305@openssh.com uses them its own way.
  - SIX CHECKS IN `sample`: the RFC's 2.4.2 cipher vector, the 2.5.2 MAC
    vector (fed in two pieces so the block buffer across an update boundary
    is exercised), the 2.8.2 AEAD ciphertext and tag, a round trip, and a
    flipped bit that must be refused. Expected values regenerated with the
    `cryptography` library, not copied from this code.
  - All six passed on the first run. The tag compare is constant-time; the
    rest is not. Gate 72; sample is 38 tests.

v0.24.0 (2026-10-07)  — AN ENTROPY POOL
  Stage S2. Key generation needs bytes nobody can predict, and a kernel
  that boots identically every time has almost none; this matters more
  than any of the ciphers.
  - src/random.c: a 32-byte pool folded through SHA-256. Fed by RDRAND
    when CPUID says the CPU has it (mixed into every output block, not
    only at boot), the TSC at every timer tick and keyboard interrupt, and
    the RTC once. The interrupt side is one XOR into a 16-word block; the
    hash runs only when the block wraps, about six times a second.
  - Output is hash(pool, counter, tsc, rdrand), and the pool is re-hashed
    after every draw so a leaked output does not give away earlier ones.
  - THERE IS NO TEST VECTOR FOR RANDOMNESS, so the checks are the ones
    that exist: `sample` requires two draws to differ and 64 KB of output
    to sit within three sigma on a monobit and a runs count (measured:
    262215 ones and 262002 runs out of 524288, inside one sigma). But a
    pool seeded from nothing passes those too, since SHA-256 of a constant
    is as balanced as SHA-256 of a secret. scripts/random-test.sh checks
    the property that matters: two boots draw different bytes. They do.
  - Gate 72; sample is 32 tests.

v0.23.0 (2026-10-07)  — SHA-256: THE FIRST SSH PRIMITIVE
  Stage S1 of the SSH plan. A hash is where everything else in the protocol
  gets its integrity from, so it goes first and gets the most vectors.
  - src/sha256.c is FIPS 180-4 as written: 64-byte block buffer, the eight
    working words, the compression function in the standard's order. HMAC
    per RFC 2104, with a key longer than a block hashed first.
  - SEVEN VECTORS IN `sample`, each chosen to probe a different path: the
    empty message, "abc", the 56-byte message whose padding spills into a
    second block, a million 'a's streamed in 1000-byte chunks, two RFC 4231
    HMAC cases and the one whose 131-byte key exceeds the block. Expected
    values were produced by Python's hashlib, never by this code.
  - All seven passed on the first run. That is worth stating because the
    vectors are the only reason to believe it: a SHA-256 with a wrong
    constant produces output exactly as plausible-looking as a right one.
  - Not constant-time, not hardened, for learning and a private network.
    The header says so. Gate 72; sample is 29 tests.

v0.22.0 (2026-10-07)  — THE PASS: soupOS OVER THE NETWORK
  Asked for SSH; this is the remote shell that comes first, so there is
  working access while the crypto is built underneath it.
  - soupOS already had everything but the carrier. console.c translates
    incoming bytes into the codes the PS/2 driver produces and mirrors output
    back - that is how the serial console works - so the translator was split
    out as console_rx_byte and a second output sink added, and remote.c pumps
    a TCP connection through both. `pass 23` opens it.
  - THE REMOTE USER DRIVES THE SAME SHELL AS THE LOCAL ONE, exactly as a serial
    user does, rather than getting a session of their own. soupOS has one
    shell; a second would mean a second cell buffer, cursor and line editor.
    It runs as a task, because a command sitting in a loop reading the socket
    could not also be the shell.
  - NOTHING IS ENCRYPTED AND NOTHING AUTHENTICATES ON THE WIRE. Whoever reaches
    the port gets the keyboard, and the login prompt they meet is typed in
    clear. The command says so in yellow when it opens. This is the
    local-network convenience before SSH, not a substitute for it.
  - Verified from the host: nc into the forwarded port, type `whoami` and
    `larder`, and both answers come back over the socket. One trap for the
    test: a string piped into nc gives it EOF at once and it hangs up before
    the server's one-second accept poll gets there, which looks exactly like a
    server that never answers. The session has to be held open.
  - Gate 72; scripts/pass-test.sh is the network check.

v0.21.0 (2026-10-07)  — MORE THAN ONE CONNECTION AT A TIME
  Third queue, item 4. `hatch` served one client, then listened again, because
  the listener WAS the connection - the same single block of state. Now there
  is a table of four, and the listener owns none of them.
  - A SYN for the listening port takes a free slot and the listener stays
    listening, so a client knocking mid-request is served instead of refused.
    tcp_input's "is this for our one connection?" became a four-tuple lookup,
    which is the whole structural change; the state machine itself did not
    move an inch.
  - The public API grew handle forms - tcp_send_on, tcp_recv_on, tcp_close_on -
    and tcp_accept returns one. The plain forms stayed, forwarding to whichever
    slot tcp_connect took, because reserve, holler and takeout each make
    exactly one outbound connection and should not have to care.
  - Four slots at 4 KB of receive buffer each is 16 KB, which is the whole cost.
  - PROVEN BY THREE CLIENTS AT ONCE: HELLO.TXT, RECIPE.TXT and SECRET.TXT
    fetched in parallel come back with their real sizes - 47, 75 and 31 bytes -
    across three DIFFERENT slots. The slot numbers are the evidence that they
    overlapped rather than queued, and hatch-test.sh now checks both.
  - THE TEST HUNG AND IT WAS MY SHELL, NOT THE KERNEL. The parallel block ended
    in a bare `wait`, which waits for every background job - including the QEMU
    process, which never exits. Timing the fetches settled it in one run: each
    took between four and twenty-one milliseconds and returned 200, so nothing
    was slow at all. It waits on the curl PIDs now, with a note saying why.
  - Gate 72.

v0.20.0 (2026-10-07)  — WRITING LONG FILENAMES
  Third queue, item 3. Reading them landed in v0.16.0 and left an asymmetry:
  a file created with a long name still got a mangled 8.3 one. Both halves
  work now, and an independent implementation agrees.
  - A name that will not survive 8.3 unchanged - too long, lower case, spaces,
    more than one dot - gets an alias plus a chain of long entries in front of
    it. Both are decided BEFORE any cluster is touched, so a directory with no
    room for the chain fails early rather than leaving a file nothing can name.
  - THE ALIAS IS THE FIDDLY HALF, as the item predicted. Spaces and dots are
    dropped rather than folded, anything outside the permitted set becomes an
    underscore, and the ~N tail is checked against a real directory lookup
    rather than assumed unique. "My Long Note.txt" and "My Long Novel.txt"
    share a stem and correctly get MYLONG~1 and MYLONG~2.
  - The chain needs CONSECUTIVE slots, which the old allocator could not
    promise - it hands out one at a time. dir_alloc_run reserves a whole run,
    numbering slots across the directory so a run may cross a sector boundary
    without the caller caring. If no run is long enough, the write falls back
    to an 8.3-only name instead of failing: a file under an ugly name beats no
    file.
  - VERIFIED BY SOMEBODY ELSE. scripts/fat-longname-test.sh writes the names
    from soupOS and then has MTOOLS read the image: a separate implementation
    seeing "My Long Note.txt" and "My Long Novel.txt" with distinct aliases is
    real evidence that the chain order, the checksum, the UCS-2 encoding and
    the tail are all right, where soupOS reading its own file back would only
    prove self-consistency.
  - FOUND WHILE LOOKING: mtools displays soupOS's aliases in lower case,
    because byte 12 of a directory entry holds VFAT's "base is lower case"
    (0x08) and "extension is lower case" (0x10) flags, and soupOS keeps its
    permission mode in that byte - FAT_PERM_OX and FAT_PERM_OW are those exact
    bits. Pre-existing, cosmetic, and now written down rather than puzzled
    over again later.
  - Gate 70 -> 72.

v0.19.1 (2026-10-07)  — THE JOB-CONTROL FLAKE: NOT REPRODUCED, NOT PAPERED OVER
  Third queue, item 1. A gate run had failed with "stopped job kept running:
  1 lines while parked" and the next passed. The item said to find out why
  before touching the check, and the honest result is that I could not.
  - Twenty-four targeted runs - ten in isolation, fourteen under the gate's own
    conditions, with two background markers already running and an `orders`
    between the stop and the resume - never reproduced it. So it is below
    about four percent, which is also why it had not been seen before.
  - What the code says, which is why the failure is interesting rather than
    obviously benign: proc_take_stop logs "stopped" at the moment it parks,
    not when the stop is requested, so a line printed on the way to the safe
    point should land BEFORE that message. A line after it is not explained by
    stop latency alone.
  - ONE DIFFERENT ANOMALY DID APPEAR, once in fourteen: the "continued" marker
    never arrived, so the job did not resume or did not log it. Recorded rather
    than chased, because one occurrence is not a diagnosis.
  - So: the check is UNCHANGED, and its failure message now prints the log
    lines around the boundary. The next occurrence will carry its own evidence
    instead of leaving the investigation to start from nothing again.
    scripts/jobcontrol-soak.sh drives the sequence in bulk for anyone with the
    machine spare.
  - Loosening a check because it caught something, without understanding what,
    is how a gate stops being worth running. It stays as it is.

v0.19.0 (2026-10-07)  — VOLUME, AND WHAT A FAILING TEST ACTUALLY MEANT
  New queue item 8, the last in that queue. `hush [0-100]` sets how loud the
  kitchen gets; bare `hush` reports it.
  - The level is applied to the SUM, not to each voice, which is what makes it
    a volume control rather than a per-sound one: the balance between music
    and effects stays put as it moves. And it is applied BEFORE the
    saturation, so turning down removes clipping instead of scaling a signal
    that has already clipped.
  - Both properties measured, as the item asked. The same tone at 100 then at
    50 peaks 32767 then 16383, exactly half. Two overlapping effects clip 12
    samples at full and zero at 40, where the peak is 13824.
  - THE MIXER TEST THEN FAILED, AND THE CAUSE WAS NOT THE CHANGE. Its envelope
    correlation dropped from 1.0000 to 0.9384 - still a better match to the
    sum than to the louder lump alone, so mixing plainly worked, but under the
    0.97 threshold. Rather than loosen it, I searched for the alignment that
    fits: 0.9999 with the pistol delayed 63 ms, which is exactly three ring
    chunks of 21 ms. That is the mixer's three-chunk lead on the card's read
    position doing its job - a voice added once playback is under way is first
    heard three chunks later. The old 1.0000 was the lucky case where both
    voices were added before the ring started, and whether that happens is a
    race against the WAD read for the second lump.
  - So the test now searches offsets and asserts what matters: the best fit is
    excellent, needs only a few chunks, and beats the single lump. That is a
    better test than the one that passed by luck, and it came from
    understanding the failure rather than adjusting a number until it went
    green.
  - sound-test.sh gained the volume check, which immediately broke its own
    effect-duration measurement: it spans a contiguous capture and now had a
    third sound in it, so 513 ms read as 853. The tone lengths are subtracted
    by a named constant with a note that adding a sound means updating it.
  THE QUEUE IS EMPTY AGAIN. Eight items, all closed.

v0.18.2 (2026-10-07)  — WARNINGS ARE ERRORS NOW
  New queue item 7. One tolerated warning teaches the eye to skip the
  compiler's output, and the `sink` one had been doing exactly that all
  session.
  - `sink` is the accumulator a deliberate cpu-burner writes and nothing read,
    so GCC was right. It is read now, in the line that reports the burn
    finished, which both silences the warning honestly and shows the loop ran
    rather than being optimised away.
  - THE EXTRA FLAGS EARNED THEIR PLACE IMMEDIATELY. Beyond -Wall -Wextra, this
    now builds with -Wshadow, -Wpointer-arith, -Wstrict-prototypes and
    -Wold-style-definition, and -Wshadow caught something from the same day:
    the `pixels` global I added to vga13h.c for the framebuffer work shadows
    vga13h_blit's parameter of the same name. Not a bug - that function wants
    its parameter and gets it - but a trap for whoever edits it next, so the
    newcomer yielded the name and is `pixel_store`.
  - -Werror, with WERROR=0 to turn it off for bisecting or for a newer GCC
    that invents a warning this code has not met. Five configurations build
    clean: default, FB=0, DOOM=0, CHALLENGE=1 DOOM=0 and PROFILE=1.
  - A FLAKE WORTH RECORDING RATHER THAN FIXING BLIND. One gate run failed on
    "stopped job kept running: 1 lines while parked" and the next passed. That
    check asserts a Ctrl+Z'd process prints nothing while parked, and one line
    slipping out looks like a race between the stop taking effect and the
    process's next write. Nothing in this commit touches job control. It is in
    the queue as its own item; loosening the check to accept what it caught
    would be the wrong instinct without understanding it first.

v0.18.1 (2026-10-07)  — WHAT EACH TASK COSTS
  New queue item 6. `ps` grew a CPU% column, and the question the item was
  really asking - what does the console renderer cost, repainting thirty times
  a second? - has an answer: NOTHING MEASURABLE. It reads 0%.
  - The timer interrupt charges each tick to the running task. The window is a
    hundred ticks of a 100 Hz timer, so the figure IS a percentage with no
    division, and every task's window rolls at the same instant, which means
    the numbers in one listing all describe the same second instead of
    drifting apart.
  - WHAT IT MEASURES, SAID PLAINLY: who HELD the cpu, not who did work. A task
    halted in a wait loop is still the current task, so the shell sitting at a
    prompt reads 100%. That makes the figure useless for "how busy is the
    machine" and exactly right for "is a background service costing more than
    it should", which is the question that prompted the item.
  - Idle, on the framebuffer build: kernel 100%, fbcon 0%. The renderer diffs
    two thousand cells thirty times a second and almost nothing changes, so
    the work is in the comparison rather than the drawing.
  - `sample` asserts no background service exceeds 50% and logs the busiest
    one by name, so a renderer that starts spinning fails the gate rather than
    being noticed eventually. 21 -> 22 assertions, gate 69 -> 70.
  - Also fixed the throwaway runshell helper to boot a COPY of disk.img, like
    the committed scripts already do: an interactive QEMU window holds a write
    lock on the image, and the helper had been failing with "cannot connect to
    monitor" whenever one was open.

v0.18.0 (2026-10-07)  — THE soupOS LOGO, AND A CONSOLE THAT FITS IT
  Queue item 5's second half, and the new boot logo, which turned out to need
  each other.
  - assets/logo.txt is the source of truth, UTF-8, eight rows and sixty-nine
    columns of block characters. scripts/gen-logo.py turns it into src/logo.h
    and REFUSES anything outside the four glyphs it is made of, so a stray
    non-breaking space cannot reach the screen as whatever CP437 has at that
    code point. The header is committed, so a build without Python still works.
  - TWO ENCODINGS, ON PURPOSE. VGA text mode prints one glyph per byte, so
    UTF-8 in a C string becomes three garbage characters per block: the screen
    gets CP437 (0xDB, 0xDC, 0xDF) through a new vga_puts_screen_only, which
    does NOT mirror to the serial console, and the log gets the UTF-8 form via
    klog. Checked: the serial log holds all eight rows as readable UTF-8.
  - Checked before writing any of it that the embedded 8x16 font really is
    CP437 at those three code points - full block, lower half, upper half -
    rather than assuming, because a Latin-1 font would have rendered the logo
    as a row of accented letters.
  - THE LOGO DID NOT FIT, AND THAT IS WHY THE CONSOLE GREW. Eight rows plus a
    tagline plus seventeen boot lines is twenty-seven rows on a twenty-five row
    console: the logo scrolled off before the login prompt, and screendumps at
    0.35 s already showed it gone. The framebuffer console is now 128x48 -
    1024x768 of 8x16 glyphs, exactly - and the geometry is set in fb_early,
    BEFORE the first character, because the cell store's row stride is the
    column count and changing it later re-interprets text already written.
    Content now ends at row 43 of 48, so nothing scrolls. On a FB=0 text boot
    the logo still scrolls off; the queue item said to say so rather than
    shrink it.
  - Two boot messages were lying - both said "80x25" as a literal while the
    console was 128x48 - and now print the real geometry.
  - The gate's two mouse checks assumed an 80x25 screen: the pointer starts at
    the console's centre, which moved. They now drive it into the corner, where
    it clamps, and move a known amount from there, so they hold at any size.
  - VERIFIED BY LOOKING. The screendump shows SOUPOS in solid cyan blocks with
    the grey tagline centred beneath and the boot log below; every block is
    solid and there are no stray characters. assets/boot-screenshot.png.

v0.17.0 (2026-10-07)  — DOOM'S MUSIC
  New queue item 4. `hum D_E1M1` plays a tune, and a level now plays its own.
  - A D_* lump is MUS: a header, an instrument list, then a score of events on
    sixteen channels timed in ticks of 140 Hz, with a variable-length delay
    after any event whose top bit is set. Five event types actually occur -
    play, release, pitch bend, controller, end - which I confirmed by walking
    D_E1M1 on the host before writing any of it.
  - Square waves, one per channel, scaled by channel volume. Doom's music was
    written for an OPL2 and emulating one is a project rather than a feature;
    square waves carry the tune, the rhythm and the harmony, which is what
    makes a level feel like that level. The instrument list is read and
    ignored, pitch bend is ignored, and channel 15 (percussion, where the note
    selects a drum rather than a pitch) is skipped, because no drums beats
    wrong drums. All three are said out loud in music.h.
  - NOT A MIXER VOICE, and that was the design question worth getting right.
    Sixteen channels do not fit in four, and music is not an effect: it runs
    continuously, it loops, and it must not be stolen when three doors open at
    once. So the mixer asks music.c for one sample per frame and adds it,
    which leaves all four voices free and keeps the channel count private.
  - VERIFIED AGAINST THE SCORE, not by ear. scripts/music-test.sh parses
    D_E1M1 on the host, works out which notes should sound in the first second
    (MIDI 40, 50, 52 -> 82, 147, 165 Hz) and finds all three in the capture,
    each far above a frequency the score never uses. Then it plays two effects
    over the music and checks the envelope rises and returns: music 1291, with
    effects 3978, music again 1554.
  - TWO SELF-INFLICTED BREAKS, both caught by the gate. DOOM=0 stopped
    linking, because music.c needs the WAD reader - the same mistake doomsnd.c
    made in v0.11.1, so the mixer now stubs music out under NO_DOOM. And the
    gate's sound-effect measurement read 4251 ms, because QEMU writes only
    while the card is active, so a tone, an effect and music land contiguously
    in one capture and a span-based measurement sweeps in whatever played
    next. Those two measurements moved to scripts/sound-test.sh, which plays
    exactly two things; in isolation they read 440 Hz and 533 ms.
  - Gate 70 -> 69 (two measurements left for their own script, one MUS check
    arrived), seven test scripts green, four configurations clean.

v0.16.0 (2026-10-07)  — LONG FILENAMES, READ SIDE
  New queue item 3. `serve` lists the name a person actually typed, and `pour`
  opens a file by either its long name or the 8.3 alias beside it.
  - A long name lives in the entries IMMEDIATELY BEFORE its 8.3 entry, in
    reverse order, 13 UCS-2 characters each in three runs - the fields a real
    entry uses for the cluster and size had to be stepped around. The
    accumulator lives outside the sector loop, because a chain can straddle a
    sector boundary.
  - THE CHECKSUM IS VALIDATED, which is the step that gets skipped. Every LFN
    entry carries a checksum OF THE 8.3 NAME, and without checking it a chain
    orphaned by a tool that removed the short entry gets attached to whatever
    entry follows, so a file lists under a name belonging to something else.
    A chain with a missing piece or a mismatched checksum is distrusted and the
    8.3 name is used instead.
  - THE SAME BUG, TWICE, IN TWO FILES. Listing worked immediately and opening
    did not, because a path component was copied into a 13-byte buffer:
    next_component in fat.c capped at FAT_NAME_MAX-1, and after fixing that it
    still failed because normalize_path in shell.c had its own identical cap.
    "A Long Recipe Name.txt" became "A Long Recip" before fat_read ever saw it.
    A file that lists but will not open means the name is being truncated on
    the way in, not that the directory parsing is wrong.
  - Writing long names is NOT done: a file created with one still gets a
    mangled 8.3 name. The item said read side, and the asymmetry is honest
    rather than hidden.
  - scripts/qemu_keys.py learned '~', '?' and '!', because an 8.3 alias
    contains a tilde and the gate now types one.
  - The gate's check helper takes an optional -cN to assert a pattern appears
    at least N times, which is how two opens of the same file are told apart
    from one. Gate 67 -> 70.

v0.15.0 (2026-10-07)  — soupOS ANSWERS: A LISTENING SOCKET AND AN HTTP SERVER
  New queue item 2. The stack could only ever dial out; now it can be dialled.
  `hatch [port]` is a serving hatch, answering HTTP GETs for files on the FAT
  volume, and `curl` from the host fetches them through QEMU's hostfwd.
  - TCP grew a passive open: TCP_LISTEN and TCP_SYN_RCVD. A listener has no
    peer yet, so the input path matches it on the local port alone and fills
    in the four-tuple from the SYN that arrives; everything else still matches
    the full tuple. A repeated SYN in SYN_RCVD re-sends the SYN-ACK, because
    the first one being lost is the normal way this fails.
  - The ACK that completes the handshake FALLS THROUGH to the data path rather
    than returning, because SLIRP puts the request's first bytes in that same
    segment often enough to matter.
  - ONE CONNECTION AT A TIME, deliberately, because the listener IS the
    connection: the same single block of state. So the server listens again
    after each client, and a SYN arriving mid-request is refused rather than
    queued. A backlog needs a table of connections, which is a different
    change; this is written in tcp.h rather than left to be discovered.
  - THE BUG THAT MATTERED, AND IT WAS NOT IN TCP. The first fetch hung with
    `[net] drop: no arp entry for 10.0.2.2`. Replies are built in interrupt
    context, where ip_send refuses to resolve - and a peer does not have to
    ARP us first: SLIRP learned our MAC from the DHCP exchange, so an inbound
    SYN arrived with nothing in the cache to address the SYN-ACK to. net_rx
    now learns the sender's hardware address from every IP frame, not only
    from ARP traffic, which is the same policy handle_arp already applied to
    the other half and is what lets soupOS answer ANY peer rather than only
    ones it has spoken to first.
  - scripts/hatch-test.sh starts the server and fetches with curl from the
    host: the right body, the right Content-Length, a 404 for a missing file,
    and a SECOND request to prove the listener re-arms. Three accepted
    connections in the log is the evidence for that last one.
  - Not in the main gate: hatch blocks until a key is pressed, so the fetch has
    to happen while the guest sits in the command, and the gate drives a
    straight keystroke sequence with no way to act in the middle of one.
  - Gate still 67 (the outbound checks unchanged), six test scripts green.

v0.14.1 (2026-10-07)  — DOOM'S FRAME COST, MEASURED (AND LEFT ALONE)
  New queue item 1. The scaler writes 2.3 MB a frame where mode 13h wrote
  64 KB, and nobody had measured what that costs. The answer is that it does
  not matter, and the evidence is worth more than a speedup would have been.
  - The blocker first: the profiler reports every 50 FRAMES and only the
    in-level render loop counts frames, so a run that never starts a level
    reports nothing at all. Getting there headlessly needs THREE returns -
    main menu, episode, skill. Two is not enough, which is exactly why this
    looked impossible last night.
  - Thousands of cycles per frame, same scene, both builds:
        framebuffer   frame=2088k  copy=558k  vbl=0k
        mode 13h      frame=1544k  copy= 34k  vbl=18k
    The scale-and-blit costs about 16x the plain memcpy it replaced, which
    makes a frame 35% more work. The vblank wait disappears, correctly, since
    there is no CRT to chase when the card is scanning out an LFB.
  - AND IT IS FREE ANYWAY. fps reads 50 in both, which is the cap:
    FRAME_TICKS is 2 ticks of a 100 Hz timer. Uncapped - FRAME_TICKS 0 - the
    framebuffer build measured 1666 fps, so the 50 fps cap uses about 3% of
    what the machine can do and the extra 35% is absorbed by idle time.
  - SO NOTHING WAS OPTIMISED. The 2x scale, the dirty-row tracking and the
    wider blit the item listed are all unnecessary; writing any of them would
    have been work in exchange for nothing, which is what measuring first is
    for. The same instinct, unmeasured, produced a 47% slowdown in v0.10.3.
  - scripts/doom-profile.sh runs both builds and prints the comparison, with
    the three-returns recipe and the FRAME_TICKS explanation in its header, so
    the next performance question costs one command.

v0.14.0 (2026-10-07)  — THE FRAMEBUFFER IS THE DEFAULT, AND DOOM RENDERS INTO IT
  soupOS now boots to a 1024x768 framebuffer with a glyph-rendered console,
  and mode 13h programs scale into it. FB=0 still builds the text-mode kernel,
  which is the only way to get real mode 13h hardware.
  - THE SAME TRICK AS THE TEXT CONSOLE, ONE LAYER DOWN. vga13h's pixel store
    became a pointer: the real 0xA0000 on a text boot, a RAM shadow on a
    framebuffer boot. Doom already rendered into its own backbuffer and copied
    to hardware in exactly ONE place, so redirecting that one copy was the
    whole integration. vga13h_present() scales 320x200 up by 3 - the largest
    whole multiple that fits 1024x768 - through a software copy of the palette.
  - The scaler builds each row once into a line buffer and lays it down three
    times, so the palette lookup happens once per SOURCE pixel instead of once
    per destination pixel, and the writes to video memory stay sequential.
  - vga13h_enter/exit now hand the screen over: the console stops painting (it
    would otherwise draw text over Doom thirty times a second) and every cell
    is marked dirty on the way back, because what is underneath is no longer
    the text we last drew. Any mode 13h program gets this for free.
  - TASKS CAN DECLARE THEMSELVES SERVICES. Doom asks for the machine to itself,
    and with a console renderer and a sound mixer always running the answer was
    permanently no. task_count_users() ignores tasks that called
    task_set_service(), which is the number that question actually wants.
  - PALETTE PRECISION, FOUND BY COMPARING FRAMES. Widening 6 bits to 8 is bit
    replication, (v<<2)|(v>>4), not a plain <<2: the latter leaves full scale
    at 252 and every bright colour a shade dark. Found by comparing a
    framebuffer frame against a mode 13h frame pixel by pixel - agreement went
    from 42% to 99.97%. The remaining 18 pixels of 64000 are a three-row band
    that differs by 13 levels, which is an animated strip between two separate
    runs.
  - DELIBERATELY NOT bit-identical to QEMU: its VGA renders odd 6-bit values as
    (v<<2)|3, replicating the low bit, which is an emulator artefact. Matching
    it would make the comparison exact and the colours wrong, so
    scripts/doom-fb-test.sh carries a 4-level tolerance and says why. A real
    fault - wrong scale, wrong offset, swapped channels, unshadowed palette -
    scores 1-45%, nowhere near the threshold.
  - `make iso` is now the framebuffer image; `make iso-text` builds the FB=0
    one. Gate still 67 on the default image, five test scripts green, five
    build configurations clean.

v0.13.0 (2026-10-07)  — THE MIXER: SEVERAL SOUNDS AT ONCE
  Night-queue item 8's third milestone, and the last open item in the queue.
  The AC97 plays exactly one stream, so overlapping sounds are summed in
  software: four voices into one cyclic ring.
  - A voice is either a Doom lump kept in its RAW form - unsigned 8-bit at the
    lump's own rate, resampled on the way out - or a generated tone, which is
    a phase accumulator and no storage at all. Keeping lumps raw makes a slot
    64 KB instead of 200. A fifth sound steals the oldest voice.
  - The ring is 32 chunks of about 21 ms over the same staging memory, topped
    up by a task that keeps a three-chunk lead on the card's read position.
    NO INTERRUPT: the scheduler already wakes a task every 10 ms, which is
    five times more often than a chunk empties, and a polled task cannot lose
    a wakeup the way a blocked one can. Idle, the card drains and halts on its
    own, and the task just looks less often.
  - Summing saturates through a 32-bit accumulator. Two full-scale sounds clip
    audibly, on purpose, rather than wrapping - which sounds like the machine
    dying. The clipping is visible in the capture as an 880 Hz harmonic when
    440 and 660 overlap.
  - `whistle` and `sizzle` both became voices, so there is one audio path
    instead of two, and `sizzle` now takes several lumps at once.
  - TWO MEASUREMENT TRAPS, BOTH WORTH MORE THAN THE FEATURE. First, DURATION
    CANNOT TELL YOU WHETHER SOUNDS MIXED: a pistol that queues behind a saw
    and a pistol that is silently dropped both leave the saw's length on the
    clock, and my first reading of "1802 ms, about right" was worthless. The
    test that works renders both lumps on the host, sums them with the same
    saturation, and correlates envelopes - mixed output matches the SUM
    (1.0000) better than the louder lump alone (0.8717). Second, THE
    KEYSTROKE DRIVER TAKES ABOUT TWO SECONDS PER COMMAND, which is longer
    than most of these sounds, so two typed sizzles never overlap at all.
    That artefact looked exactly like a mixing bug for a while. Both lumps now
    start from one command, which is why `sizzle` takes a list.
  - scripts/mixer-test.sh carries that correlation check. The main gate's tone
    check moved to the mixer's log line, and the effect's duration window grew
    on the upper side because the ring drains ~35 ms after a voice ends.
  - ALSO FIXED: every test script now boots a throwaway COPY of disk.img.
    An interactive QEMU window holds a write lock on the image, so running a
    test while one is open failed with "Failed to get write lock" - and a
    hermetic disk also stops state leaking between runs, which has caused a
    flaky check before.
  - Gate 66 -> 67 checks, four test scripts green, all four configurations
    build from clean.
  THE NIGHT QUEUE IS NOW EMPTY.

v0.12.1 (2026-10-07)  — THE FRAMEBUFFER CONSOLE
  Night-queue item 9 finished: a full 80x25 text console rendered in the
  classic 8x16 font on the linear framebuffer, and nothing above vga.c knows.
  - THE DESIGN IS ONE SENTENCE: the 80x25 cell buffer stays the authoritative
    console, and a task repaints changed cells as glyphs thirty times a
    second. The shell, the editor, the clock, the whole stack keep writing
    text cells exactly as before; the renderer just watches. The diff is 2000
    uint16 compares a frame, which is nothing.
  - WHAT KILLED THE FIRST VERSION: the legacy window at 0xB8000 is DEAD in a
    linear mode on QEMU's VGA - writes discarded, reads all-ones. My aliasing
    test before building had checked that text writes did not corrupt the
    picture, which proved the window was not aliased into VRAM, and silently
    did not prove it stored anything. I tested the failure I feared instead of
    the property I relied on. The heartbeat that settled it was one line: the
    task cycling happily, painting zero cells, because every read came back
    0xFFFF and matched the 0xFFFF the shadow was seeded with.
  - So the cell store is now a POINTER in vga.c: the hardware buffer on a text
    boot, a RAM array on a framebuffer boot, switched by fb_early() before the
    first character is printed. The editor and the bgclock, which poked
    0xB8000 directly, go through vga_cells() now - the only two callers
    outside vga.c, found by grep rather than assumed.
  - The font is the classic 8x16 (kbd's default8x16), embedded as a C array:
    a kernel booted straight into graphics has no text mode to steal the VGA
    character generator from, so the plane-2 trick vga13h.c uses is not
    available. Mode 13h commands (doom, vgademo, bounce) decline politely on
    a framebuffer boot, with the reason printed.
  - VERIFIED GLYPH-FOR-GLYPH: framebuffer-test.sh logs in through the rendered
    console, types a command, and finds the typed text in a screendump
    pixel-identical to the same font rendered on the host - at (344,184),
    which is exactly cell 19 of row 0. One matcher lesson recorded in the
    script: anchor the search on the glyphs' mid-height row, because the top
    row of most letters is blank and an all-black anchor matches the first
    black run in the image instead of the text.
  - The scroll self-test runs in both modes again, since the cell store is
    real memory either way. Gate still 66 on the text image; all four
    configurations build from clean.

v0.12.0 (2026-10-07)  — A REAL FRAMEBUFFER, STAGE ONE
  Night-queue item 9, the detection-and-drawing half. GRUB hands over a linear
  framebuffer; soupOS maps it and draws, verified pixel by pixel.
  - boot.asm's multiboot header can request video (make FB=1), kernel_main
    reads address, pitch, width, height and depth from the multiboot info, and
    fb.c maps the thing - at 0xFD000000 on QEMU, the first memory soupOS has
    ever touched outside the identity-mapped low range.
  - THE WRONG ASSUMPTION, CAUGHT BY THE GATE. I believed grub.cfg's gfxpayload
    decides the mode and the header only states a preference. It is the
    OPPOSITE: a header that requests video gets a linear framebuffer even from
    a menu entry that says gfxpayload=text. The first attempt shipped the
    request unconditionally, and the ordinary text boot came up in 1024x768
    graphics - the gate caught it as a failing VGA scroll self-test, because
    0xB8000 stops behaving like a text buffer in a graphics mode. So the
    request is a build flag, the framebuffer is its own image (make iso-fb),
    and the reason is written in boot.asm where the next person will look.
    Runtime switching is not possible from protected mode and the note says
    why.
  - Pitch is bytes per scanline, NOT width times bytes per pixel: a row starts
    at y * pitch. Assuming otherwise shears the picture down the screen.
  - scripts/framebuffer-test.sh boots the FB image, screendumps, and checks
    four regions of a drawn pattern pixel-exactly: three colour bands and a
    white centre square. A wrong pitch or a swapped colour order fails there.
  - sample's VGA scroll test now skips itself on a framebuffer build, because
    there is no text buffer to scroll; checked rather than left to fail.
  - Gate still 66 on the text image; all four configurations build.
  Left in item 9: the console on top - the 8x16 font blitter behind the same
  vga_* interface. That is stage two.
