  - doomsnd.c calls the WAD reader, and DOOM=0 filters out doom.c and wad.c,
    so the link failed on wad_find_lump, wad_read_lump and wad_init. doomsnd.c
    now joins that filter, and `sizzle` sits behind #ifndef NO_DOOM with its
    dispatch entry, its name in the completion list and its help line.
  - MY PROCESS FAILED, not just the code. I check all the configurations before
    committing precisely so this cannot happen, and this time the shell line
    was `for cfg in ...; done; make && ... && git commit`, so the loop printed
    FAILED while the default build still satisfied the && chain and the commit
    went through. The check has to GATE the commit, not merely precede it.
  - All four configurations verified from clean here: default, DOOM=0,
    CHALLENGE=1 and CHALLENGE=1 DOOM=0. Gate still 66.

v0.11.1 (2026-10-07)  — DOOM HAS SOUND
  Night-queue item 8, second milestone. The pistol fires and the doors open
  with the WAD's own effects.
  - A DS* lump is DMX format: eight bytes of header (format 3, sample rate,
    sample count) then UNSIGNED eight-bit samples centred on 128. Each sample
    needs recentring, widening to signed sixteen-bit, and resampling from
    11025 Hz to the card's 48000. Nearest-neighbour, because on eight-bit
    effects recorded in 1993 an interpolating resampler is inaudible; what IS
    audible is getting the ratio backwards, which plays a pistol at quarter
    speed and sounds like a different weapon.
  - `sizzle <LUMP>` plays any effect from the shell, which is how this is
    tested without driving the game. doom.c fires DSPISTOL on the shot being
    spent and DSDOROPN when a door starts opening, both non-blocking so the
    frame carries on.
  - AC97 grew a staging area and a non-blocking play, since a sound effect
    cannot block a game frame for half a second.
  - THE BUG, AND HOW IT MISLED ME. The reset write at the top of the play path
    was `outb_((uint8_t)cr, ...)` - a truncating cast that sent it to port 0x1B
    instead of 0xC41B. The first sound after boot worked and every later one
    was silent. My first hypothesis was wrong: I predicted two tones in a row
    would also fail, tested it, and both played, because the blocking path
    clears the run bit itself and that was enough. What settled it was reading
    the controller's registers 50 ms in - CIV, PICB, SR, CR - which showed the
    card running and consuming samples at exactly 48000 frames a second while
    producing nothing. The channel was fine; the reset was going to the wrong
    port.
  - Verified against the source material, not by ear: the captured effect is
    0.513 s, the same as the lump's 5661 samples at 11025 Hz, and its envelope
    correlates with the original at 1.0000.
  - Gate 64 -> 66. QEMU's wav backend writes only while the card is active, so
    the tone and the effect land back to back as one 813 ms burst; measuring
    across both averages a sine with a gunshot and reports 585 Hz. The tone is
    measured over the first 250 ms and the effect is the remainder.
  Left in item 8: mixing more than one effect at once, which needs a software
  mixer because the hardware has a single PCM-out channel.

v0.11.0 (2026-10-07)  — SOUND: AC97, AND A TONE THAT IS MEASURED
  Night-queue item 8, first milestone. The PCI groundwork from the network card
  was most of it: find the device, enable bus mastering, read two BARs.
  - What is different is the shape of the DMA. The RTL8139 takes one buffer per
    packet; AC97 reads a BUFFER DESCRIPTOR LIST of up to 32 entries, each
    naming a block of samples, and walks it itself. Hand it the list, say which
    entry is last, set the run bit.
  - `whistle <hz> <ms>` plays a sine through it (`beep` remains the PC
    speaker). Boot reports the card and its rate.
  - THE BUG THE MEASUREMENT CAUGHT, which is the whole reason the queue said to
    verify this on the host rather than by ear. The phase accumulator's full
    32-bit range is one period, so the step is hz * 2^32 / rate; I wrote 2^24.
    That plays a 1.7 Hz wave: inaudible, full amplitude, and indistinguishable
    from silence by ear. The capture showed it immediately - a signal with NO
    zero crossings in 0.39 s and no spectral peak anywhere near 440 Hz. After
    the fix: 440.00 Hz measured over 172 periods, with 60x the power of its
    neighbouring bins.
  - The step is computed as hz * (0xFFFFFFFF / rate) so every operation stays
    32-bit and the kernel needs no 64-bit division helper.
  - Volume on AC97 is attenuation, so 0 is loudest and 0x8000 is mute, which
    reads backwards from every other volume control in existence.
  - A descriptor's length is a count of SAMPLES, not bytes and not frames.
    Getting it wrong plays the right sound at the wrong speed, which is easy to
    mistake for a sample-rate problem.
  - Gate 62 -> 64 checks: one that the card accepted the tone, and one that
    runs on the HOST over the samples QEMU captured (-audiodev wav) and asserts
    438-442 Hz. That second one is the only check here that proves the DMA, the
    descriptor list, the mixer and the oscillator all worked together.
  Next in item 8: Doom's DS* lumps (11025 Hz unsigned 8-bit) resampled to the
  card's rate. Mixing several at once is a milestone after that.

v0.10.5 (2026-10-07)  — A `let` IN A while LOOP STOPS RUNNING OUT
  Found while writing the disk-fill script in v0.10.4, which died at iteration
  64 with "too many variables".
  - N_FOR already dropped the bindings its body made, once per iteration.
    N_WHILE never did, so a `let` inside a while body accumulated a binding
    every pass and hit MAX_VARS, which is 64. Not many iterations for a loop.
    N_WHILE now does exactly what N_FOR does, which makes the two consistent
    as well as correct.
  - Checked that nothing relied on the leak: no script on the disk uses a
    variable after the loop that declared it, and recursion through a while
    body (FN.SC's factorial and fib tables) is unaffected, since function
    calls save and restore nvar around the call.
  - Gate 61 -> 62: 200 iterations each declaring a variable, asserting the
    exact sum, so a scoping change that drops the wrong bindings shows up as a
    wrong total rather than as a crash.

v0.10.4 (2026-10-07)  — ITEM 7 FINISHED: THE LEAK, PROVEN
  The cluster leak fixed in v0.10.3 was established by reading all five
  failure paths. Now it is demonstrated, and the demonstration is repeatable.
  - scripts/fullness-test.sh builds a 32 MB image that is already nearly full
    (a 64 KB file to copy, filler leaving 22 KB free), then copies the 64 KB
    file, which cannot fit. WITH THE LEAK: 22 KB free before, 0 KB after -
    every remaining cluster allocated to a file whose directory entry never
    got written, referenced by nothing, gone until a reformat. WITH THE FIX:
    22 KB before, 22 KB after. The script was then checked the other way, by
    putting the leak back and confirming it reports FAIL; a test that cannot
    fail is worth nothing.
    Not in smoke-test.sh because it needs a disk of its own. Filling the real
    image from inside soupOS (soup FILLDISK.SC) also works but takes minutes.
  - TAB COMPLETION: MEASURED, AND DELIBERATELY NOT CACHED. The entry has asked
    for a per-directory cache since v0.7. A directory scan, the thing a Tab
    press pays for, measured 1.9 ms on a normal root (33 entries) and 5.5 ms
    on a crowded one (128). Both are far below anything a person can feel, so
    a cache would buy nothing and cost an invalidation bug surface that got
    worse the moment spawn() made another task able to write files while the
    shell holds a listing. Closed as won't-do, with the numbers, rather than
    left open to tempt someone.
  - soupyc's remove(path), because writing FILLDISK.SC turned up that the
    language could create files but not delete them, so a script that fills a
    disk cannot clean up after itself.
  - FOUND WHILE WRITING THAT SCRIPT: a `let` inside a loop body makes a fresh
    binding on every iteration, and MAX_VARS is 64, so any loop that declares
    a variable dies after 64 passes with "too many variables". Scopes are only
    popped on function return, not at the end of a block. Queued as its own
    item; FILLDISK.SC hoists its declarations and says why.
  - Gate 60 -> 61 checks.
  Item 7 is now closed: of its five entries, four were resolved by measuring
  or reading rather than by assuming, and one was a real bug that was
  mis-described.

v0.10.3 (2026-10-07)  — THE KNOWN-BUGS SWEEP, VERIFIED FIRST
  Night-queue item 7, taking the list seriously enough to check it. Two of the
  four open entries were wrong, and one named a fix that would have made things
  worse.
  - VGA SCROLL: the complaint was right, the suggested remedy was not. The
    entry said to use memmove. str.c's memmove is a BYTE loop, so handing it
    3840 bytes does twice the work of the 1920 cell copies it replaced:
    measured, it was 47% SLOWER (600 scrolled lines, 504 ticks -> 743). Copying
    32-bit words instead - two cells per store, and 0xB8000 plus an even
    VGA_WIDTH make the alignment exact - runs in 271 ticks, so the same
    workload is about twice as fast as where it started. Guarded now by a
    `sample` test that checks the scroll moves exactly 24 rows and blanks the
    last, because a bad word count would shift the screen by half a cell and
    look like a font bug.
  - FAT: the entry said fragmented free clusters are mishandled. They are not;
    fat_alloc_cluster scans from a hint and wraps, so it finds free clusters
    wherever they are, and the write loop chains them correctly. The REAL bug
    is next door, and it is worse: all five failure paths in the write loop
    returned -1 with clusters already allocated and no directory entry written,
    so nothing on the disk referenced them and nothing ever would. Space lost
    until a reformat, and the way to hit it is exactly the case the entry
    mentioned - a write that runs out of room halfway. Every failure now gives
    the chain back, with the one exception that must not: once the directory
    entry references the chain, freeing it would leave a dangling entry, which
    is worse than lost space.
  - There was NO WAY TO SEE FREE SPACE from inside soupOS, which is part of why
    that leak could sit there unnoticed. `larder` now reports clusters used and
    KB free, and `sample` asserts the invariant the leak broke: a write
    followed by a delete gives back exactly what it took.
  - prompt_row: the entry asks for a bounds check that already exists.
    vga_set_cursor_impl clamps row and col, and the multi-match completion path
    calls prompt(), which re-records prompt_row after the candidate list has
    scrolled the screen. Marked stale. Worth knowing: spawn() (v0.10.2) means
    arbitrary output can now land while the line editor is active, which can
    desync prompt_row in a way background job reports were specifically handled
    to avoid. Queued as its own item rather than guessed at here.
  - Gate 59 -> 60 checks, `sample` 19 -> 21 assertions.
  That is three of the four list entries resolved by checking rather than by
  assuming, after item 4 already retired a fourth the same way.

v0.10.2 (2026-10-07)  — spawn(): A SCRIPT FUNCTION AS A TASK
  Night-queue item 6, finished. This is what the context work was for.
  - spawn("worker") runs a no-argument soupyc function on a task of its own and
    returns the task id, which `ps` lists and `kill` accepts.
  - THE LIFETIME DECISION. The child gets an INDEPENDENT COPY of the code, not
    a borrowed pointer into the parent's node pool. Borrowing would have been
    a memcpy cheaper, but soupyc_run frees the parent context on the way out,
    so the child could not then outlive the `soup` command - and the shell
    getting its prompt back while the child keeps printing is the entire point.
    Node links all point inside one contiguous pool, so copying it and adding
    a fixed delta to every link relocates the whole tree; string literals live
    in the string store rather than the pool, so each is re-interned into the
    child's own store.
  - sleep(ms) as well, bounded at 10 s. A spinning child would work, since the
    scheduler preempts, but it would burn the machine to do it.
  - The struct members now carry an m_ prefix. Code that holds a context
    pointer explicitly - the clone, the child's entry - has to say ctx->m_x,
    and without the prefix the bare name expanded into the member position.
    Same class of collision as sc_state.pool in v0.10.1, now impossible.
  - Gate 54 -> 59 checks, and they test the properties rather than the happy
    path: that spawn returns an id, that `ps` catches the child mid-flight
    (which is the proof it outlived its parent), that the parent's arrays
    survive the child allocating its own and vice versa (the pool is shared,
    with per-context ownership of slots), and that the child finishes.
  - Nothing was wrong with `ps` - it lists blocked tasks fine. My first two
    attempts to see the child there ran `ps` at the exact moment the child was
    exiting. Check the timing before suspecting the code; that is twice in two
    nights.

v0.10.1 (2026-10-07)  — soupyc IS REENTRANT
  Night-queue item 6, second part. The preemption audit named this as the one
  thing standing between soupyc and a background spawn: 24 file-scope
  declarations holding every bit of interpreter state, safe only because
  nothing ran soupyc off the shell task.
  - One context per running script (soupyc_ctx_t, ~60 KB), allocated in
    soupyc_run and hung off the task that is interpreting, so two interpreters
    never share a variable table, a node pool or a lexer position. The
    previous value is saved and restored, so a nested run cannot lose its
    parent's state.
  - THE 1500 LINES UNDERNEATH DID NOT CHANGE. The state names are now accessor
    macros - `pool`, `tok`, `src` and the rest each resolve through the current
    task - so the interpreter still reads as plain names and the diff stays
    reviewable. The types moved to src/soupyc_types.h so the context struct can
    be declared before anything that touches it.
  - WHAT STAYS GLOBAL, AND WHY. sc_state (the array pool) cannot move: stage 4
    of the challenge reaches after_hook by a fixed distance from an array's
    elements, so the layout is load-bearing. Instead each context records which
    slots it allocated and releases only those, and the blanket "clear all
    arrays" at startup is GONE - it would have pulled the rug from under a
    script running on another task. rng_state stays global because shared
    entropy is the point.
  - The challenge layout is now frozen by a compile-time assertion on the two
    offsets that distance is made of, so editing that struct fails the build
    instead of quietly breaking the challenge.
  - Two collisions found by the compiler, both from macro names meeting member
    names: sc_state's own `pool` member (renamed to arr_slots - same type,
    same order, same offsets) and `ctx->arr_owned` in the new entry point,
    which has to go through the accessor like everything else.
  - Gate still 54 checks, every soupyc one now exercising the context path;
    all three build configurations verified. The statics are gone from .bss.
  Still open from item 6: spawn() itself, which is what this was for.

v0.10.0 (2026-10-07)  — soupyc STRINGS LEAVE THE 47-CHARACTER CAGE
  Night-queue item 6, first and largest part. docs/next-steps.md has carried
  this as the longest-standing limitation in the tree: a string value was a
  fixed char[48], which is why a soupyc script could not write an ELF.
  - val_t now carries a pointer and a length instead of a buffer. SVAL_LEN
    stays, but only for identifiers and keywords, where 48 is plenty.
  - THE OWNERSHIP QUESTION, which is the whole design. val_t is copied by
    value everywhere - returned from eval_node, stored into variables and
    array slots, passed as arguments - so one string can be referenced from
    several places with no record of how many. Freeing on overwrite would be a
    use-after-free the moment a temporary outlived its slot, and a collector
    cannot help: the temporaries live in C locals, invisible to anything that
    scans the interpreter's own structures. So the store is an ARENA for the
    duration of one script, released in full when the script ends, with a 2 MB
    budget. A runaway loop gets a clean "out of string memory" error instead of
    starving the 8 MB heap that Doom's zone allocator needs.
  - Literals are interned once at parse time and referenced, never copied, so
    a literal inside a loop costs nothing.
  - Everything that used to truncate at 47 now allocates what it needs:
    concatenation, upper, lower, substr, read, input, ai. read() losing its cap
    is the one that matters - a script can now read a file and write it back.
  - Gate 50 -> 54 checks, with exact lengths so a truncation anywhere shows up
    as a wrong number: built 100, literal 97, file 100, and the runaway script
    failing cleanly with the shell still alive afterwards.
  - MY OWN BUG, recorded because the symptom was so misleading: adding the new
    drive commands REPLACED the takeout line instead of following it, so an
    HTTP check failed with "no match" and looked like a TCP regression. Nothing
    in this commit touches TCP. Worse, I then read a stale serial log - the
    gate deletes its log unless KEEP=1, so `ls -t` handed me one from three
    hours earlier, whose contents are evidence of an ALREADY-FIXED problem.
    Use KEEP=1 and check the timestamp.
  Still open from item 6: the per-invocation context, then spawn().

v0.9.9  (2026-10-07)  — DRAG-SELECT IN jot
  Night-queue item 5, finished. The mouse now does something.
  - jot used to block in keyboard_getchar, which cannot see a mouse at all, so
    its input loop now waits on either source and redraws when a packet moves
    the pointer. Press drops the mark, dragging moves the cursor, and because
    that is the same mark-and-cursor pair the keyboard selection uses, ^C ^X ^V
    work on a mouse selection with no new code behind them.
  - cell_to_offset maps a screen cell to a buffer offset, clamping past the end
    of a line to its end and past the end of the file to the end of the buffer,
    so dragging into empty space selects up to where the text stops instead of
    doing nothing.
  - The pointer is drawn by inverting whatever is under it rather than owning a
    glyph, so it never hides a character.
  - Gate 49 -> 50 checks: press at the start of the line, drag five cells, ^C,
    and assert the clipboard holds "abcde". The CONTENT, not the length - a
    mapping off by one still copies five bytes, just the wrong five.
  - THE GATE ALSO CAUGHT SOMETHING UNRELATED. Three TCP checks went red, and it
    was not the kernel: the host listeners take a 300-second accept timeout as
    a budget for the whole drive sequence, and when it expires they exit and
    everything after gets a refused connection. The run now takes 321 seconds.
    Raised to 900 with a comment saying what the number is for, because the
    failure looks like a TCP regression and is not one.
  - Note for test-writing: my first two attempts at this failed because the
    press landed on row 12 of a one-line file, where mark and cursor both clamp
    to the same offset and the selection is empty. The driver was fine both
    times. Check the coordinates before suspecting the code.

v0.9.8  (2026-10-07)  — PS/2 MOUSE
  Night-queue item 5, first half. The copy-and-paste work said selection would
  stay keyboard-driven "unless a PS/2 mouse driver is built first"; this is it.
  - The i8042's auxiliary device on IRQ 12. Three things make it fiddlier than
    the keyboard, and each one is commented where it bites:
      * IRQ 12 is on the slave PIC, so the line needs opening AND the cascade
        on the master does too. irq_unmask does both - the lesson the network
        card taught in v0.9.0.
      * Commands reach the mouse through the controller with a 0xD4 prefix and
        are each answered with 0xFA. Not waiting for that acknowledgement
        leaves the next command talking to a device still replying to the last.
      * Packets are three bytes with no framing, so a single dropped byte
        misaligns the stream forever. Bit 3 of the first byte is always set,
        and anything else at that position is discarded and counted.
  - Positions are text cells (80x25) so the pointer lines up with everything
    else drawn on screen, with four raw counts per cell because the device
    reports far finer movement than a character grid can show.
  - Shell: `skewer` reports position, buttons, packets and resyncs.
  - Gate 48 -> 49 checks, driven through the QEMU monitor rather than by hand:
    the pointer starts centred at 40,12 and two injections of (40,-20) land it
    at exactly 60,2, so a sign error or a scaling change fails the check.
  Next: drag-select in jot, so the existing ^C/^X/^V work with a mouse
  selection (the second half of the queue item).

v0.9.7  (2026-10-07)  — `sample`: IN-KERNEL SELF-TESTS
  Night-queue item 4. The smoke gate drives the machine from outside and reads
  the serial port, which cannot see a subsystem that no command exercises.
  These are those edges, 19 assertions in src/selftest.c behind the `sample`
  command, ending in one machine-readable line the gate greps.
  - Heap: allocate/write/free, sixteen blocks, and - the one that matters - a
    block spanning them after freeing in order AND in reverse, which only
    succeeds if free blocks are merged in both directions.
  - VFS handle table: fill it, confirm it refuses a seventeenth rather than
    handing one out, confirm closing returns the slots.
  - Pipe ring: push six times its capacity through in chunks, checking content
    rather than counts, so an off-by-one at the wrap shows up as a wrong byte;
    then confirm a drained pipe with no writer reads EOF instead of blocking.
  - A checksum with a known answer computed independently, a FAT round trip on
    the real disk, and the hand-written string routines including memmove's
    overlap case.
  - IT IMMEDIATELY CORRECTED THE RECORD. This list has carried "kfree only
    forward-coalesces, so long sessions fragment the heap" as an open bug. It
    is stale: heap.c walks for the predecessor and absorbs into it, and the
    reverse-free test passes. Fixed at some point without the note being
    updated, which is exactly the sort of thing a test finds and a reader
    never does. Corrected here and in the night queue.
  - Gate 47 -> 48 checks.

v0.9.6  (2026-10-07)  — THE PREEMPTION AUDIT, CLOSED
  Night-queue item 3. docs/next-steps.md has carried "drivers are not
  preemption-safe" since before processes existed; this closes it with an
  answer per file rather than a vague worry. docs/preemption-audit.md has the
  whole thing; the summary:
  - TWO REAL RACES, both reachable, both fixed:
      1. The pipe ring. pipe_read_op and pipe_write_op both read-modify-write
         p->count, and the reader and writer are by definition different
         tasks, so a preemption inside either copy loop loses or duplicates
         bytes. Both loops now run under preempt_disable.
      2. The network transmit staging buffers. txf and the IP and UDP buffers
         are file-scope, and transmits happen from task context (ping, DNS,
         tcp_send) AND interrupt context (ICMP reply, ARP reply, TCP ACK). A
         task halfway through building a frame, interrupted by the card, had
         its frame overwritten before the hardware saw it. Build and hand-over
         now run with interrupts off.
  - SAFE TODAY, with the reason recorded: soupyc (69 statics, but reached only
    from two call sites in shell.c, both on the shell task - and the trigger
    that changes that is spawn(), which is why item 6 lists a per-invocation
    context as a prerequisite rather than a tidy-up), users.c and shell.c (shell
    task only), the rest of vfs.c (handle table guarded in v0.8.5; per-node
    state belongs to one process because fd tables do).
  - KNOWN AND LEFT: klog's ring buffer is written from both contexts, but the
    modulo keeps the indices in range, so the worst case is two interleaved
    lines in a debugging aid. Guarding it means interrupts off around a serial
    poll at 38400 baud, which would be felt. Deliberate.
  - The method is in the doc: by call graph, not by looking for missing locks.
    The files declared safe are safe because nothing can reach them from two
    tasks, not because their contents look harmless.

v0.9.5  (2026-10-07)  — TCP, STAGE THREE: CLOSE, AND AN HTTP CLIENT
  Night-queue item 2c, and the end of the TCP arc. `takeout example.com /`
  fetches a page.
  - The close state machine: FIN_WAIT_1, FIN_WAIT_2, CLOSE_WAIT, LAST_ACK and
    TIME_WAIT, with both the active close (we hang up first) and the passive
    one (they do) handled. Our FIN occupies a sequence number, exactly like the
    SYN, and is retired when the peer's acknowledgement reaches snd_nxt.
  - TIME_WAIT lingers 200 ms rather than the two minutes a real stack waits.
    The trade is written down in the file: soupOS uses a fresh ephemeral port
    per connection, so a late duplicate has nothing to be confused with, and a
    shell that froze for two minutes after every close would be unusable.
  - `takeout <host>[:port] [path]`: HTTP/1.0 with Connection: close, so the end
    of the body is the end of the connection and there is no Content-Length or
    chunked decoding to write for a command whose job is to prove TCP works.
  - TWO BUGS, both found by reading a packet capture rather than the code:
      1. The input path began with `if (state != ESTABLISHED) return;`, so the
         moment the peer's FIN arrived we went deaf - including to the
         acknowledgement of data we were still retransmitting. One early return
         caused both symptoms; the capture showed the request going out three
         times while the server sat there having already answered it.
      2. A close that timed out waiting for the peer's FIN marked the
         connection CLOSED and said nothing, leaving it half-open at the other
         end still holding a socket. It now sends a RST, which also stopped the
         gate's listener stalling for five seconds after every `reserve`.
  - A THIRD THING, recorded rather than fixed: three connections in a row to
    the same SLIRP port leave the third unanswered, with no RST and no SYN|ACK.
    Each works alone and the frames are identical, so this is QEMU-side state
    outliving our close. The gate gives each check its own listener port.
  - Gate 45 -> 47 checks: an HTTP page fetched over TCP, and a connection that
    closed cleanly in both directions.

v0.9.4  (2026-10-07)  — TCP, STAGE TWO: DATA
  Night-queue item 2b. `holler 10.0.2.2 19100 souptcp` sends over TCP and
  prints what comes back.
  - Stop-and-wait, and the header says so: one segment in flight, acknowledged
    before the next goes out, retransmitted four times before giving up. No
    congestion control, no window scaling, no selective acknowledgement. An
    out-of-order segment is dropped and re-acked at the old position, which is
    this stack's way of asking for a resend. On a virtual network that costs
    nothing; the comment says it would not be free on a real one.
  - Acknowledgement comparisons use signed arithmetic on the difference
    ((int32_t)(a - snd_una) > 0) rather than a plain >, because the sequence
    space wraps and a naive comparison breaks once the numbers pass 2^31.
  - The receive buffer is filled by the interrupt handler and drained by
    tcp_recv, which takes its snapshot with interrupts off rather than trying
    to be clever with a lock-free ring for one reader.
  - A FIN is acknowledged and noted but not acted on: the close sequence is
    stage 2c. Acking it at least stops the peer retransmitting it at us.
  - Shell: `holler <host> <port> <text>`.
  - Gate 43 -> 45 checks. The gate's listener now echoes what it is sent,
    upper-cased, so a reply proves our segment arrived, was acknowledged, and
    theirs was taken in sequence and handed to the reader. Verified by eye too:
    `souptcp` out, `SOUPTCP` back.

v0.9.3  (2026-10-07)  — TCP, STAGE ONE: CONNECTION SETUP
  Night-queue item 2a. soupOS completes a three-way handshake.
  - src/tcp.c: one connection at a time, the same single-slot model the
    resolver uses, which keeps the state machine in one visible struct instead
    of a table. CLOSED -> SYN_SENT -> ESTABLISHED, SYN retransmitted twice
    before giving up, and a peer RST reported as a refusal.
  - The rule that matters for everything above it: a SYN occupies one sequence
    number. snd_nxt is iss + 1 after sending ours, and rcv_nxt is their seq + 1
    on receiving theirs. Getting that wrong is the classic way to acknowledge
    one short and stall forever.
  - A duplicate SYN/ACK is re-acked rather than ignored, which a packet capture
    showed was needed: the peer retransmits when our ACK is late, and silence
    makes it keep going until it gives up.
  - tcp_close() sends RST, not FIN. The graceful close is stage 2c, and
    pretending otherwise here would leave a half-closed connection nobody
    handles. Said so in the file.
  - Shell: `reserve <host> <port>`, which books a table and releases it.
  - Gate 42 -> 43 checks, hermetic: the gate starts a listener on the host's
    loopback, which the guest reaches through QEMU's 10.0.2.2, so no external
    network is involved.
  - ONE THING LEFT UNEXPLAINED, deliberately. QEMU ignores the first SYN after
    an ARP exchange and answers the retransmit 7 ms later, costing one second
    on the first connect. Both SYNs are byte-identical on the wire, same
    destination MAC, same checksum, so the frame soupOS emits is correct and
    the behaviour is on QEMU's side. Recorded rather than chased.

v0.9.2  (2026-10-07)  — DHCP
  soupOS takes its address from the network instead of having it compiled in.
  Night-queue item 1.
  - DISCOVER, OFFER, REQUEST, ACK, with the address, netmask, gateway and
    resolver read out of the ACK's options. Lease time is logged and then
    deliberately ignored: there is no timer-driven background work to renew on,
    and a hobby OS that reboots more often than its lease expires loses nothing.
  - The compiled-in addresses remain as the fallback for a network with no DHCP
    server, and boot says which it used: "IP 10.0.2.15 (dhcp)" or "(static)".
    `faucet` says the same, and `plumbing dhcp` re-leases on demand.
  - Two things the IP layer had to learn, both predicted in the queue entry:
    sending from a source of 0.0.0.0 (we have no address when we ask for one)
    and sending to 255.255.255.255 without trying to ARP for it. ip_send is now
    a wrapper over ip_send_from, and net_accept_any_dst opens the "is this for
    us?" test for the duration of the exchange, since the reply is addressed to
    an address we do not hold yet.
  - A BUG WORTH RECORDING: the first version sent three DISCOVERs, got three
    OFFERs on the wire, and reported "no offer". SLIRP answers from inside the
    same process, fast enough that the reply reached the interrupt handler
    before net_udp_wait had armed the port, so every one was dropped as
    unexpected. The fix is net_udp_listen, called BEFORE the request goes out;
    DNS had worked only because it happened to arm first by accident.
  - Gate 41 -> 42 checks. This one is hermetic, unlike the DNS check: SLIRP
    serves DHCP itself, so it needs no real network.

v0.9.1  (2026-10-06)  — UDP, DNS, AND KITCHEN NAMES FOR ALL OF IT
  `sniff example.com` resolves, and `sip example.com` pings the result: soupOS
  talks to the real internet by name.
  - UDP with the pseudo-header checksum, and one outstanding request at a time
    rather than a socket table, which is all a resolver needs.
  - DNS: one question, A records, no cache. The fiddly part of a reply is name
    compression (a length byte >= 0xC0 is a pointer), so records are walked by
    skipping names rather than following them. A dotted quad passes straight
    through the resolver, so `sip` takes a name or an address through one path.
  - A BUG WORTH RECORDING: the first query was logged as sent and never
    reached the wire. ip_send requires the next hop to be in the ARP cache and
    returned silently when it was not; net_ping resolved first, and the new
    UDP path did not. Two fixes: net_udp_send resolves its own next hop (it
    runs in task context, so it may block), and ip_send now says when it drops
    a packet, because that failure being invisible is what cost the time.
  - KITCHEN NAMES. The commands added over this session were tonally wrong for
    a kitchen OS, so: jobs/fg/bg -> orders/plate/steep, clip -> scraps,
    netinfo/ifconfig/arp/ping/nslookup -> faucet/plumbing/table/sip/sniff.
    Two things fell out of the rename worth noting: a blanket
    function-name replace turned the unrelated cmd_bgclock into
    cmd_steepclock, and the old `str_startswith(p, "bg")` dispatch had been
    shadowing the pre-existing `bgclock` command since job control landed.
    Both fixed.
  - Gate 40 -> 41 checks. The DNS ANSWER is deliberately not asserted: it
    needs the host to have working DNS, and a gate that fails when you are
    offline is a bad gate. What is asserted is that the query is built and
    handed to the card.

v0.9.0  (2026-10-06)  — NETWORKING: soupOS CAN PING
  From no concept of a network at all to `sip 10.0.2.2` replying, verified
  against QEMU's own packet capture rather than against what soupOS claims.
  - RTL8139 DRIVER (src/rtl8139.c). Chosen because QEMU emulates it and it is
    all I/O ports plus two DMA areas: no MMIO window, no firmware. The buffers
    are static arrays, which works precisely because the kernel is
    identity-mapped, so their addresses already ARE the physical ones the card
    wants. 8 KB receive ring with the datasheet's wrap allowance, four 1792
    byte transmit buffers used round-robin.
  - PCI gained what a driver actually needs: pci_find, config-space reads AND
    writes, pci_bar, pci_irq_line, and pci_enable_bus_master. Without the
    bus-master bit the card DMAs nothing and says nothing about it.
  - STACK (src/net.c): Ethernet, ARP with an 8-entry cache, IPv4 with header
    checksums, and ICMP echo both ways, so soupOS answers pings as well as
    sending them. Host byte order everywhere above the wire, converted in one
    place. Receive runs in the interrupt handler, which is not what a grown-up
    kernel does, but this one's handlers already do real work and inventing
    deferred work for one driver is the wrong order.
  - Addresses are compiled-in defaults matching QEMU's user-mode network
    (10.0.2.15, gateway 10.0.2.2); DHCP is a later problem. `plumbing` can
    change the address at runtime.
  - Shell: faucet, plumbing, table, sip, sniff.
  - TWO BUGS WORTH RECORDING, both found by the packet capture rather than by
    reading code:
      1. Nothing transmitted at all. The transmit path decided a descriptor
         was busy from the OWN bit, whose polarity is documented
         inconsistently; inverted, every descriptor looked busy forever. It
         now tracks completion through TOK/TUN/TABT, which are unambiguous.
      2. Transmits worked, replies never arrived. idt_init unmasks only IRQ0
         and IRQ1, so the card's IRQ 11 never reached the CPU. New
         irq_unmask() opens a line, and for a slave line it also opens the
         cascade on the master, which is the part that is easy to forget.
  - Gate 38 -> 40 checks, including a real ARP-then-ICMP exchange.
  Design notes: docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-06-networking-design.md
  Not included: DHCP, UDP, TCP, DNS, anything above ICMP.

v0.8.5  (2026-10-06)  — THE PAGE ALLOCATOR WAS NOT SAFE FOR TWO PROCESSES
  pmm_alloc_page() was completely unguarded:

      for (i = 1; i < MAX_PAGES; i++)
          if (!is_used(i)) { set_used(i); used_pages_++; return ...; }

  A read-modify-write on shared state. That was fine while one program ran at
  a time, and stopped being fine in v0.8.0: every proc_spawn calls
  paging_new_dir, and every ELF load and every sbrk calls map_user_page, so
  since then several processes can be inside that loop at once. Two of them
  seeing the same page free hands the SAME physical frame to both, which is
  silent cross-process memory corruption. used_pages_++ has the same window,
  and that counter is what the gate's leak check reads.
  - PROVEN, not assumed. New user/memtest.c fills its own pages with a tag and
    checks them after yielding. Four copies launched by hand never collided,
    because typing the commands staggers them by a second each. Launching them
    as one pipeline starts them microseconds apart, and with a deliberate
    switch inserted between the check and the claim the log read
    "[pmm] RACE: page 2541 was taken while we slept" and the processes died.
    Worth recording what did NOT reproduce it: widening the window with a busy
    spin instead. A collision needs the OTHER task's short scan to land inside
    the window, so stretching the window after the scan barely changes the
    odds. That is also why this is rare in practice rather than constant.
  - FIXED with irq_save/irq_restore around both the claim and the free,
    matching heap.c. Interrupts off rather than a mutex because the critical
    sections are a few instructions, never yield, and no interrupt handler
    allocates, so there is nothing to block on.
  - vfs.c's alloc_node had the same shape of bug: two processes in vfs_open or
    vfs_pipe could both claim one slot in the 16-entry handle table. Guarded
    with preempt_disable, which is enough because the scan never yields.
  - Gate 35 -> 38 checks: three processes started by one pipeline, each
    verifying its own pages survived the others allocating.

v0.8.4  (2026-10-06)  — COPY AND PASTE
  A system clipboard shared by the editor and the shell, so a span cut in jot
  pastes at the prompt and the text Ctrl+U kills comes back with Ctrl+V. The
  last of the four process/UX milestones.
  - NEW src/clip.c + clip.h: one 4 KB fixed buffer, set/get/len/peek/clear.
    Fixed rather than heap-allocated because the shell touches it on every
    Ctrl+U and nothing here is allowed to fail, which keeps every caller free
    of error handling.
  - jot gains a mark. Ctrl+B anchors it at the cursor; the span between mark
    and cursor renders highlighted (inverse video) and is what Ctrl+C copies
    and Ctrl+X cuts. Ctrl+V pastes at the cursor. The status bar shows [MARK]
    while one is set, and the hint line lists the keys.
    The mark is an anchor, not a direction: it can sit either side of the
    cursor, and sel_span() orders them.
  - The shell's line editor fills the clipboard from the text it already
    throws away: Ctrl+U (kill to start) and Ctrl+W (kill word) now copy what
    they removed, so Ctrl+V undoes them. A pasted newline or tab becomes a
    space, because the prompt is one line and half a command submitting itself
    would be a nasty surprise.
  - New `scraps` command shows the contents, and mirrors them to the kernel log,
    which is what lets the headless gate check a copy by its actual text
    rather than by its length.
  - There is no mouse driver, so selection is keyboard-driven throughout. A
    PS/2 mouse would make drag-select possible and is not needed for this.
  - Gate 32 -> 35 checks: Ctrl+U's text arriving on the clipboard, jot copying
    a marked span and pasting it (checked through the saved file's byte and
    word counts), and Ctrl+V pasting at the prompt (checked by pasting the
    word `scraps` back and letting it run, so the command only executes if the
    paste worked). The gate deletes the test file with `strain` first, so a
    second run starts from the same state as the first.

v0.8.3  (2026-10-06)  — PIPES, REDIRECTION, AND A SEGMENT BUG FROM v0.7.0
  `cook echo.elf hi | upper.elf | wc.elf` and `cook cat.elf < A.TXT > B.TXT`
  work. Getting there turned up a latent kernel bug that had been waiting since
  ring 3 was introduced.
  - THE BUG. The ISR/IRQ/syscall stubs never touched the segment registers, so
    after a trap from ring 3 the kernel ran on the USER data selector. That was
    survivable only because it is flat, and because segment registers are
    global state that task_switch does not save. The first time a ring-3
    program BLOCKED inside a syscall (which nothing did before pipes), the
    sequence became: the reader blocks; another task runs and leaves DS set to
    the kernel selector (user_mode_exit does exactly that); the reader resumes
    and irets to ring 3 holding a DPL-0 data segment; the CPU nulls every data
    segment whose DPL is below the new CPL; the program's next memory access
    faults; the fault handler faults the same way because DS is null; the
    machine dies with interrupts off before anything can be logged.
    Diagnosed from a register dump showing DS=ES=FS=GS=0000 and QEMU's -d int
    showing no exceptions at all. Each stub now saves the interrupted
    context's data selector, loads the kernel one for the handler, and
    restores it before iret, so the kernel no longer depends on who it
    interrupted. registers_t gains `ds` as its first member to match.
  - FDS 0-2 ARE BINDABLE. An entry in proc_t.ufds wins over the console
    default, so a spawn can be handed stdin/stdout. usermode_run clears the fd
    table from 3 up now, or it would wipe what the spawner just bound.
  - PIPES are a VFS backend: one 4 KB ring shared by two ordinary vfs_node_t
    handles. A reader blocks while the pipe is empty and a writer exists, and
    reads 0 once the last writer closes; a writer blocks while full and a
    reader exists, and fails once every reader closes. Both give up when the
    running process has a kill pending, so a program parked on a pipe nobody
    will write to stays killable, and both enable interrupts to block for the
    same reason proc_take_stop does.
  - THE SHELL runs pipelines: `|` up to 4 stages, `<` and `>` on the ends, a
    trailing `&` for the whole thing. Every stage is checked before any is
    started, the last stage gets the terminal, and the shell waits for it
    first, because it is the one Ctrl-C and Ctrl-Z act on.
  - cat.elf became a filter with no argument; upper.elf and wc.elf are new.
  - fork() was dropped from this milestone with reasons: soupOS's shell lives
    in the kernel, so nothing duplicates itself before exec, and proc_spawn
    already makes a process from nothing. It is worth building the first time
    a user program needs a child.
  - Gate 27 -> 32 checks.
  Design notes: docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-06-pipes-and-redirection-design.md

v0.8.2  (2026-10-06)  — JOB CONTROL
  Ctrl-Z stops the foreground program and hands the shell back; `steep` resumes it
  in the background, `plate` resumes it in the foreground and waits again. M2 of
  the process milestones.
  - proc_state_t gains PROC_STOPPED. A stopped process is alive, keeps its
    address space, and its task is BLOCKED, so it costs nothing but RAM.
  - It parks at the same three safe points the kill unwind uses, for the same
    reason: no kernel lock is held there, so parking cannot strand a mutex.
    Syscall entry, syscall exit, and an IRQ that interrupted ring-3 code. That
    third one is what lets a program making no syscalls be stopped at all.
  - proc_take_stop enables interrupts to park (both safe points are interrupt
    gates, and task_block_on ends in hlt, so parking with IF clear would never
    wake) and restores the caller's IF afterwards. The IRQ handler in
    particular must not continue with interrupts on, since it goes on to call
    sched_preempt.
  - Ctrl-Z (0x1A) is raised in the keyboard IRQ next to Ctrl-C, because a
    foreground program means the shell is blocked in proc_wait reading nothing.
  - proc_wait reports two outcomes now: 1 exited (code written, slot
    collected), 0 stopped (slot kept, job still exists). A stopped child wakes
    its parent exactly like an exited one, which is what returns the prompt.
  - proc_kill continues a stopped process as well as flagging it, or the kill
    would be noticed only when something else woke it, which may be never.
  - `plate [pid]`, `steep [pid]`, both defaulting to the newest job; `orders` shows T
    for stopped; proc_most_recent counts stopped jobs as live, so a bare `plate`
    and `kill %` can reach them.
  - Deliberately not included: process groups (a job is one process until pipes
    exist, so a group of one is bookkeeping with no behaviour) and user-space
    signal handlers (every disposition here is taken by the kernel, so there is
    no new ABI to get wrong).
  - Gate 22 -> 27 checks. The new ones use marker.elf, which prints a tagged
    line every 100 ms, so its output IS the evidence: nothing printed while
    parked, printing again after bg, the shell taking commands in between, and
    a stopped job still being killable.
  Design notes: docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-06-job-control-design.md

v0.8.1  (2026-10-06)  — DOOM IS 23x FASTER
  "Doom runs horribly." It did, for two independent reasons, one in the port
  and one in how QEMU was being started. Both were found by measuring rather
  than guessing: PROFILE=1 builds the port with per-phase rdtsc counters that
  report fps and kilocycles per phase to the serial log every 50 frames.
  - THE PORT. Of 6.13M cycles per frame, draw_flats was 5.45M, or 89%. It
    called find_sector_at() for every floor and ceiling pixel, and that is a
    BSP descent plus four dependent lookups (subsector -> seg -> linedef ->
    sidedef), ~100 cycles, 53,760 times per frame. Real Doom knows the sector
    from the BSP traversal and draws visplanes.
    Along one screen row the visible floor is a straight line in world space,
    so the sector only changes where that line crosses a sector boundary.
    flat_row_runs() samples every 16 pixels and bisects where two samples
    disagree, giving the exact boundary pixel for a few dozen descents per row
    instead of 320. Frame 6.13M -> 1.53M cycles (4.0x); flats 5.45M -> 0.86M
    (6.4x). Verified by screendump: 0 of 256,000 pixels changed.
  - THE HOST. No accelerator was ever requested, so QEMU emulated every
    instruction with TCG. The run targets now probe /dev/kvm and use it when
    it is there, falling back to TCG when it is not. Same scene: 9.9M cycles
    per frame under TCG against 1.53M with KVM.
    The smoke test deliberately stays on TCG and the stock qemu32 CPU: that
    is the configuration whose missing SSE2 caught the v0.7.4 triple-fault,
    and -cpu host would mask that class of bug.
  - Together: 35M cycles per frame before, 1.53M after.
  - TURNING. 32 view angles meant each step rotated the world 11.25 degrees,
    which looks like stop-motion at any frame rate. Now 128 steps of 2.8125
    degrees, with TURN_DELAY 4 -> 1 so the turn rate is unchanged. All the
    derived constants go through ANG_N/ANG_90/ANG_PER_DIR/ANG_SKY_U. The
    spawn frame is pixel-identical to the 32-angle build.
  Not changed, measured and found innocent: the vblank spin (~15k cycles,
  QEMU's default retrace emulation just toggles the bit) and the 64,000-byte
  copy to VRAM (~33k with KVM). Both are rounding errors next to the renderer.

v0.8.0  (2026-10-06)  — CONCURRENT RING-3 PROCESSES
  A program is a process now, not a coroutine the shell runs on its own task.
  Several run at once, background jobs work, and a program that refuses to
  cooperate can be killed.
  - PER-TASK RING-0 STACK. tss.esp0 was set once at boot to a single static
    kstack and tss_set_esp0() was never called at all; kernel_resume_esp was
    one word in .bss. With two programs that is a corruption bug: the timer
    fires while A is in ring 3, the CPU pushes A's frame onto kstack,
    sched_preempt switches away with that frame live, then B syscalls onto the
    same esp0 and lands on top of it. Both are now task_t.user_resume_esp, and
    task_yield moves tss.esp0 with `current`. It is the resume esp, not the
    stack top, because user_mode_enter leaves four saved registers on that
    stack for as long as ring 3 runs. (src/task.c, usermode_asm.asm, gdt.c)
  - NEW src/proc.c + proc.h. A process IS a kernel task (pid == task id), so
    the scheduler, wait queues, mutexes and reaper are reused as-is. proc_t
    holds what used to be file-scope in usermode.c (exit code, fd table, heap
    break, args, page budget, in-user flag) which is exactly what limited the
    machine to one program. 8 static slots, no allocation.
  - The slot outlives the task on purpose: the reaper frees a DEAD task_t and
    its stack, so an exit code kept there would vanish before the parent read
    it. Zombie until proc_wait collects it or the next prompt announces it.
  - exec_elf(path, args) -> usermode_run(proc_t *), called on the process's own
    task. cmd_cook no longer enters ring 3 on the shell task, which is why a
    program that never exits no longer wedges the shell.
  - KILLING. proc_kill only sets a flag; marking the task DEAD would leak the
    whole address space, because the loader would never return to free it. The
    flag is taken where no locks are held: syscall entry/exit, and an IRQ that
    interrupted ring-3 code ((cs & 3) == 3). An IRQ inside the kernel half of a
    syscall must not unwind - it may hold the FAT mutex, and abandoning that
    frame would lock the filesystem forever. The IRQ path is also the only way
    to kill a program that makes no syscalls, which user/hog.c is.
  - Ctrl-C is raised in the keyboard IRQ, the way a tty does it: while a
    foreground program runs the shell is blocked in proc_wait and reads no
    keys. 0x03 flags the foreground process and is swallowed; with no
    foreground process it still cancels the shell's input line.
  - stdin belongs to the foreground process; others read EOF instead of
    stealing keys. That wait is interruptible and sti's first: int 0x80 is an
    interrupt gate, so waiting with IF clear waited forever for the keyboard
    IRQ that fills the buffer. (Nothing had exercised ring-3 stdin before.)
  - mapped[1024]/is_mapped deleted: dedup asks paging_is_mapped, which is
    authoritative, costs no memory and is not O(n) per page. The counter left
    is just the per-process budget.
  - task_count_alive() ignores DEAD tasks, so `doom` no longer refuses to start
    merely because a program finished before the reaper swept it.
  - Shell: `cook prog &`, `orders`, `kill %` (newest process), `ps` marks which
    tasks are processes, finished background jobs announced before the prompt.
  - New user programs: marker.elf (tags every step with its argument, so two
    copies prove concurrency) and hog.elf (loops forever, no syscalls).
  - Gate grew from 14 checks to 22: two processes interleaving, the per-task
    esp0 invariant asserted as two different measured numbers, both kill paths,
    Ctrl-C on a foreground program, shell survival after a kill, and equal free
    page counts across two runs of one program (no address-space leak).
  Design notes: docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-06-concurrent-processes-design.md
  Still open: fork/exec and pipes, fg/bg and process groups, and the
  preemption-safety debt below (soupyc, vfs, users, shell still have unguarded
  file-scope state).

v0.7.6  (2026-08-26)  — DOOM ENEMY AI FIXED (line of sight)
  The long-standing "enemy AI completely broken" bug is resolved, and the
  recorded diagnosis was wrong: nothing was failing to allocate. The wake
  radius (512 units) was simply smaller than the distance to the nearest
  monster in E1M1 (about 816), so enemies never activated. Widening it
  revealed that the AI had no line-of-sight test at all, so every woken
  monster shot through walls from any range.
  - New los_clear()/seg_crosses() in doom.c gate both waking and the ranged
    attack on visibility. int64 cross products; one-sided linedefs block.
  - ENEMY_SEE_D2 -> 1024*1024, new ENEMY_RNG_D2 (768*768) caps hitscan range.
  - thing_think distance math moved to int64 (fire_weapon already did this).
  - fire_weapon checks the level is loaded before spending a round.
  See the RESOLVED section below for the measurements.

v0.7.5  (2026-08-26)  - DOOM IS BACK
  The port is un-parked. doom.c/doom.h/wad.c/wad.h moved from doom-disabled/
  into src/; shell.c regained the include, cmd_doom(), the dispatch branch,
  the tab-completion name and a menu line. No Makefile change (it globs
  src/*.c). Kernel grew 561 KB -> 712 KB.
  - It needed no porting. Checked before starting: doom.c and wad.c compile
    with zero errors and zero warnings under the current CFLAGS including
    -mgeneral-regs-only (the port is pure fixed-point, no float), resolve
    every symbol against the current kernel, and still work against the
    fat_f* streaming API. v0.6.0's absolute-path change does not break the
    bare "DOOM1.WAD" in wad.c because path_split walks from the root.
  - `doom` refuses to start when task_count() > 1. doom.c has no task_yield()
    in its gameplay path: it was written for the cooperative scheduler where
    monopolising the CPU was correct. Since v0.7.4 the timer preempts and
    only heap.c is preemption-safe, so a background task can be scheduled
    mid-frame and write to the VGA text buffer while Doom owns mode 13h and
    the DAC, or race fat.c's file-scope state while Doom streams lumps.
    Refusing was chosen over preempt_disable() around the session:
    sched_locked is a single global that unbalances across a context switch,
    and Doom's menus call keyboard_getchar(), which yields, so wrapping the
    session would leak the lock into an unrelated task.
  - Also guards on the WAD being present, pointing at `make wad`.
  - Verified end to end: WAD loads (1264 lumps), mode 13h entered, menu and
    episode/skill selection navigate, level loads and renders with textures,
    flats, sprites and the status bar, movement works, ESC leaves the level,
    and Quit Game returns to a clean text-mode shell.
  - Two previously documented Doom bugs turned out to be already fixed by the
    2026-08-25 vga13h work: the death screen's stuck PLAYPAL (restore_regs
    restores all 768 DAC bytes) and garbled text after exit (the 8 KB plane-2
    font is now saved and restored). Confirmed visually.
  Still open: the enemy AI regression (see below).

v0.7.4  (2026-08-25)  — PREEMPTIVE MULTITASKING + GCC 16 BUILD FIX
  The scheduler stops being cooperative-only: IRQ0 now switches tasks.
  (The kernel side of this had been sitting unreleased in the tree since
  2026-06-16; this entry documents it and makes it build again.)
  - Timer-driven preemption. task.c gains `preempt_armed` + `sched_locked`,
    `sched_preempt()`, and `preempt_disable/enable`. irq_handler calls
    sched_preempt() after the IRQ0 EOI with interrupts still off; it switches
    only when preemption is armed, the scheduler is not already locked, and
    more than one task exists. kernel_main arms it after the cooperative
    self-test. (src/task.c, src/task.h, src/isr.c, src/kernel.c)
  - sched_locked balances *across* a context switch: whoever is switched to
    runs the matching sched_unlock (after its own task_switch, or via the
    first-run trampoline task_start), so a running task always observes
    sched_locked == 0.
  - heap.c wraps kmalloc/kfree in irq_save/irq_restore — the free list is
    shared mutable state and a preemption mid-splice would corrupt it.
  - New shell `spinbg`: a CPU-bound task that never calls task_yield. Under
    the old scheduler it froze the box for 4 s; now the shell stays live and
    other tasks keep running. Run `yieldbg` then `spinbg` — the [bg] sleeper
    holds its exact 30-tick cadence straight through the [spin] window,
    which can only happen if IRQ0 is switching tasks.
  - BUILD FIX: added -mgeneral-regs-only to CFLAGS and UCFLAGS. GCC 12+
    auto-vectorises plain byte loops at -O2; on GCC 16 the TSS-zeroing loop
    in gdt_init compiled to `pxor xmm0,xmm0`. The kernel never enables SSE
    (no CR4.OSFXSR / FXSAVE area) and QEMU's default qemu32 CPU does not
    expose SSE2, so that is an invalid opcode — and with no IDT loaded yet
    it went #UD -> #GP -> #DF -> triple fault, before the GDT was even
    installed. The tree had 545 such instructions in the kernel and 83 in
    each user ELF; the flag takes both to zero. Without it soupOS does not
    boot on any modern toolchain.
  - New scripts/smoke-test.sh — headless boot + shell driver (via the QEMU
    monitor, scripts/qemu_keys.py) that greps the serial log for the marker
    each subsystem emits. Exits non-zero on the first miss, so it works as
    a gate. Covers boot, preemption arming, all four ring-3 syscall paths,
    scheduler interleaving, and "did not panic".
  Still open: the non-heap subsystems are not preemption-safe yet — see
  KNOWN BUGS / DEBT.

v0.7.3  (2026-06-16)  — SOUPYC LANGUAGE POLISH
  Three ergonomics/robustness fixes to the soupyc interpreter.
  - Triple-quoted multi-line strings: """...""" runs verbatim (newlines
    kept, no \-escapes) until the closing """; single-quoted "" keeps the
    one-line \n/\t form. Both share the 47-char value cap. (src/soupyc.c
    next_tok)
  - Line-numbered errors: every AST node carries the source line it began
    on (node_t.line, stamped from the current token; binop/index/var/call
    nodes inherit the correct start line rather than drifting past it).
    Both "parse error (line N)" and "runtime error (line N)" now point at
    the offending line. Verified: undefined var and a too-long concat each
    reported the exact line.
  - String overflow is now an error, not a silent truncation: a string
    literal or a "+" concatenation that would exceed 47 chars raises
    "string too long (max 47 chars)" instead of quietly dropping bytes.
  Note: soupyc strings are still a fixed char[48]; lifting the 47-char cap
  needs heap-backed strings (a bigger value-system rework — see below).
  Not done this round: spawn(fn) as a background task — that needs the
  tree-walker made reentrant (per-task interpreter state) first, since all
  its state is file-scope globals; deferred to its own focused change.

v0.7.2  (2026-06-16)  — COOPERATIVE SYS_YIELD + SYS_TICKS
  Ring-3 programs can now cooperate with the scheduler.
  - SYS_YIELD is wired to task_yield() (was a no-op). It re-enables
    interrupts first: the int 0x80 gate is a DPL-3 *interrupt* gate so IF
    is clear in the handler, and a peer that hlts waiting for an IRQ would
    deadlock with interrupts off. Safe because only one user program runs
    at a time, so the frame left on the TSS (esp0) kernel stack can't be
    clobbered by another ring3->ring0 trap while we're switched away; the
    user's IF is restored from its saved EFLAGS by iret. (src/usermode.c)
  - SYS_TICKS (call #8): returns timer_get_ticks() (100 Hz uptime), so a
    user program can pace itself in wall-clock time. ulib gains sys_ticks()
    and eprint_int() (decimal to serial). (syscall_nr.h, user/ulib.*)
  - New program user/spin.c: loops on ticks+yield for ~3 s, emitting [u]
    markers to serial. New shell `yieldbg`: spawns a background task that
    logs [bg] markers every 300 ms via task_sleep. Run `yieldbg` then
    `cook spin.elf` — the [bg]/[u] markers interleave in the serial log,
    which only happens if the ring-3 yield reaches the scheduler. Verified
    headless: bg kept ticking on schedule all through spin's 3 s busy loop.
  - QoL: `menu` is now paged. The 25-row text screen has no scrollback, so
    the ~60-line listing used to scroll its top off. cmd_menu drives the
    lines through a small pager (page_line/PAGE_ROWS) that pauses with a
    "-- more --" prompt each screenful; any key shows the next page, q/ESC
    stop. (src/shell.c)
  Still open: per-process page directories (one user program at a time).

v0.7.1  (2026-06-16)  — SYSCALL DEPTH + USER RUNTIME + PROGRAMS
  Fills out the ring-3 ABI that v0.7.0 stubbed. The syscall table grows
  from 4 to 8 calls, gains a real user-space runtime, and ships three
  new programs. Verified end-to-end headless (systest.elf emits TESTOUT
  markers to serial; all three pass).
  - Syscalls: SYS_OPEN/SYS_CLOSE (per-process fd table, 16 slots, fd>=3
    via the VFS), SYS_SBRK (heap break grows user pages on demand, won't
    collide with the stack), SYS_ARGS (the cook command line). All gate
    user pointers through user_ok() so a hostile program can't steer the
    kernel at kernel memory. (src/usermode.c, syscall_nr.h)
  - exec_elf now takes an args string; shell `cook <prog.elf> [args]`
    splits the program name from the rest of the line and passes it down.
    Per-run state (fd table, brk, args) resets on each exec. (src/shell.c)
  - User runtime: user/ulib.{c,h} — _start (calls main, exits with its
    return), int 0x80 wrappers for every syscall, and conveniences
    (print/eprint/print_int, strlen_, a bump malloc over sbrk). A program
    is now just `int main(void)`.
  - New user programs: user/echo.c (print args), user/cat.c (open+read+
    write a file), user/systest.c (exercises args/open/read/sbrk and
    reports to serial). `make user` builds all four ELFs; the disk recipe
    mcopies ECHO/CAT/SYSTEST.ELF alongside HELLO.ELF.
  Still open: per-process page directories (one user program at a time),
  sys_yield wired to the scheduler, write to a streaming fat backend.

v0.7.0  (2026-06-16)  — RING 3 + SYSCALLS + ELF LOADER
  The big OS-ification jump (path step 3). soupOS now runs real
  user-mode processes with hardware memory protection.
  - GDT: added ring-3 code/data segments (0x1B/0x23) and a TSS (0x28)
    holding ss0/esp0 — the kernel stack the CPU switches to on a
    ring3->ring0 trap. `ltr` loads it at boot. (src/gdt.c, gdt.h)
  - Paging: map_page now propagates the user bit to the PDE when a user
    PTE is added (privilege is AND-ed across levels, so kernel pages stay
    supervisor-only). Added paging_unmap. (src/paging.c)
  - Syscalls: `int 0x80` gate at DPL 3 (idt.c) + a syscall stub
    (isr_stubs.asm) -> syscall_dispatch. Calls: exit, write (fd 1=screen,
    2=serial), read (kbd line), yield (no-op for now). (src/usermode.c)
  - Ring transitions: user_mode_enter builds an iret frame into ring 3;
    the exit syscall calls user_mode_exit, which unwinds back to the
    launcher (one-shot coroutine swap). (src/usermode_asm.asm)
  - ELF loader: exec_elf reads an ELF32 ET_EXEC i386 binary, maps each
    PT_LOAD into fresh user pages (USER_BASE 0xC0000000), copies the
    image, zeroes .bss, sets up a 16 KB user stack, runs it, and tears
    the pages down on exit. (src/usermode.c)
  - Shell `cook <prog.elf>` runs a program in ring 3. First user program:
    user/hello.c (freestanding, syscalls only) -> HELLO.ELF on the disk;
    `make user` builds it. Verified end-to-end headless: ring-3 writes
    reach the kernel, exit returns to the shell, pages free, re-exec works.
  - Boot diagnostic: usermode_selftest fires int 0x80 from ring 0.
  Not yet: per-process address spaces (one user program at a time),
  brk/open/close syscalls, scheduler-integrated yield.

v0.6.4  (2026-06-16)
  - THE LLM (serial bridge, Option A): `ai <prompt>` asks a host-side
    LLM and streams the reply back into the OS. New COM2 (0x2F8) polled
    UART in serial.c (serial2_*), probed via the 16550 scratch register
    so it degrades cleanly when absent (no COM2 = "oracle asleep", no
    hang). New src/ai.{c,h}: ai_send writes "<prompt>\n", ai_getc reads
    reply bytes until 0x04 (EOT), yielding to the scheduler between polls
    so background tasks keep running. Shell `ai` streams to the screen
    and mirrors the assembled reply to klog/COM1 (visible in dmesg). Also
    a soupyc `ai(prompt)` builtin (reply capped at the 47-char string
    limit). Host side: tools/ai_bridge.py (Ollama by default, AI_FAKE=1
    for offline) + a `make run-ai` target wiring COM2 to a unix socket.
    Verified end-to-end headlessly: prompt reaches the daemon, reply
    reaches the kernel.

v0.6.3  (2026-06-16)
  - Text editor: `jot <file>` — a full-screen nano-like editor in new
    src/editor.{c,h}. Loads an existing file or starts empty; flat 8 KB
    buffer with in-place tail shifts for insert/delete. Keys: arrows /
    Home / End move, Backspace / Del erase, Enter splits a line, Ctrl+S
    writes back via fat_write, Esc / Ctrl+Q quit (with a discard prompt
    when modified). Renders straight to the 0xB8000 cell buffer (never
    scrolls), with a status bar showing the file, Ln/Col, and a * when
    dirty. Shell `jot` gates on read+write (existing) or parent write
    (new); clears the screen on exit so the prompt comes back clean.
  - soupyc: five new builtins — sum(arr), reverse(arr), sort(arr)
    (in-place, ascending by int value) and contains(s,sub)/find(s,sub)
    (substring test / first index, -1 if absent).

v0.6.2  (2026-06-16)
  - soupyc: `include "file.sc"` — parses another script inline via the
    VFS. Functions register into the global table; the included file's
    top-level statements run where the include sits. Nests up to 4 deep;
    the lexer state is saved/restored around the recursive parse and the
    kmalloc'd source buffer is freed once the AST has copied what it needs.
  - soupyc: nested indexed assignment `a[i][j] = x` (any depth). The
    parser folds every bracket but the last into N_INDEX over the base;
    because arrays are a reference type, indexing yields the inner array
    to mutate. (Read-side nested indexing already worked.)
  - soupyc: recursion depth cap raised 8 -> 16 (MAX_CALL_DEPTH). fib(9)+
    used to hit "call stack overflow"; fib(12) now evaluates fine.

v0.6.1  (2026-06-16)
  - File management: `decant <src> <dst>` (copy) and
    `relabel <src> <dst>` (move/rename). Both resolve relative paths
    against the cwd, honour copy-into-bowl semantics (a destination
    that names an existing bowl keeps the source's basename inside it),
    enforce the same advisory rwx checks as stir/strain, and refuse to
    clobber a bowl or copy onto themselves. relabel = copy + delete, so
    it works as both an in-place rename and a move between bowls. Copy
    is staged through a single 64 KB buffer (fat_write has no streaming
    form); larger files are refused. New: shell cmd_decant/cmd_relabel
    plus the prep_file_op/copy_file_core helpers in src/shell.c.

v0.6.0  (2026-05-21)
  - Bowls: FAT16 subdirectory support. fat.c walks '/'-separated
    paths over the fixed root + cluster-chain subdirs; subdirs grow
    a cluster at a time. New: fat_mkdir/fat_rmdir/fat_is_dir/
    fat_exists. Shell: cd, pwd, mkbowl, rmbowl, bowltest; the prompt
    shows the current bowl.
  - Users: the "kitchen staff". Accounts in /etc/kitchen
    (name:uid:hexhash); uid 0 = headchef (superuser), others are
    cooks. Boot login prompt; clockout/whoami/roster; hire/fire
    (headchef only). New module: src/users.{c,h}.
  - Access control: an owner uid + rwx mode are stamped into spare
    FAT dirent bytes (fat_stat/fat_chmod/fat_chown). The shell's
    may() enforces read/write/enter on pour/soup/stir/strain/cd/
    serve/mkbowl/rmbowl. `perms` shows/sets a file's mode.
    Advisory only — soupOS is ring 0 (see OS-IFICATION step 3).
  - Elevation: `chef <command>` runs one command as the headchef,
    prompting for the headchef secret. Bare `chef` is still CPU info.

v0.5.0  (2026-05-20)
  - Doom port parked in doom-disabled/ (excluded from the build) to
    focus on core OS work; re-enable per doom-disabled/README.txt.
  - Cooperative scheduler finished: task_sleep + wait-queue primitives
    in use; every in-tree busy-wait now yields (speaker, ATA PIO,
    shell wipe/bounce/clock/cat).
  - VFS shim (vfs.c) — uniform open/read/write/seek/close over FAT
    files plus /dev/{null,zero,serial,kbd}. Shell: vfstest.
  - serial.c extracted; klog.c kernel log ring buffer mirrored to
    COM1; shell `dmesg`. Panic now dumps registers + CR2 + an EBP
    stack walk (build uses -fno-omit-frame-pointer).
  - soupyc grew up: 13 stdlib builtins (len/abs/min/max/rand/time/
    int/str/chr/ord/substr/upper/lower), arrays (literals, indexing,
    push/pop, reference semantics), file I/O (open/read/write/close/
    eof via the VFS), `for v in a..b` loops, and string indexing s[i].
  - Demo scripts on the disk image: STDLIB.SC, ARRAY.SC, FILEIO.SC.

v0.4.0
  - Pre-changelog baseline (FAT16 r/w, soupyc fn/return, Doom port).

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
RESOLVED 2026-08-26 (v0.7.6) — DOOM ENEMY AI
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

The AI was never broken. Diagnosed by instrumenting level_load and
thing_think instead of following the hypotheses that were recorded here,
all of which turned out to be wrong:

  - lv_things and lv_thing_ai both allocate fine. Measured on E1M1:
    nthings=138, lv_things=0x18af98, lv_thing_ai=0x18b508, 552 bytes. No
    heap exhaustion. thing_ai_t is 4 bytes, so that allocation is about
    1 KB; the theory that an 8 MB heap failed it, and the suggested fix of
    shrinking MAX_SPRITES, were both aimed at an allocation orders of
    magnitude larger.
  - thing_think runs every frame and finds all 29 monsters.

The real cause was ENEMY_SEE_D2 = 512*512. In E1M1 the nearest monster
spawns about 816 units from the player start (measured min_d2 = 665920), so
nothing ever woke unless you walked into it. That alone produces every
symptom in the original report: enemies never move toward the player, never
deal damage, and the walk cycle never starts.

Widening the radius then exposed the bug the small radius had been hiding:
there was no line-of-sight test anywhere in the AI. Every woken monster
could shoot the player from any distance, through walls, so raising SEE_D2
made the whole map converge and kill the player in about a second (health
100 -> 10 in 120 ticks).

Fixed:
  [x] los_clear() + seg_crosses() in doom.c — segment intersection against
      one-sided linedefs, int64 throughout. Both waking and the ranged
      attack are gated on it, so monsters neither notice nor shoot through
      walls. Two-sided lines count as see-through, so a monster can see
      through a closed door; a known simplification.
  [x] ENEMY_SEE_D2 raised to 1024*1024 so enemies engage at all.
  [x] ENEMY_RNG_D2 (768*768) bounds the ranged hitscan. Chasing stays
      unbounded; only shooting is limited.
  [x] thing_think's distance math moved to int64, matching fire_weapon.
      dx*dx + dy*dy overflows int32 across a large map and can go negative,
      which would make the proximity test pass for distant things.
  [x] fire_weapon now checks the level is loaded BEFORE spending a round.
      The old order decremented ammo and then bailed, which is exactly the
      reported "ammo decrements but nothing happens".

Verified in QEMU from status bar readings across one run: 050 ammo / 100%
health at spawn, 050 / 085% after approaching, 041 / 050% after firing nine
rounds. Enemies wake on sight, close distance, and deal damage at a
survivable rate. Death now requires pushing into a room and fighting rather
than happening within a second of spawn.

Not directly confirmed: a monster dying from player fire. Shots are fired
and ammo decrements, but no test drove a single enemy to zero HP.

Also fixed earlier, by the 2026-08-25 vga13h work rather than here: the
death/level-complete screen no longer shows garbled rainbow pixels, because
restore_regs restores all 768 DAC bytes on exit from mode 13h.

────────────────────
OS-IFICATION PATH  (make it feel like a real OS, in order)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Current gap: soupOS boots, pages, heaps, runs a FS + shell + Doom
— but it's still mostly single-tasking, ring 0 only, with apps
baked into the kernel binary. These four steps close that gap:

  1. Cooperative scheduler  (Tier 2)   — DONE
     [x] task_t, task_init, task_spawn, task_yield, task_exit
     [x] task_switch.asm (callee-saved reg swap + esp swap)
     [x] Reaper: dead tasks are unlinked + stacks freed on next yield
     [x] keyboard_getchar yields instead of busy-waiting
     [x] Shell commands: ps, kill <id>, bgclock
     [x] task_sleep(ms) + wait-queue primitives (task_block_on/task_wake)
     [x] speaker_beep: pause loop replaced with task_sleep(ms)
     [x] ATA PIO wait loops yield periodically (task_yield every ~16k
         polls) so a slow drive can't starve the scheduler. Full
         IRQ14-driven blocking is still optional future work — see
         Tier 3 "ATA DMA".
     [x] shell live loops yield: tick_delay (wipe/bounce) now uses
         task_sleep; clock and cat yield before hlt. (doom is parked.)

  2. VFS shim over fat_*  (Tier 2)   — DONE
     [x] vfs_open/read/write/seek/close + vfs_node_t (src/vfs.{c,h}).
     [x] FAT backend: read-only files stream via fat_f*; write-mode
         files buffer in heap and flush via fat_write on close.
     [x] Char devices: /dev/null, /dev/zero, /dev/serial, /dev/kbd.
     [x] vfs_ls merges the FAT root with the /dev nodes.
     [x] `vfstest` shell command exercises the whole API.
     fat_* still works untouched — the VFS is an additive layer, so
     existing shell commands did not need rewriting.

  3. ELF loader + ring 3 + syscalls  (Tier 3)   — DONE (v0.7.0)
     [x] User-mode GDT segments (0x1B/0x23) + TSS (esp0/ss0), ltr at boot
     [x] int 0x80 dispatch (DPL-3 gate -> syscall_dispatch)
     [x] ELF32 PT_LOAD mapping into user pages at USER_BASE 0xC0000000
     [x] jump to entry in ring 3 (user_mode_enter) + exit back (user_mode_exit)
     [x] syscalls: write, read, exit, yield, open, close, sbrk, args
         (v0.7.1) + ticks (v0.7.2). yield is now wired to task_yield (v0.7.2).
     [x] shell `cook <prog.elf> [args]`; programs hello/echo/cat/systest,
         a user runtime (user/ulib.{c,h}) provides _start + syscall wrappers
     Still single-address-space: one user program at a time, added to the
     kernel page directory and torn down on exit. Per-task page
     directories + concurrent processes are the next layer.

  4. Better panic + kernel log ring  (quality of life)   — DONE
     [x] serial.c — COM1 driver extracted from kernel.c.
     [x] klog.c — 8 KB ring buffer, klog()/klog_puts(), mirrors every
         byte to COM1. Boot messages route through it.
     [x] `dmesg` shell command dumps the ring.
     [x] Panic (isr.c) dumps all GP registers + eip/cs/eflags, the
         page-fault CR2 + decoded cause, and an EBP stack walk; the
         whole report is mirrored to klog/serial. Build now uses
         -fno-omit-frame-pointer so the stack walk has frames.

All four steps are done. Step 3 landed in v0.7.0: soupOS runs ring-3
user-mode ELF programs over an int 0x80 syscall interface with real
(U/S-bit) memory protection. v0.7.1 filled out the syscall table
(open/close/sbrk/args + a user runtime) and v0.7.2 added ticks and wired
sys_yield to the scheduler. What remains is depth, not breadth — per-process
page directories so multiple user programs can coexist (and run as real
scheduler tasks rather than the current one-shot coroutine swap).

TIER 2 — Medium Effort (a weekend each)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

Filesystem
  [x] FAT subdirectory support — directories are "bowls".
      fat.c walks '/'-separated paths over the FAT16 root + cluster-chain
      subdirs; fat_mkdir/fat_rmdir/fat_is_dir/fat_exists added; fat_ls and
      all path calls take an absolute path. Shell: cd, pwd, mkbowl, rmbowl,
      bowltest; the prompt shows the current bowl. Verified via bowltest.
  [ ] FAT32 write support (currently read-only)
  [x] cp / mv / rename commands — `decant` (copy) and `relabel`
      (move/rename). Copy-into-bowl semantics; advisory rwx checks;
      staged through a 64 KB buffer (single fat_write, no streaming).
  [ ] File size limit lifted beyond single cluster chain (already
      works in theory; needs end-to-end testing with large files)
  [x] Simple text editor — `jot <file>`, a full-screen nano-like editor
      (src/editor.c). Arrows/Home/End, insert/delete, Ctrl+S save, Esc
      quit; 8 KB buffer. Verified: create/edit/save round-trips to disk.

soupyc arrays   — DONE (see soupyc.c N_ARRAY/N_INDEX; demo: ARRAY.SC)
  [x] let arr = [] / [e1, e2]  — array literal
  [x] arr[i]                   — indexed read (also nested: arr[i][j])
  [x] arr[i] = x               — indexed write
  [x] push(arr, x)             — append
  [x] pop(arr)                 — remove + return last
  [x] len(arr)                 — element count
  [x] arr[i][j] = x            — nested indexed write (any depth)
  Arrays are a reference type (fixed pool of 24, 48 elems each). Sharing
  a value shares storage, so functions can mutate a passed-in array.

soupyc file I/O   — DONE (backed by the VFS; demo: FILEIO.SC)
  [x] open(name [,"w"])      — handle int, or -1 if missing; "w" = write
  [x] read(handle, n)        — read up to n bytes as a string
  [x] write(handle, str)     — write string, returns bytes written
  [x] close(handle)          — flush (write mode) + release
  [x] eof(handle)            — 1 once all bytes are read
  Works on disk files and /dev nodes alike (open("/dev/serial","w")).
  Handles map to VFS nodes; the table is closed between script runs.

Graphics (VGA mode 13h — 320×200, 256 colors)
  [ ] vga_set_mode13() / vga_set_text_mode()
  [ ] plot(x, y, color)
  [ ] draw_line, draw_rect, fill_rect
  [ ] Colored Mandelbrot renderer (much prettier than ASCII)
  [ ] Julia set explorer with parameter tuning
  [ ] Plasma / fire effect demo

PS/2 Mouse
  [ ] Mouse driver (IRQ 12, port 0x60/0x64)
  [ ] Cursor rendering in mode 13h
  [ ] Click events exposed to soupyc via mouse_x() / mouse_y() / mouse_btn()

Multitasking (cooperative, not preemptive)
  [x] Simple task struct: stack, instruction pointer, state
  [x] task_yield() — hand off to next task
  [x] Round-robin scheduler + dead-task reaper
  [x] keyboard_getchar yields → background tasks keep running
      while the shell waits for input (try `bgclock` then typing)
  [x] Shell: ps, kill <id>, bgclock
  [x] task_sleep(ms), wait-queues (task_block_on / task_wake)
  [x] All remaining in-tree blocking loops yield (beep, ATA, clock,
      bounce, cat)
  [ ] spawn(fn_name) in soupyc — blocked on making the tree-walker
      reentrant (interpreter state is all file-scope globals; two evals
      interleaving via task_yield would corrupt each other). Needs a
      per-invocation context struct first.

TIER 3 — Big Projects (week+ each)

Preemptive multitasking   — DONE (v0.7.4)

      kernel_main after the cooperative self-test)

  [x] kill <id> — terminate a task
  [x] spinbg — CPU-bound task that never yields, as the proof
  [ ] Per-task sched_locked. It is currently one global counter shared with
      preempt_disable(), so a preempt_disable region that yields has its
      decrement run by whichever task is switched to — the critical section
      leaks into an unrelated task. Latent (nothing calls preempt_disable
      today) but it must be fixed before anything does.
